    - Benefits of World Heritage Site Status
        - International recognition for preservation and conservation needs.
        - Boosts tourism, ensuring economic benefits.
        - UNESCO funding for restoration, preservation, and training.
        - Strengthens ties with the United Nations, offering prestige and support.
        - Provides access to global project management resources.
        - Facilitates partnerships between government, private sector, and NGOs.
        - Legal protection under the Geneva Convention against destruction or misuse during wartime.


- SECTION B - PERFORMING ARTS

- INDIAN MUSIC
    - Introduction
        - Music is the soul of any culture, and India has a long tradition of musical ingenuity.
        - Narada Muni is said to have introduced music to Earth, teaching about Nada Brahma, the sound that pervades the universe.
        - Musicologists suggest that “Om” is the source of all ragas and notes.
        - Panini (4th century BC) made the first proper reference to music-making.
        - Bharata’s Natyashastra (200 BC–200 AD) was the first text discussing musical theory.

	- History and Evolution of Indian Music
        - Indian music dates back to the Indus Valley Civilization, where a seven-holed flute was found at excavation sites.
        - The Ravanahatha, originating from Sri Lanka’s Hela Civilization, is one of the world’s oldest instruments.
        - The first literary references appear 2,000 years ago in the Vedic period.
            - The seven notes of raga Kharaharapriya appear in descending order in the Sama Veda.
            - The science of music, Gandharva Veda, is an Upaveda of the Sama Veda.
            - The Veena is mentioned in Aitareya Aranyaka, and Jaiminiya Brahmana speaks of dance and music together.
        - Music evolved through devotional performances:
            - In the later Vedic period, Sangama involved chanting verses to musical patterns.
            - Epics were narrated with Jatigan music.
        - Bharata’s Natyashastra identified the octave and its 22 shrutis (keys).
        - Dattilam confirmed 22 shrutis per octave, suggesting they were the only ones a human could produce.
        - Sarangadeva (13th century) supported this view in his Sangeet Ratnakara:
            - Defined 264 ragas from both North Indian and Dravidian traditions.
            - Classified microtones into different categories.
        - Other medieval texts focused on specific aspects:
            - Brihaddeshi (6th–8th century, Matanga) defined raga.
            - Swaramela-Kalanidhi (16th century, Ramamatya) focused on ragas.
            - Chaturdandi-Prakashika (17th century, Venkatamakhin) detailed Carnatic musicology.
        - Persian influence reshaped North Indian music:
            - Dhruvapad (devotional) transformed into Dhrupad by the 15th century.
            - Khayal emerged by the 17th century.
            - Many folk singing styles developed.

	- Gurukul System in Music Education
        - Also known as the Ashram or hermitage system.
        - Followed the Guru-Shishya tradition, emphasizing close teacher-student relationships.
        - Music education in ancient times:
            - Teachers were sages.
            - Students lived in the hermitage for 12 years, gaining knowledge by serving the master.
        - Patronized by kings and wealthy individuals.
        - Life in the hermitage was rigorous, reflective, and based on direct experience.
        - All students, regardless of status, were treated equally, with no discrimination.

    - Anatomy of Indian Music
        - Before one delves into the different types and kinds of Indian music it is necessary to understand the anatomy of Indian classical music. 
        - There are three main pillars of Indian classical music:
            - Swara
                - In ancient times, “Swara” was associated with Vedic recitation. Over time, it came to define the note or scale degree in a composition.
                - Bharata’s Natyashastra divided Swaras into a 22-note scale.
                - The modern Hindustani notational system consists of: Sa, Re, Ga, Ma, Pa, Dha, Ni, collectively called Saptak or Sargam.
                - Difference between Swara and Shruti:
                    - A Shruti is the smallest pitch gradation, representing frequency quality.
                    - There are 22 Shrutis, of which only 12 are audible (7 Suddha Swaras and 5 Vikrit Swaras).
                - He listed each pitch using the following names:
                    

                        

                            Name of the Pitch
                            Function
                            Abbreviation
                        

                        

                            Shadja
                            Tonic
                            Sa
                        

                        

                            Rishabha
                            Supertonic
                            Re
                        

                        

                            Gandhara
                            Mediant
                            Ga
                        

                        

                            Madhyama
                            Sub-dominant
                            Ma
                        

                        

                            Panchama
                            Dominant
                            Pa
                        

                        

                            Dhaivata
                            Sub-mediant
                            Dha
                        

                        

                            Nishada
                            Subtonic
                            Ni
                        

                    

            - Raga
                - Basic info
                    - The term Raga comes from Sanskrit Ranj, meaning “to delight or satisfy.”
                    - Raga forms the basis of melody, while Tala governs rhythm.
                    - Each raga evokes a distinct personality, mood, and emotion.
                - Types of Ragas (Jaatis) Based on Notes:
                    - Audav/Odava Raga 
                        - Five-note (pentatonic) raga.
                    - Shadava Raga 
                        - Six-note (hexatonic) raga.
                    - Sampurna Raga 
                        – Seven-note (heptatonic) raga.
                - Classification of Ragas (Raga Bhed):
                    - Shuddha Raag 
                        – Even if extra notes are played, the nature of the raga remains unchanged.
                    - Chhayalag Raag 
                        – If extra notes are played, the raga’s character changes.
                    - Sankeerna Raag 
                        – A combination of two or more ragas.
                    - 
                - Key Components of Raga:
                    - Vaadi (King) 
                        – The principal note.
                    - Samvaadi (Queen) 
                        – The second-most important note.
                    - Anuvaadi 
                        – Supporting notes.
                    - Vivadi 
                        – Notes absent in the composition.
                - Movement of Notes:
                    - Aroha (Ascent): 
                        - Notes arranged in increasing pitch (e.g., Sa Re Ga Ma Pa Dha Ni).
                    - Avaroha (Descent): 
                        - Notes arranged in decreasing pitch (e.g., Ni Dha Pa Ma Ga Re Sa).
                - Laya (Speed of Raga Performance):
                    - Vilambit 
                        – Slow tempo.
                    - Madhya 
                        – Medium tempo.
                    - Drut 
                        – Fast tempo.
                - Although there are 72 melas or parent scales on which ragas are based under carnatic music, there are six main ragas under Hindustani music repertoire and all of these are time and season specific and evoke a particular type of emotion (Rasa):
                    

                        

                            Raga
                            Time
                            Season
                            Mood (Rasa)
                        

                        

                            Bhairav
                            Dawn
                            Any season
                            Peace
                        

                        

                            Hindol
                            Morning
                            Spring
                            Evokes sweetness of a young couple
                        

                        

                            Deepak
                            Night
                            Summer
                            Compassion
                        

                        

                            Megh
                            Late Night
                            Rainy
                            Courage
                        

                        

                            Shree
                            Evening
                            Winter
                            Gladness
                        

                        

                            Malkaush
                            Midnight
                            Winter
                            Vir or Brave
                        

                    

                - RASA
                    - Ragas evoke emotional responses in performers and audiences. 
                    - These emotions are called Rasas or “Aesthetic Delights.” Originally, eight rasas were identified, but later, Shanta Rasa (peace) was added, forming the Navarasas (Nine Rasas).
                    - These are:
                        

                            

                                Type of Rasa
                                Mood it Evokes
                            

                            

                                Shringara
                                Love
                            

                            

                                Hasya
                                Humour/Laughter
                            

                            

                                Karuna
                                Pathos
                            

                            

                                Raudra
                                Anger
                            

                            

                                Bhayanak
                                Horror
                            

                            

                                Veer
                                Bravery
                            

                            

                                Adbhuta
                                Wonder
                            

                            

                                Bibhatsya
                                Disgust
                            

                            

                                Shant
                                Peace or calm
                            

                        

                    - After the 15th century, Bhakti (devotion to God) was widely accepted as an additional rasa. Some argue that Bhakti and Shant Rasa are the same.
                - Samay (Time Theory of Ragas)
                    - Each raga is performed at a specific time to enhance its effect.
                    - Time classification:
                        - 12 AM – 12 PM: Poorva Bhaag (Morning to Noon) → Poorva Raga
                        - 12 PM – 12 AM: Uttar Bhaag (Afternoon to Night) → Uttar Raga
                    - Saptak Shift by Time Period:
                        - Poorvang Period (Morning–Noon): Sa to Ma (Sa, Re, Ga, Ma).
                        - Uttarang Period (Afternoon–Night): Pa to Sa (Pa, Dha, Ni, Sa).
                - Other Key Components of Raga Performance
                    - Alap 
                        – A slow exposition of the raga, emphasizing Vaadi and Samvaadi.
                    - Sthayee/Mukhda 
                        – The primary melody section, repeated frequently.
                    - Antara 
                        – The secondary melody section.
                    - Taan 
                        – Fast, intricate patterns of notes.
                        - Short, fast taans with 3-4 notes are called Murki.
                    - Alankar (Ornamentation) 
                        – Pattern-based embellishment (e.g., Sa Re Ga, Ga Ma Pa).
            - Tala (Rhythm in Indian Music)
                - 
                - Basic info
                    - Tala (or Taal) is the rhythmic structure that organizes beats into cycles.
                    - Talas range from 3 to 108 beats.
                    - Hindustani and Carnatic music follow different time measurement systems.
                    - Tempo (Laya) of tala determines uniformity:
                        - Slow (Vilambit)
                        - Medium (Madhya)
                        - Fast (Drut)
                - Common Talas in Hindustani Music:
                    - Dadra
                    - Kaharba
                    - Rupak
                    - Ektal
                    - Jhaptal
                    - Teen-Taal (most used, 16 beats)
                    - Chautal
                - Carnatic Tala System:
                    - Three components: Laghu, Dhrutam, and Anu Dhrutam.
                    - Originally 35 talas, each having 5 subtypes, leading to 175 talas.
                    - Aditala is the most prominent tala in Carnatic music.
            - Thaat (Classification of Ragas)
                - Thaat is a system of organizing ragas into 10 categories, developed by V.N. Bhatkhande.
                - Unlike Raga, Thaat is not sung—it serves as a structural framework.
                - Rules for Thaat:
                    - Must have 7 notes in ascending order (Aarohi).
                    - Based on the 12 Swaras (7 Suddha + 5 Vikrit Swaras).
                10 Thaats in Hindustani Music:
                    - Bilawal
                    - Khamaj
                    - Kafi
                    - Asavari
                    - Bhairavi
                    - Bhairav
                    - Kalyan
                    - Marwa
                    - Poorvi
                    - Todi
                    

                        

                            Basis of Difference
                            Thaat
                            Raga
                        

                        

                            Origin
                            These are scales which are produced from 12 swara (notes).
                            Ragas belong to the genre of thaats.
                        

                        

                            Number of Notes
                            Thaat must have seven notes in it.
                            Raga must have at least five notes in it.
                        

                        

                            Types of Notes
                            It only has Aaroha or ascending notes.
                            It has Aaroha and Avaroha notes.
                        

                        

                            Melody
                            It is not necessary for thaat to be melodious as they are not sung.
                            Ragas are sung and hence they are melodious.
                        

                        

                            Important Notes
                            Thaats don’t have vaadi and samvaadi.
                            Ragas have vaadi and samvaadi.
                        

                        

                            Naming
                            Thaats are named after the popular ragas.
                            Ragas are named after the emotion they evoke.
                        

                    

    - CLASSIFICATION OF INDIAN MUSIC
        - 
        - There are many types of music prevalent in the Indian sub-continent that belongs to different categories. Some are closer to the classical bent and some are experimenting with the global music. Recently, there has been a trend to create a fusion of the classical heritage with newer musical strands like pop, jazz, etc. and this is garnering attention of the masses. 
        - The classification of Indian music is as follows:
            - Classical Music
                - Over time, two distinct schools of Indian classical music evolved:
                    - Hindustani Music
                        - Basic info
                            - Originates from Natyashastra.
                            - Separated from Carnatic music in the 14th century.
                            - Focuses on structure and improvisation.
                            - Uses Shudha Swara Saptaka (Octave of Natural Notes).
                        - Main styles:
                            - Dhrupad
                                - Basic infor
                                    - One of the oldest & grandest forms of Hindustani music.
                                    - Mentioned in Natyashastra (200 BC–200 AD).
                                    - Derived from Prabhanda and Dhruvapada.
                                    - Name comes from dhruva (fixed) + pada (verse).
                                    - Became classical in the 13th century.
                                    - Reached its peak in Akbar’s court.
                                - Patronage
                                    - Akbar’s court musicians:
                                        - Tansen (one of Navratnas)
                                            - 16th-century musician in Akbar’s court.
                                            - Title: Mian (learned man).
                                            - Master of Dhrupad.
                                            - Created several new ragas.
                                            - Composed music on Hindu Gods, Goddesses, and Emperor Akbar.
                                            - Tomb located in Gwalior.
                                            - 
                                        - Baba Gopal Das
                                        - Swami Haridas
                                    - Baiju Bawra possibly sang at Akbar’s court.
                                    - Practiced in Gwalior under Raja Man Singh Tomar.
                                    - Major form of singing in the medieval period.
                                    - Declined in the 18th century.
                                - Performance Style
                                    - Begins with Alap (slow, wordless melody).
                                    - Alap sets the mood of the raga.
                                    - Tempo gradually increases.
                                    - Dhrupad starts after Alap, with Pakhawaj accompaniment.
                                - Dhrupad Characteristics
                                    - Temple origin.
                                    - Uses Sanskrit syllables.
                                    - Compositions have 4-5 stanzas.
                                    - Performed by two male vocalists.
                                    - Tanpura and Pakhawaj accompany.
                                - Dhrupad Gharanas
                                    - Dagari Gharana
                                        - Dagar family sings Dagar Vani.
                                        - Emphasizes Alap.
                                        - Traditionally performed in pairs.
                                        - Mostly Muslim singers but use Hindu texts.
                                        - Famous duo: Gundecha Brothers.
                                    - Darbhanga Gharana
                                        - Sings Khandar Vani & Gauhar Vani.
                                        - Strong raga alap with layakari improvisation.
                                        - Leading family: Mallik family (Ram Chatur Mallik, Prem Kumar Mallik, Siyaram Tewari).
                                    - Bettiah Gharana
                                        - Performs Nauhar & Khandar Vani.
                                        - Unique techniques only taught within families.
                                        - Leading family: Mishra family.
                                        - Connected with the Haveli style of Dhrupad.
                                    - Talwandi Gharana
                                        - Sings Khandar Vani.
                                        - Based in Pakistan, making it difficult to sustain in Indian music.
                                - Gharana System
                                    - Gharana = musical lineage & style.
                                    - From ghar (house/family).
                                    - Influences performance, teaching, and appreciation.
                                    - Major gharanas:
                                        - Agra
                                        - Gwalior
                                        - Indore
                                        - Jaipur
                                        - Kirana
                                        - Patiala
                            - Khayal
                                - Basic info
                                    - Derived from Persian Khyal = “idea” or “imagination”.
                                    - Attributed to Amir Khusrau.
                                    - More popular than Dhrupad due to improvisation flexibility.
                                    - Short compositions (Bandish) of 2-8 lines.
                                    - Patronized by Hussain Shah (Jaunpur Sultanate, 15th century).
                                    - Frequent use of taans, less Alap than Dhrupad.
                                - Types of Khayal
                                    - Bada Khayal – slow tempo.
                                        - Bada Khayal is the main part of a performance.
                                    - Chhota Khayal – fast tempo.
                                - Themes: 
                                    - Love, devotion, praise of kings/Gods.
                                - Famous compositions dedicated to Lord Krishna.
                                - Khayal Gharanas
                                    - Gwalior Gharana
                                        - Oldest & most elaborate Khayal Gharana.
                                        - Balanced approach to melody & rhythm.
                                        - Prefers simple ragas.
                                        - Exponents: Nathu Khan, Vishnu Paluskar.
                                    - Kirana Gharana
                                        - Founded by Nayak Gopal, made popular by Abdul Karim Khan & Abdul Wahid Khan.
                                        - Famous for slow tempo ragas.
                                        - Focus on melody, pronunciation, & traditional ragas.
                                        - Exponents: Bhimsen Joshi, Gangubai Hangal.
                                        - Pandit Bhimsen Joshi
                                            - Major Khayal exponent of Kirana Gharana.
                                            - Received Bharat Ratna (2008).
                                            - Famous ragas: Suddha Kalyan, Miyan ki Todi, Multani, Bhimpalasi.
                                            - Recognized for Mile Sur Mera Tumhara (1988).
                                    - Agra Gharana
                                        - Established by Khudabuksh (19th century) or Haji Sujan Khan.
                                        - Revived by Faiyyaz Khan, renamed Rangila Gharana.
                                        - Blend of Khayal & Dhrupad-dhamar styles.
                                        - Exponents: Mohsin Khan Niazi, Vijay Kichlu.
                                    - Patiala Gharana
                                        - Founded by Bade Fateh Ali Khan & Ali Baksh Khan (19th century).
                                        - Sponsored by Maharaja of Patiala.
                                        - Famous for ghazal, thumri, Khayal.
                                        - Focus on emotion & intricate taans.
                                        - Exponent: Bade Ghulam Ali Khan (Raga Darbari expert).
                                    - Bhendibazaar Gharana
                                        - Founded by Chhajju Khan, Nazir Khan, Khadim Hussain Khan (19th century).
                                        - Unique long-breath singing technique.
                                        - Incorporates Carnatic ragas.
                            - Tarana
                                - Rhythm-centric style.
                                - Short melody repeated with variations.
                                - Uses fast-paced syllables.
                                - Requires advanced rhythmic skills.
                                - Invented by Amir Khusrau (13th-14th century).
                                - Used by Guru Gobind Singh.
                                - Fastest Tarana Singer: Pandit Rattan Mohan Sharma (Mewati Gharana).
                                - Given the title “Tarana ke Baadshah” at Pandit Motiram Sangeet Samaroh (Hyderabad).
                        - Semi-Classical Styles of Hindustani Music
                            - Basic info
                                - Semi-classical music is based on swara (notes) but deviates slightly from the strict structure of a raga. 
                                - It uses lighter versions of ragas like Bhoopali or Malkaush and employs madhyam or dhrut laya (faster tempo). 
                                - Unlike classical music, it focuses more on bhava (expression) and lyrics rather than alap-jor-jhala.
                            - Prominent Semi-Classical Styles                                
                                - Thumri
                                    - Based on mixed ragas and considered semi-classical.
                                    - Compositions are romantic or devotional, often inspired by the Bhakti movement.
                                    - Lyrics often depict a girl’s love for Krishna.
                                    - Language: Braj Bhasha dialect of Hindi.
                                    - Usually sung in a female voice and characterized by sensuality.
                                    - Allows for improvisation with flexible raga usage.
                                    - Linked to the classical dance Kathak.
                                    - Other lighter forms: Dadra, Hori, Kajari, Saavan, Jhoola, Chaiti.
                                    - Major gharanas: Varanasi & Lucknow.
                                    - Famous exponents: Begum Akhtar, Girija Devi (Purab Ang of Benaras Gharana).
                                - Tappa
                                    - Rhythm plays a crucial role.
                                    - Characterized by fast, subtle, and knotty phrases.
                                    - Originated from folk songs of camel riders in North-West India.
                                    - Gained recognition in the Mughal court of Muhammad Shah.
                                    - Popular among both elites and common classes.
                                    - Baithaki style evolved in elite assembly halls (baithak khanas, jalsaghar).
                                    - Now rare, few musicians practice it today.
                                    - Famous exponents: Laxman Rao Pandit (Gwalior Gharana), Shanno Khurana.
                                - Ghazal
                                    - A poetic form with rhyming couplets and a refrain.
                                    - Expresses love, pain, and separation.
                                    - Origin: Iran (10th century AD).
                                    - Spread to South Asia in the 12th century, flourished in the Mughal period.
                                    - Amir Khusrau (1253–1325) was one of the earliest exponents.
                                    - Many ghazal poets were Sufis or influenced by Sufi philosophy.
                                    - Themes: Love (divine or human), often open to interpretation.
                                    - Modern ghazals use simplified language for a wider audience.
                                    - Famous poets: Muhammad Iqbal, Mirza Ghalib, Rumi, Hafez, Kazi Nazrul Islam.
                    - Carnatic Music
                        - Basic info
                            - Carnatic music is played in the traditional octave and is kriti-based, focusing more on saahitya (lyrics). 
                            - The kriti is a highly evolved musical song set to a specific raga and a fixed tala (rhythmic cycle).        
                            - Purandara Dasa (1484-1564) is regarded as the Pitamaha (father/grandfather) of Carnatic music.
                        - Structure of a Carnatic Composition
                            - Pallavi:
                                - The first or second thematic line of the composition.
                                - Repeated in each stanza.
                                - Considered the highlight of Carnatic music, especially in Ragam Thanam Pallavi, allowing great improvisation.
                            - Anu Pallavi:
                                - Two lines that follow the Pallavi.
                                - Sung at the beginning and sometimes at the end.
                                - Not necessarily repeated after every stanza (Charanam).
                            - Charanam:
                                - The final and longest verse that concludes the song.
                        - Other Components
                            - Swara-Kalpana: Improvised section performed with the drummer in medium and fast paces.
                            - Tanam: A melodic improvisation in free rhythm with mridangam.
                            - Ragam: Similar to Tanam but without mridangam.
                        - Early Proponents of Carnatic Music
                            - Annamacharya (1408-1503)
                                - First known composer of Carnatic music.
                                - Composed sankirtanas in praise of Lord Venkateswara (a form of Lord Vishnu).
                                - Compositions were mainly in Telugu.
                                - Recognized as the Grandfather of Telugu Songwriting.
                            - Purandara Dasa (1484-1564)
                                - One of the founding proponents of Carnatic music.
                                - A devotee of Lord Krishna.
                                - Known as Pitamaha (father/grandfather) of Carnatic Music.
                                - Believed to be an avatar of Sage Narada.
                                - Famous compositions include Dasa Sahithya.
                            - Kshetrayya (1600-1680)
                                - Telugu poet and prominent composer.
                                - Composed Padams and Keertanas, mainly based on Lord Krishna.
                                - Traveled extensively.
                                - His Padams are still performed in Bharatanatyam and Kuchipudi.
                            - Bhadrachala Ramadasu (1620-1680)
                                - His compositions were mainly in praise of Lord Rama.
                                - Wrote primarily in Telugu.
                                - One of the famous Vaggeyakaras (composers who write lyrics and set them to music).
                                - Other famous Telugu Vaggeyakaras: Annamacharya, Tyagaraja, Syama Sastri.
                        - Trinity of Carnatic Music (All Born in Tiruvarur)
                            - Tyagaraja
                                - Composed mostly in Telugu, a few in Sanskrit, praising Lord Rama.
                                - Famous for Pancharatna Kritis.
                                - A crater on Mercury is named after him.
                                - Created several new ragas.
                            - Muthuswami Dikshitar
                                - Oldest among the Trinity.
                                - Compositions were mostly in Sanskrit, centered on Hindu gods and temples.
                                - Emphasized gamakas (ornamentations).
                                - Signature mudra (name): Guru-Guha.
                                - Skilled Veena player.
                            - Syama Sastri
                                - Composed mainly in Telugu, praising Goddess Kamakshi.
                                - Wrote fewer kritis compared to the other two.
                                - Signature mudra: Syama Krishna.
                                - Associated with the Svarajati musical genre.

                                    There are several sub-styles which lead to emergence of ‘Gharanas’.
                                    Only one particular prescribed style of singing.

                                    Instruments equally important as vocals.
                                    More emphasis on vocal music.

                                    Flute and Violin
                                

                            

            - Folk Music in India
                - India’s geographical diversity is reflected in its rich folk music traditions, with each state having its own distinct style.
                - Characteristics of Indian Folk Music
                    - Unlike classical music, which follows the Natyashastra and guru-shishya (student-mentor) tradition, folk music is the music of the people with no rigid rules.
                    - Based on diverse themes, often rhythmic and dance-oriented.
                    - Each state has its own folk music traditions, reflecting its culture and history.
                - Folk Music by Region
                    - North India
                        - Wanawan (Jammu and Kashmir)
                            - Sung during wedding ceremonies.
                            - Considered auspicious.
                        - Alha (Madhya Pradesh)
                            - A heroic ballad with intricate lyrics.
                            - Sung in Braj, Awadhi, and Bhojpuri.
                            - Related to Mahabharata, glorifying heroes as reincarnations of the Pandavas.
                            - The five Pandavas are represented as Alha, Udal, Malkhan, Lakhan, and Deva.
                        - Pai Song (Madhya Pradesh)
                            - Sung during festivals, especially monsoon-related festivals.
                            - A prayer for good rainfall and harvest, sung by farmer communities.
                            - Often accompanied by the Saira dance.
                    - West India
                        - Lavani (Maharashtra)
                            - A famous folk music and dance form.
                            - Performed to the beats of Dholki (percussion instrument).
                            - Known for strong rhythm and energetic performances.
                            - 
                        - Ovi (Maharashtra, Goa)
                            - Sung by women during household chores and leisure time.
                            - Short, four-line poetic songs.
                            - Themes include marriages, pregnancy, and lullabies.
                        - Powada (Maharashtra)
                            - Ballads narrating the heroic deeds of past warriors.
                            - Often sung in praise of Shivaji.
                        - Mando (Goa)
                            - A blend of Indian and Western musical traditions.
                            - Uses guitar, violin, and ghumot drum.
                    - East India
                        - Baul (West Bengal, Assam, Tripura)
                            - A Bengali religious sect as well as a musical tradition.
                            - Baul Sangeet is influenced by Hindu Bhakti and Sufi traditions.
                            - Represents mysticism through songs.
                            - Notable exponents: Yotin Das, Purno Chandra Das, Lalon Fakir, Naboni Das, Sanatan Das Thakur Baul.
                            - 
                    - South India
                        - Kolannalu / Kolattam (Andhra Pradesh)
                            - A music and dance combination, similar to Dandiya.
                            - Ancient stick dance involving rhythmic movements in two circles.
                            - 
                        - Bhavageete (Karnataka, Maharashtra)
                            - Emotional songs, musically similar to Ghazals.
                            - Sung at a slower pitch.
                            - Themes include nature, love, and philosophy.
                    - Central & North-Eastern India
                        - Pandavani (Chhattisgarh)
                            - Based on the Mahabharata, focusing on Bhima as the hero.
                            - Includes gayan (singing) and vadan (playing instruments).
                            - Accompanied by tambura.
                            - Famous artist: Teejan Bai (Padma Shri 1987, Padma Bhushan 2003, Padma Vibhushan 2019).
                        - Khongjom Parba (Manipur)
                            - A ballad narrating the Battle of Khongjom (1891).
                            - Describes the struggle between British and Manipuri forces.
                    - Western India – Rajasthan & Gujarat
                        - Panihari (Rajasthan)
                            - Thematically related to water.
                            - Depicts women fetching water and village life.
                            - Sometimes romantic or about mother-in-law vs. daughter-in-law conflicts.
                            - 
                        - Maand (Rajasthan)
                            - Developed in royal courts, also recognized in classical circles.
                            - Lies between Ragas and folk songs.
                            - Sung by bards praising Rajput rulers.
                            - Famous song: Kesariya Balam (in Maand style).
                        - Dandiya Raas (Gujarat)
                            - Associated with Holi and Krishna-Radha’s Lila in Vrindavan.
                            - Main dance of Navratri celebrations.
                            - In Dandiya Raas, men and women dance in two circles with sticks.
                            - Related form: Garba (Raas Garba).
                            - 
                - Other major folk music traditions from the country are:

                            Assam

                            Himachal Pradesh
                            This song celebrates the extra-marital romance
                        

                    

            - Fusion of Classical and Folk Music
                - Basic info
                    - Over time, classical and folk music have intermingled, giving rise to new musical forms that incorporate elements from both traditions. 
                    - Devotional music has played a major role in this fusion, as it was patronized by both royalty and commoners, leading to a blend of structured classical elements and free-flowing folk traditions.
                - Major Forms of Classical-Folk Fusion Music
                    - Bhajan (North India)
                        - Originated from the Bhakti movement, where saints spread the message of God through hymns.
                        - Lyrics are set to simple melodies and sung in one or more ragas.
                        - Themes: Stories from the Mahabharata, Ramayana, and lives of deities.
                        - Instruments: Chimta, Dholak, Dafli, Manjira.
                        - Major composers: Mirabai, Tulsidas, Surdas, Kabir.
                        - Famous singers: Anup Jalota, Anuradha Paudwal.
                    - Shabad (Punjab, Sikh Tradition)
                        - Devotional songs sung in Gurudwaras, dedicated to Sikh Gurus.
                        - Developed by: Guru Nanak and his disciple Mardana.
                        - Types:
                            - Raga-based Shabad singing.
                            - Traditional Shabad (from Adi Granth).
                            - Lighter versions for general audiences.
                        - Guru Granth Sahib is organized into ragas, each containing multiple Shabads.
                    - Qawwali (Sufi Tradition)
                        - Devotional music in praise of Allah, Prophet Muhammad, or Sufi saints.
                        - Sung in Urdu, Punjabi, Hindi, with some Brajbhasha and Awadhi words.
                        - Performed in Sufi shrines, often with intense build-up leading to a transcendental state.
                        - Instruments: Tabla, Dholak, Harmonium.
                        - Notable Qawwals: Sabri Brothers, Nusrat Fateh Ali Khan, Aziz Warsi.
                        - Possible Originator: Amir Khusrau (disputed claim).
                        - Amir Khusrau’s Contribution
                            - Invented the Sehtar, a three-stringed instrument.
                        - 
                    - Rabindra Sangeet (Bengal)
                        - Composed by Rabindranath Tagore.
                        - A blend of classical elements and Bengali folk strains.
                        - Themes: Worship, devotion to nature, beauty, love, life, and patriotism.
                        - More than 2,000 compositions are still performed today.
                        - 
                    - Haveli Sangeet (Rajasthan, Gujarat)
                        - Originally performed inside temples but later moved to public performances.
                        - Practiced by Pushtimarg Sampradaya, a community believing in Pushtimarg as a path to salvation.
                    - Some other varieties of the fusion of Classical and Folk are:

                                Kerala
                                Originated in the temples, the theme is religious in nature.
                            

                        


    - Musical Instruments
        - Music is created using various instruments, which can be classified into four major traditional categories based on their characteristics.
            - Avanaddha Vadya (Membranophones)    
                - 
                - These instruments have an outer membrane that is beaten to produce sound. 
                - They are also known as percussion instruments as they require striking to generate music.
                    - They usually have one or two faces covered with skin.
                    - The Bhumi Dundubhi (earth drum) is the most ancient instrument in this category.
                    - Common examples: Tabla, Drum, Dhol, Congo, Mridangam.
                        - Tabla is a key accompaniment in Hindustani classical music.
                        - Mridangam is used in Carnatic music performances.
            - Sushira Vadya (Aerophones - Wind Instruments)
                - 
                - These instruments produce sound through air vibration.
                    - Common examples: Bansuri (Flute), Shehnai, Pungi, Ninkirns.
                    - Shehnai is a double-reeded wind instrument.
                        - Ustad Bismillah Khan is considered the Shehnai King.
                    - Flute (Bansuri) is widely used and has been revered since the Vedic period.
                        - Lord Krishna’s flute is an iconic symbol in Hindu culture.
                        - Pandit Hariprasad Chaurasia is one of the most famous Indian flutists.
            - Ghana Vadya (Idiophones - Percussion Without Membranes)    
                - 
                - These are non-drum percussion instruments that do not require tuning.
                    - Examples: Manjira, Jaltarang, Kanch-tarang, Ghungroo, Ghatam, Khartal.
                    - Manjira (Jhanj) is a small brass cymbal used in religious ceremonies.
                        - Harappan Civilization excavations show the use of Manjira in ancient India.
                    - These instruments are primarily used for keeping rhythm and time.
            - Tata Vadya (Chordophones - String Instruments)
                - 
                - These instruments produce sound through vibrating strings, modified by hand.
                - Types of Tata Vadya:
                    - Bowed Instruments: Played using a bow.
                        - Examples: Sarangi, Esraj/Dilruba, Violin.
                    - Plectral Instruments: Strings are plucked using fingers or a plectrum.
                        - Examples: Sitar, Veena, Tanpura, Guitar.
                    - Struck Instruments: Played using small hammers or sticks.
                        - Examples: Gotuvadyam, Swaramandal.
                - Additional information
                    - Sarod: 
                        - The Bhangash family pioneered its 20th-century development.
                    - Sitar: 
                        - Played across various gharanas (Jaipur, Varanasi, Etawah/Imaad Khani).
                    - Veena: 
                        - One of the most ancient instruments, associated with Goddess Saraswati.
                        - Santoor
                            - A 100-string instrument, traditional to Jammu and Kashmir.
                            - Commonly used in Sufiana Kalam music.
        - Folk Musical Instruments
            - Apart from classical instruments, India has a rich tradition of folk instruments, categorized into four types:
                - Chordophones (String Instruments)
                    - Tumbi – Played during Bhangra in Punjab.
                    - Ektara (Tun Tuna) – A one-stringed instrument played by wandering monks.
                    - Dotara – A two-stringed instrument used by Bauls of Bengal.
                    - Chikara – A bowed instrument used in Rajasthan, Uttar Pradesh, and Madhya Pradesh.
                    - Dilruba/Esraj – Used in Punjab and Rabindra Sangeet (Eastern India).
                    - Onavillu – A bamboo instrument from Kerala.
                    - Sarinda – A tribal instrument used by Santhals in Eastern India, Rajasthan, and Assam, similar to the Sarangi.
                - Aerophones (Wind Instruments)
                    - Pungi (Been) – Used by snake charmers, made from a dried bottle gourd and bamboo.
                    - Algoza – A double flute, popular in North-West India, especially Punjab.
                    - Tangmuri – A folk instrument of the Khasi people in Meghalaya.
                    - Titti – A bagpipe-like instrument made from goat skin, used in Kerala and Andhra Pradesh.
                    - Mashak – A folk bagpipe used in Garhwal (Uttarakhand), Rajasthan, and Uttar Pradesh.
                    - Gogona – A bamboo instrument used in the Bihu festival of Assam.
                - Membranophones (Drum-Based Instruments)
                    - Ghumot – A drum played during Ganesh festival in Goa.
                    - Idakka – A damru-like instrument from Kerala.
                    - Udukai – An hourglass-shaped instrument like damru, from Tamil Nadu.
                    - Sambal – A drum played with sticks in the Konkan region of Maharashtra.
                    - Tamak – A two-headed drum used by the Santhal tribe.
                    - Diggi – A folk drum from Ghariya village, Uttar Pradesh.
                - Idiophones (Self-Sounding Percussion Instruments)
                    - Chimta – Evolved from fire tongs, used in Punjab.
                    - Gharha – Earthen pots, used in folk music of Punjab.
                    - Andelu – Used in Burra-Katha, made of hollow metal rings.

    - Modern Developments in Music
        - Basic info
            - Music has been evolving continuously since its inception in the early historic period. 
            - The 21st century has seen significant advancements in the music industry, including the establishment of institutions that teach music academically and provide theoretical knowledge. 
            - Additionally, there has been a rise in organizations that showcase artists’ talent to the public through open performances.
        - Key Developments
            - Gandharva Mahavidyalaya
                - Founded by V.D. Paluskar in 1901 to preserve and teach Indian classical music and dance.
                - Originally established in Lahore, later shifted to Mumbai in 1915.
                - Focuses on both Hindustani and Carnatic classical music.
            - Prayag Sangeet Samiti
                - Established in 1926 in Allahabad (now Prayagraj).
                - Dedicated to imparting education in Hindustani classical music.
            - Sangeet Natak Akademi
                - Founded in 1952 as India’s first national academy for performing arts.
                - Established by the Government of India to promote music, drama, and dance.
                - Serves as the primary body for showcasing performing arts and preserving India’s intangible heritage.
            - Marris College of Music (Bhatkhande Music Institute Deemed University)
                - Founded in 1926 by legendary musicologist Vishnu Narayan Bhatkhande in Lucknow.
                - One of India’s premier institutions for classical music.
                - Focuses on both theory and practical training in singing and playing instruments.
                - Later renamed Bhatkhande Music Institute Deemed University.
            - SPIC MACAY (Society for Promotion of Indian Classical Music and Culture Amongst Youth)
                - Founded in 1977 by Kiran Seth as a voluntary youth movement.
                - Aims to revive interest in Indian classical music, dance, yoga, meditation, and cultural heritage.
                - Organizes free-entry events to engage young audiences.
                - Has expanded into a global organization with multiple chapters.
                - Headquarters: Delhi.
                - 
            - Chaturprahar Festival
                - An annual Indian classical music festival held at the National Centre for Performing Arts (NCPA), Mumbai.
                - Based on the concept of time association with ragas, where specific ragas are performed according to the time of the day.

    - Communities related to music community
        

            

                State

                Langha (Muslim Tribal Community) - Entertain Muslim Rajputs by praising them through songs.
            

            

                Rajasthan
                Manganiar or Merasi (Muslim Community) - Use an instrument Kamaicha to sing classical folk music.
            

            

                West Bengal
                Karta Bhajas - They have four classes and are indulged in chanting mantras leading to perfection as per their belief.
            

            

                West Bengal
                Bauls - Mystic group of singers in the countryside. They are one of the classes of Kartabhaja.

                Muria tribes - Sing songs called Relo.

                Tamil Nadu
                Pulayar hill tribes - They perform melodies called Talams.
            

            

                Arunachal Pradesh
                Idu Mishmis - Perform ritual dance and fertility dance.
            

            

                Andaman and Nicobar
                Moken Tribe - They have no religion and perform only ancestor worship. They perform a ritual tribal dance.
            

            

                Punjab
                Bazigar (Goaar) Community - They perform in festivals and occasions and display strength, balance, and courage.
            

        


- INDIAN DANCE FORMS
    - Background
        - Natya Veda: 
            - Created by Lord Brahma as the fifth Veda, combining elements from the four Vedas:
            - Pathya (words) – from the Rig Veda
            - Abhinaya (gestures) – from the Yajur Veda
            - Geet (music) – from the Sam Veda
            - Rasa (emotions) – from the Atharva Veda
        - Sacred Importance of Dance:
            - Shiva’s Tandava symbolizes creation, preservation, and destruction.
            - Parvati’s response represents grace and harmony.
            - Dance is deeply rooted in Indian mythology.
        - Historical Evidence:
            - Bhimbetka cave paintings depict community dances.
            - Bronze Dancing Girl (Harappan Civilization) highlights dance as a social activity.
        - Natya Shastra (200 BCE – 200 CE):
            - The first formal reference to Indian dance.
            - A comprehensive treatise detailing:
                - Postures, techniques, emotions, and expressions.
                - Ornaments, costumes, stage setup, and audience engagement.
            - Describes dance as the “complete art”, integrating music, sculpture, poetry, and drama.

    - Aspects of Dance
        - According to Natya Shastra, Indian classical dance has two fundamental aspects:
            - Lasya 
                – Represents grace, bhava (expression), rasa (emotion), and abhinaya (acting).
                - Symbolizes the feminine elements of dance.
            - Tandava 
                – Focuses on rhythm and movement.
                - Symbolizes the masculine aspects of dance.
        - According to Abhinaya Darpan (5th–4th century BCE) by Nandikeshwara, a dance performance consists of three key elements:
            - Nritta 
                – Pure rhythmic dance steps with no expression or storytelling.
            - Natya 
                – Dramatic representation, where a story is conveyed through dance.
            - Nritya 
                – Expressive dance involving sentiment and emotions through mudras (hand gestures) and facial expressions.
        - Nayaka-Nayika Bhav
            - The deity (Nayaka - hero) is the eternal subject of devotion.
            - The devotee (Nayika - heroine) expresses love and surrender through dance.
        - Nine Rasas (Emotions) Expressed in Dance
            - Shringara – Love
            - Roudra – Anger
            - Bibhatsa – Disgust
            - Veera – Heroism
            - Shaant – Peace & Tranquility
            - Haasya – Laughter & Comedy
            - Karuna – Tragedy
            - Bhayanak – Horror
            - Adbhuta – Wonder
        - Mudras (Hand Gestures & Postures)
            - 108 fundamental mudras are used in various combinations to depict specific emotions and expressions in dance.

    - Indina classical dance forms
        - Evolution of Indian Dance Styles:
            - Different regions of India have developed distinct dance styles, each with unique nuances.
            - Despite variations, all dance forms follow the fundamental rules of the Natya Shastra.
        - Guru-Shishya Parampara:
            - True knowledge transfer occurs only through a guru.
            - The guru imparts knowledge of different traditions (sampradayas) to the disciple.
            - This tradition forms the core of Indian classical dance.
        - Eight Classical Dance Forms (As per Sangeet Natak Akademi):
            - Bharatanatyam
                - 
                - Basic info
                    - Oldest among all classical dance forms.
                    - The name derives from Bharata Muni and Natyam (Tamil for dance).
                    - Some scholars attribute Bharata to:
                        - Bhava (expression)
                        - Raga (melody)
                        - Tala (rhythm)
                - Origins and Revival:
                    - Evolved from Sadir, a solo dance performed by Devadasis in Tamil Nadu.
                    - Previously known as Dashiattam.
                    - Declined with the end of the Devadasi system.
                    - Revived by E. Krishna Iyer, a freedom fighter.
                    - Gained global recognition through Rukmini Devi Arundale.
                    - Originally performed by solo female dancers, now popular among male and group artists.
                - Tanjore Quartet and the Bharatnatyam Recital:
                    - Four dance teachers from Thanjavur (Chinnaiah, Ponniah, Vadivelu, Shivanandam) structured the recital.
                    - Bharatnatyam is also called Tanjore Natyam in their honor.
                - Elements of a Bharatnatyam Recital:
                    - Alarippu: 
                        - Invocatory piece with rhythmic syllables, seeking divine blessings.
                    - Jatiswaram: 
                        - Pure dance (Nritta), focusing on poses and movements without expressions.
                    - Shabdam: 
                        - Expressive (Abhinaya) piece, usually in praise of God.
                    - Varnam: 
                        - Combination of dance and emotions (Nritya), most important part of the performance.
                    - Padam: 
                        - Mastery of Abhinaya (expressions) with a spiritual message.
                    - Jawali: 
                        - Short love-lyrics performed at a fast tempo.
                    - Thillana: 
                        - Concluding pure dance (Nritta) with intricate rhythmic variations.
                - Distinct Features of Bharatnatyam:
                    - Known as the “fire dance”, movements resemble a dancing flame.
                    - Balances both Tandava (vigorous) and Lasya (graceful) aspects.
                    - Emphasizes Mudras (hand gestures), especially Katakamukha Hasta, symbolizing “Om”.
                        - 
                    - Characterized by Ekacharya Lasyam, where one dancer plays multiple roles.
                    - Performed with bent knees and equal weight distribution on both feet.
                - Famous Proponents:
                    - Yamini Krishnamurthy, Lakshmi Viswanathan, Padma Subramaniam, Mrinalini Sarabhai, Mallika Sarabhai, etc.
            - Kuchipudi
                - 
                - Origin and Evolution
                    - Originated as a group performance by Kusselavas, traveling actors.
                    - Named after the village Kusselavapuri (or Kuchelapuram) in Andhra Pradesh.
                    - Systematized in the 17th century by Siddhendra Yogi, who authored Bhama Kalapam and other plays.z
                    - Historical Development:
                        - Influenced by Vaishnavism, became a male Brahmin monopoly and temple performance.
                        - Bhagavata Purana became the central theme, and performers were called Bhagavathalus.
                        - Flourished under Vijayanagara and Golconda rulers but remained confined to villages.
                        - Revived in the 20th century by Balasaraswati and Ragini Devi.
                        - Lakshminarayan Sastri introduced solo performances and female participation.
                - Key Features of Kuchipudi:
                    - Involves intricate foot movements and is primarily a group performance.
                    - Recitals are based on Bhagavata Purana but often have secular themes, emphasizing Shringara Rasa.
                    - Each principal character introduces itself with a Daaru (a short dance-song composition).
                    - Incorporates all three classical dance elements:
                        - Nritta (pure dance)
                        - Natya (dramatic element)
                        - Nritya (expressive dance)
                    - Similar to Bharatanatyam but distinct in style.
                - Elements of Performance:
                    - Sollakath (Patakshara): Nritta component, focusing on body movements.
                    - Kavutvams: Nritya component, involving acrobatics; sometimes performed as Nritta.
                    - Symbolizes the manifestation of earthly elements in the human body.
                    - Combines dance and drama, with dancers sometimes singing as well.
                    - Balances both Lasya (graceful) and Tandava (vigorous) elements.
                - Solo Performance Elements:
                    - Manduk Shabdam: Story of a frog.
                    - Tarangam: Dancer performs on the edge of a brass plate while balancing a pot of water or lit lamps.
                    - Jala Chitra Nrityam: Dancer draws patterns on the floor with their toes while dancing.
                - Music and Accompaniment:
                    - Performed with Carnatic music, mainly in Telugu.
                    - Principal instruments: Violin and Mridangam.
                - Famous Proponents:
                    - Radha Reddy, Raja Reddy, Yamini Krishnamurthy, Indrani Rahman, etc.
            - Kathakali
                - 
                - Origins & Evolution:
                    - Developed from Ramanattam and Krishnattam, two dance-dramas of Kerala.
                    - Patronized by feudal lords, narrating episodes from Ramayana and Mahabharata.
                    - Closely linked to Koodiyattam (Sanskrit drama tradition) and ancient martial arts.
                    - Combination of music, dance, and drama.
                    - Declined with the fall of feudalism but revived in the 1930s by V. N. Menon under Mukunda Raja’s patronage.
                - Key Features:
                    - Performed by an all-male troupe.
                    - Minimal props but elaborate facial makeup and headgear:
                        - Green: Nobility, divinity, virtue.
                        - Red patches near the nose: Royalty.
                        - Black: Evil, wickedness.
                        - Yellow: Saints, women.
                        - Completely red face: Evil.
                        - White beard: Higher consciousness, divinity.
                    - Intertwined dance and drama—not clearly separable.
                    - Represents the eternal conflict between good and evil, drawing from epics and puranas.
                    - Known as the Ballad of the East.
                    - Language: Manipravalam (a mix of Malayalam and Sanskrit).
                    - Music: Crucial for conveying drama; different compositions add depth.
                    - Gestures:
                        - Key feature of Navarasas (nine facial expressions) expressed through eye and eyebrow movements.
                        - Extensive hand gestures require rigorous training.
                    - Performance Setting:
                        - Open-air theatres or temple premises with lush greenery as the backdrop.
                        - Lighting: Traditional brass lamps.
                    - Symbolism: Represents the element of sky/ether.
                    - Performance begins and ends with drumbeats from Chhenda and Maddala.
                    - Famous Proponents:
                        - Guru Kunchu Kurup, Gopi Nath, Kottakal Sivaraman, Rita Ganguly, etc.
            - Mohiniattam
                - 
                - Origins & Evolution:
                    - Mohini means beautiful woman and Attam means dance.
                    - Solo female dance form, further developed by Vadivelu in the 19th century.
                    - Gained prominence under Travancore rulers, especially Swathi Thirunal.
                    - Revived by V. N. Menon and Kalyani Amma.
                - Key Features:
                    - Blends the grace of Bharatanatyam with the vigour of Kathakali.
                    - Gentle footwork, no forceful stamping.
                    - Narrates the feminine dance of Vishnu.
                    - Combines Nritta and Nritya like other classical dances.
                    - Dominated by Lasya (beauty & grace), making it primarily a female dance form.
                    - Accompanied by music & songs.
                    - Costume:
                        - White & off-white with gold brocade designs.
                        - No elaborate facial makeup.
                        - Ankle bells (Ghungroo) attached to a leather strap.
                    - Symbolism: Represents the element of air.
                    - Movements:
                        - Atavakul (Atavus): A collection of 40 basic movements.
                    - Musical Accompaniment:
                        - Instruments: Cymbals, Veena, Drums, Flute, etc.
                - Famous Proponents:
                    - Sunanda Nair, Kalamandalam Kshemavathy, Madhuri Amma, Jayaprabha Menon, etc.
            - Odissi
                - 
                - Origins & Evolution:
                    - Oldest classical dance from Eastern India.
                    - Earliest evidence found in Udayagiri-Khandagiri caves.
                    - Mentioned in Natya Shastra as Odra Nritya.
                    - Practiced by Mahris (temple dancers), patronized by Jain King Kharavela.
                    - Vaishnavism led to the decline of the Mahari system; replaced by Gotipuas (young boys dressed as females).
                    - Nartala variant continued in royal courts.
                    - Gained international acclaim in the mid-20th century due to Charles Fabri and Indrani Rahman.
                - Key Features:
                    - Similar to Bharatanatyam in using Mudras & postures for expression.
                    - Signature Postures:
                        - Tribhanga: Three-bent posture.
                            - 
                        - Chowk: Squared stance, symbolizing masculinity.
                    - Movements:
                        - Lower body remains static, while torso moves gracefully.
                        - Hand gestures convey expressions in Nritya.
                    - Known as ‘Mobile Sculpture’ due to its intricate body shapes & patterns.
                - Elements of Odissi:
                    - Mangalacharan: Begins with an offering of flowers to Mother Earth.
                    - Batu Nritya: Includes Tribhanga & Chowk postures.
                    - Pallavi: Expresses the essence of the song.
                    - Tharijham: A pure dance section before the conclusion.
                    - Two types of concluding sequences:
                        - Moksha: Joyful movements signifying liberation.
                        - Trikhanda Majura: Performer bids farewell to the gods, audience, and stage.
                - Music & Accompaniment:
                    - Hindustani Classical Music.
                    - Instruments: Manjira (Cymbals), Pakhawaj (Drums), Sitar, Flute, etc.
                    - Lyrics: Gita Govinda by Jayadeva and compositions by local poets.
                - Symbolism: Represents the element of water.
                - Costume & Jewelry:
                    - Elaborate hairstyles, silver jewelry, long necklaces.
                - Famous Proponents:
                    - Guru Pankaj Charan Das, Guru Kelu Charan Mohapatra, Sonal Mansingh, Sharon Lowen (USA), Anandini Dasi (Argentina).
            - Manipuri
                - 
                - Origins & Evolution
                    - Mythologically linked to the celestial dance of Shiva and Parvati in the valleys of Manipur, accompanied by the local Gandharvas.
                    - Gained prominence in the 15th century with the advent of Vaishnavism, making Krishna the central theme.
                    - Traditionally performed by females.
                    - 18th century: Raja Bhag Chandra attempted to revive the dance.
                    - Rabindranath Tagore reintroduced it to the national stage by incorporating it into Shantiniketan (West Bengal).
                - Key Features
                    - Emphasizes devotion over sensuality.
                    - Thin veils cover the dancers’ faces; hand gestures and gentle foot movements hold greater importance than facial expressions.
                    - Incorporates both Tandava and Lasya, with greater emphasis on Lasya.
                    - Female dancers wear unique long skirts, focusing on slow, graceful hand and knee movements.
                    - Nagabandha mudra (body curved in an ‘8’ shape) is a key posture.
                        - 
                    - Ras Leela (Radha-Krishna love story) is a recurring theme.
                    - Musical accompaniment includes Pung (drum), flute, Khartals (wood clappers), dhols, with compositions from Jayadeva and Chandidas.
                    - Influences can be seen in Thang-Ta and Sankirtana traditions.
                - Famous Exponents
                    - Jhaveri Sisters (Nayana, Suverna, Ranjana, Darshana)
                    - Guru Bipin Singha
                    - N. Madhabi Devi
            - Kathak
                - 
                - Origins & Evolution
                    - Originated from the Ras Leela tradition of Brajbhoomi.
                    - Derives its name from Kathikas (storytellers) who narrated epics through gestures and music.
                    - Mughal Era:
                        - Shifted into a court dance with Persian influences.
                        - Became more lascivious in style.
                    - 20th century: Lady Leela Sokhey led efforts to revive the classical Kathak tradition.
                - Key Features
                    - Developed into different gharanas within the Hindustani musical tradition:
                        - Lucknow: Flourished under Nawab Wajid Ali Shah, emphasizing expression and grace.
                        - Jaipur: Founded by Bhanuji, known for fluency, speed, and rhythmic complexity.
                        - Raigarh: Patronized by Raja Chakradhar Singh, focusing on percussion music.
                        - Banaras: Developed under Janakiprasad, emphasizing floor movements and symmetry.
                    - Characterized by intricate footwork and pirouettes.
                - Elements of a Kathak Recital
                    - Ananda 
                        – Introductory sequence, marking the dancer’s stage entry.
                    - Thaat 
                        – Soft, varied movements.
                    - Todas & Tukdas 
                        – Short, fast-paced rhythm sections.
                    - Jugalbandi 
                        – A competitive interplay between dancer and tabla player.
                    - Padhant 
                        – Dancer recites bols (syllabic rhythms) before demonstrating them.
                    - Tarana 
                        – A rhythmic movement sequence similar to Thillana.
                    - Kramalaya 
                        – Concluding intricate footwork segment.
                    - Gat Bhaav 
                        – Storytelling through silent movements, without music or chanting.
                - Music & Accompaniment
                    - Traditionally accompanied by Dhrupad music.
                    - Taranas, Thumris, and Ghazals were introduced during the Mughal period.
                - Famous Exponents
                    - Birju Maharaj
                    - Lacchu Maharaj
                    - Sitara Devi
                    - Damayanti Joshi
            - Sattriya
                - 
                - Origins & Evolution
                    - Introduced in 15th century Assam by Vaishnava saint Sankaradeva.
                    - Originated in Vaishnava monasteries (Sattras), where it was primarily practiced.
                    - Finds mention in Natya Shastra by Bharat Muni.
                    - Inspired by the Bhakti Movement.
                - Key Features
                    - Fusion of Assamese dance traditions, mainly Ojapali and Devdasi.
                    - Devotional in nature, narrating mythological stories of Vishnu.
                    - Comprises three main components:
                        - Nritta (pure dance)
                        - Nritya (expressive dance)
                        - Natya (dramatic representation)
                    - Traditionally performed in groups by male monks (Bhokots) as part of daily rituals and festivals.
                    - Strong emphasis on rhythmic syllables, dance postures, and intricate footwork.
                    - Combines both Lasya and Tandava elements.
                    - Strictly codified in terms of hand gestures and footwork.
                - Music & Accompaniment
                    - Instruments used: Khol (drum), Cymbals (Manjira), Flute.
                    - Lyrics are mainly Borgeets (compositions of Sankaradeva).
                - Costume & Jewelry
                    - Male dancers: Dhoti and Paguri (turban).
                    - Female dancers: Ghuri and Chador (made of Pat silk), with traditional Assamese jewelry.
                    - Waist cloth is worn by both genders.
                - Modern Evolution
                    - Developed into two distinct styles:
                        - Gayan-Bhayanar Nach – Focuses on singing and dancing.
                        - Kharmanar Nach – A more stylized form.
                    - Ankia Naat:
                        - A play or musical-drama using Sattriya techniques.
                        - Originally written in an Assamese-Maithili hybrid called Brajavali.
                        - Bhaona is a related form based on Krishna’s stories.
        - Recognition
            - Sangeet Natak Akademi recognizes eight classical dance forms.
            - Ministry of Culture acknowledges nine, including Chhau.

    - Folk Dances of India
        - Basic info
            - India’s folk dances reflect its diverse cultural heritage, incorporating local songs, myths, and traditions. 
            - These dances are spontaneous and untrained, often specific to a region or community, passed down through generations.
        - Major Folk Dances of India:
        - Major Folk Dances of India
            - Eastern India
                - Chhau (Jharkhand, Odisha, West Bengal)
                    - 
                    - Originates from ‘Chhaya’ (shadow).
                    - A martial mask dance narrating mythological and natural themes.
                    - Three styles:
                        - Saraikella Chhau (Jharkhand)
                        - Mayurbhanj Chhau (Odisha) – No masks.
                        - Purulia Chhau (West Bengal)
                    - Recognized by UNESCO (2010) as an Intangible Cultural Heritage.
                - Jat-Jatin (Bihar, Mithila region)
                    - Depicts the love and quarrels of a married couple.
                - Biraha / Bidesia (Bihar)
                    - Expresses women’s sorrow for absent partners.
                    - Performed only by males, who play female roles.
                - Jhumair (Jharkhand, Odisha)
                    - A harvest dance, with two variations:
                        - Janani Jhumair (women)
                        - Mardana Jhumair (men)
                - Danda-Jatra (Odisha)
                    - One of India’s oldest folk arts, blending dance, drama, and music.
                    - Themes revolve around Shiva, social harmony, and brotherhood.
                - Paika (Odisha)
                    - A martial dance performed with wooden spears and shields.
                    - The word Paika signifies battle.
                    - 
            - Western India
                - Garba (Gujarat)
                    - 
                    - Performed during Navratri.
                    - Revolves around a Garbha Deep (earthen lamp) placed inside a perforated pot.
                - Dandiya Raas (Gujarat)
                    - A stick dance, symbolizing Durga’s battle with Mahishasura.
                - Ghoomar (Rajasthan)
                    - Performed by Bhil women.
                    - Characterized by pirouetting movements and vibrant costumes.
                - Kalbelia (Rajasthan)
                    - 
                    - A sensuous dance mimicking serpent movements.
                    - Uses Been (snake charmer’s flute).
                    - Recognized by UNESCO (2010) as an Intangible Cultural Heritage.
                - Tera Tali (Rajasthan)
                    - Performed by the Kamar tribe.
                    - Women sit on the ground, playing cymbals (Manjiras) tied to their bodies.
                    - 
                - Tippani (Gujarat, Saurashtra region)
                    - Performed exclusively by women, who beat floors with wooden sticks.
            - Northern India
                - Bhangra & Giddha (Punjab)
                    - Bhangra: A high-energy men’s dance, with drum beats.
                    - Giddha: The female counterpart of Bhangra.
                        - 
                - Charba (Himachal Pradesh)
                    - A Dussehra festival dance.
                - Jawara (Madhya Pradesh, Bundelkhand region)
                    - A harvest dance, performed with a basket of jawar (sorghum) on the head.
                    - 
                - Matki (Madhya Pradesh, Malwa region)
                    - Solo dance, performed by women balancing earthen pots.
                    - Variants: Aada and Khada Nach.
                - Dumhal (Jammu & Kashmir)
                    - Performed by the Wattal tribe.
                    - Features tall conical hats and colorful costumes.
                    - 
            - Southern India
                - Kolattam-Parichakali (Kerala, Lakshadweep)
                    - A martial dance using wooden sticks and shields.
                - Kaikottikali (Kerala)
                    - A temple dance, performed during Onam.
                - Padayani (Kerala)
                    - A martial dance honoring Goddess Kali.
                    - Dancers wear large masks (Kolams).
                    - 
                - Kummi (Tamil Nadu, Kerala)
                    - A women’s dance, performed in a circle with rhythmic clapping.
                - Mayilattam (Tamil Nadu, Kerala)
                    - Peacock dance, where young girls dress as peacocks.
                    - 
                - Butta Bommalu (Andhra Pradesh, West Godavari district)
                    - Dancers wear mask-like costumes, resembling toy figures.
                - Pata Kunitha (Karnataka, Mysore region)
                    - A religious dance, where men carry bamboo poles decorated with ribbons.
                - Hulivesha (Coastal Karnataka)
