                - Guru Har Rai & Guru Har Krishan: Detained by Aurangzeb.
                - Guru Tegh Bahadur:
                    - Opposed Aurangzeb’s policies.
                    - Executed in Delhi (1675).
            - Guru Gobind Singh & Foundation of Khalsa (1699)
                - Last physical Guru—ended the system of personal Guruship.
                - Transferred authority to:
                    - Guru Granth Sahib (Adi Granth) – Sikh holy scripture.
                    - Guru Panth – The collective Sikh community.
                - Founded the Khalsa warrior community.
                - Initiated the first five Sikhs (“Panj Pyare”) into Khalsa.
                - Declared:
                    - All baptized Sikh men to take the name “Singh”.
                    - All baptized Sikh women to take the name “Kaur”.
            - Khalsa & Sikh Identity
                - Introduced distinct Sikh identity through dress and symbols.
                - Five Ks of Khalsa (Panj Kakar):
                    1.	Kesh – Uncut hair.
                    2.	Kangha – Wooden comb.
                    3.	Kara – Iron bracelet.
                    4.	Kirpan – Dagger/sword.
                    5.	Kachcha – Cotton undergarment.
                - Distinction between:
                    - Khalsa Sikhs – Baptized warriors of Guru Gobind Singh.
                    - Non-Khalsa Sikhs (Sahajdhari Sikhs, Nanak-Panthis, Bhallas, Udasis) – Followed Sikh principles but not Khalsa initiation.
        - Zoroastrianism
                - Origins & Beliefs
                    - Founded by Prophet Zarathushtra in 7th century BC in Persia.
                    - Monotheistic religion – Worships Ahura Mazda, the epitome of justice and goodness.
                    - Eternal struggle between:
                        - Ahura Mazda (good).
                        - Angra Mainyu (evil).
                    - End of times belief: One day, good will triumph over evil.
                - Zoroastrians in India
                    - Arrived in India between 8th–10th century AD, fleeing Islamic invasions in Iran.
                    - Two major communities:
                        - Parsis – Larger group, settled in Mumbai, Goa, Ahmedabad.
                        - Iranis – A smaller, more recent group in India.
                    - One of the smallest and rapidly shrinking communities in India.
                - Sacred Texts: Zend Avesta
                    - Written in Old Avestan.
                    - Consists of 17 sacred songs (Gathas) and Athuna Vairyo (sacred chant).
                    - Translations & glossaries of these texts are called Zend.
                    - Divided into five parts:
                        - Yasna – Worship with ceremony and offerings.
                        - Vendidad – Laws against demons and evil spirits.
                        - Yashts – Worship through praise.
                        - Khordeh Avesta – Common prayer book.
                        - Gathas – A part of Yasna, further divided into:
                            - Ahunavaiti
                            - Ushtavaiti
                            - Spenta-Mainyu
                            - Vohu-Khshathra
                            - Vahishto-Ishti
                - Worship & Rituals
                    - Fire Worship: Fire is sacred, along with earth.
                    - Temple Worship:
                        - Temples called Atash Bahram.
                        - Only eight exist in India (details in Chapter 1).
                    - Death Rituals:
                        - Dead bodies are considered corrupting.
                        - Traditionally placed in Dakhma (Towers of Silence) to be consumed by vultures (Dakhma Nashini).
                            - 
                        - Only active Towers of Silence in Mumbai.
                        - Due to the Indian vulture crisis, some have started burying their dead.                
                - Qissa-i Sanjan
                    - It is an account of the migration of Zoroastrians (Parsis) and their settlement in the Indian Sub-continent. 
                    - Its first chapter ends with establishment of fire temple at Sanjan (Gujarat).
                - There are three major calendar forms among the Parsis:
                    

                        

                            Type
                            Description
                        

                        

                            Shahenshahi (Majority)
                            Calculates their calendar from the last Sassanian king, Yazdegerd III.
                        

                        

                            Qadimi
                            Claims to have the oldest calendar.
                        

                        

                            Fasli
                            Claimed to be the most accurate religious calendar and the most recent one.
                        

                    

        - Judaism
            - Origins & Beliefs
                - One of the oldest religions but has faced significant persecution.
                - Monotheistic – Worships Yahweh, the one true God instituted by Abraham.
                - Predates Christianity and Islam, which have borrowed from Judaic philosophies.
                - Day of Judgment belief:
                    - A Messiah will come.
                    - The pious will go to heaven.
                    - The evil will be relegated to hell.
            - Sacred Texts
                - Torah – The primary religious text, also the first five books of Tanakh.
                - Talmud – A compilation of legal, ethical, and historical Jewish writings.
            - Major Figures in Judaism
                - Abraham – Ancestor of all Jews, blessed by God for following His injunctions.
                - Isaac & Jacob (Israel) – Sons of Abraham, continued the covenant with God.
                - Moses – Received the Ten Commandments (Sefer Torah) at Mount Sinai, guiding how Israelites (descendants of Jacob/Israel) should live.
                - Jacob’s 12 Sons – Ancestors of the 12 Tribes of Israel.
            - Religious Practices
                - Place of Worship: Synagogues.
                - Religious Services: Eliyahu-hanavi (Thanksgiving to Elijah the Prophet).
                - Prayer Rituals:
                    - Male Jews wear a tsisith (prayer shawl) during prayers.
            - Jewish Communities in India
                - First Jewish settlers arrived on the Western coast of India.
                - Though numerically small, India has five major Jewish communities:
                    - Cochin Jews – Malayalam-speaking Jews in Kerala.
                    - Bene Israel – Marathi-speaking Jews (Largest Jewish community in India).
                    - Baghdadi Jews – Traders from West Asia, settled in Surat, now mainly in Mumbai & Kolkata.
                    - Bnei Menashe – Claim descent from the lost Menasseh Tribe:
                        - Live in Manipur & Mizoram (India-Myanmar border).
                        - Descendants of Mizo, Kuki, and Chin tribes.
                        - Believe they were enslaved by Assyrians, escaped to China, and then migrated to India.
                        - Call themselves “Children of Menasseh.”
                    - Bene Ephraim – Telugu-speaking Jews, converted to Judaism in the 1990s.
            - Prominent synagogues in India
                

                    

                        Name
                        Located at
                        Affiliated to

                        Kolkata
                        Baghdadi Jews
                    

                    

                        Paradesi Synagogue
                        Cochin
                        Cochin Jews
                    

                    

                        Ohel David Synagogue (Largest in India, also called Lal Deval)
                        Pune
                        Baghdadi Jews
                    

                    

                        Knesset Eliyahoo Synagogue
                        Mumbai
                        Baghdadi Jews
                    

                    

                        Nariman House
                        Mumbai
                        It was one of the targets of the 2008 Mumbai attack.
                    

                

        - Other prominent religions in India
            - Sanamahism
                - Practiced mainly by the Meitei people of Manipur.
                - Involves shamanistic worship of:
                    - Ancestors
                    - Almighty God
                    - Sky
                    - Elements
                    - Forests
                - Sacred Text: Puya
                - Major Festival: Lai Haraoba (worship of traditional deities and ancestors).
                - Sanamahi: A household deity, generally kept at the southwestern corner of homes.
                - Note: This religion is not considered a part of Hinduism.
            - Ayyavazhi
                - Considered a part of Hinduism and followed in Tamil Nadu and Kerala.
                - Centered on the life and teachings of Ayya Vaikundar.
                - Sacred Texts:
                    - Akilathirattu Ammanai
                    - Arul Nool
                - Important Observance:
                    - Ayya Vaikunda Avataram (incarnation day of Vaikundar) is a state holiday in Tamil Nadu and Kerala.
                - Unique Worship Practice:
                    - Instead of a deity, worship centers place:
                        - Flame-shaped copper structure
                        - Saffron cloth
                        - Garlands of rudraksha and flowers
                    - This setup represents Elunetru, signifying a formless God.
            - Sarnaism (Adi Dharam)
                - Indigenous tribal religion followed by:
                    - Munda, Ho, Bhumij, Santal, Baiga, and Khuruk communities.
                - Found in Jharkhand, Odisha, West Bengal, Bihar, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, and Chhattisgarh.
                - Nature Worship:
                    - Centered on the worship of trees, especially the Sal Tree.
                - Place of Worship: Jaher Than (Sarna temples).
            - Bahai Faith
                - Originated in Persia (19th century).
                - Founder: Bahaullah, who claimed to be a prophet sent by God.
                - Core Beliefs:
                    - Monotheism – Belief in a single, all-powerful God.
                    - Goal of a unified world order ensuring prosperity for all races, creeds, and classes.
                    - Three Central Principles:
                        - Unity of God
                        - Unity of Religion
                        - Unity of Humanity
                - Major Worship Site in India: Lotus Temple (Delhi).
                    - 
                - Unique Calendar:
                    - Bahai Year consists of 19 months, each with 19 days.
                - Global Presence: Considered one of the fastest-growing religions in the world.
        

- Busshism and Jainism
    - Buddhism
        - Origins:
            - One of the major world religions, originating in the Indian subcontinent.
            - Has spread widely across South-East Asia.
            - Founded on the teachings, traditions, and beliefs of Siddhartha Gautama, who came to be known as Buddha.
        - Global Presence:
            - Fourth-largest religion in the world after Christianity, Islam, and Hinduism.
            - Followed by approximately 7% of the world’s population.
        - Buddhism in India:
            - 0.7% of India’s population (~8.4 million individuals) identify as Buddhists.
            - Maharashtra has the largest Buddhist population in India.

        - Basics About Buddha
            - 
            - Birth and Early Life:
                - Born as Siddhartha Gautama in 563 BC at Lumbini (present-day Nepal).
                - Parents: King Suddhodana (Sakyan Kingdom, Kshatriya Clan) and Queen Maya.
                - Born on the auspicious day of Vaishakhi Purnima.
                - Married Princess Yashodhara and had a son named Rahul.
            - The Great Renunciation:
                - At 29 years old, he left his palace on his horse Kanthaka, accompanied by Charioteer Channa, to seek the truth of life.
                - Wandered as an ascetic for six years.
            - Enlightenment (Nirvana):
                - At 35 years old, on the same day as his birth, he attained enlightenment under a pipal tree in Bodh Gaya (Bihar).
                - Became Buddha, the Enlightened One.
            - First Sermon & Establishment of Sangha:
                - After attaining Nirvana, he gave his first sermon at Deer Park, Sarnath (near Varanasi).
                - This event is known as Dharma-Chakra-Pravartana (Turning the Wheel of Law).
                - His first five disciples (companions at Sarnath):
                    1.	Kaudinya
                    2.	Bhadrika
                    3.	Vashpa
                    4.	Mahanaman
                    5.	Ashvajit
                - Together, they formed the Sangha (monastic order).
            - The Three Jewels (Triratna) of Buddhism:
                1.	Buddha – The Enlightened One.
                2.	Dhamma – The Teachings of Buddha (doctrine).
                3.	Sangha – The Monastic Order (established during the first sermon).
            - Final Years & Mahaparinirvana:
                - Attained Mahaparinirvana at Kushinagar (Malla Mahajanapada, Uttar Pradesh) at the age of 80 (c. 483 BC).
                - Contemporary of King Bimbisara (for most of his life) and Ajatashatru (Haryanka Dynasty, last few years).
            - Titles & Successors:
                - Also known as Tathagata and Sakyamuni in Buddhist texts.
                - Predecessor: Kassapa Buddha.
                - Successor: Maitreya (Future Buddha).
        - Early Buddhist Schools
            - After the passing of Buddha, the original Sangha is believed to have split into two major early schools between 383 BC and 250 BC:
                - Sthavira Nikaya
                    - Sub-sects:
                        - Mahisasaka
                        - Sarvastivada
                        - Sankrantika
                        - Sautrantika
                        - Dharmaguptaka
                        - Vatsiputriya
                        - Dharmottariya
                        - Bhadrayaniya
                        - Sannagarika
                        - Sammitiya
                - Mahasamghika
                    - Sub-sects:
                        - Gokulika
                        - Prajnaptivada
                        - Bahusrutiya
                        - Ekavyaharikas
                        - Caitika
            - Other Early Buddhist Sub-sects
                - Some sub-sects that did not fall under the two main schools:
                    - Hemavatika
                    - Rajagiriya
                    - Siddhatthaka
                    - Pubbaseliya
                    - Aparaseliya
                    - Apararajagirika
            - Later Buddhist Schools
                - Theravada
                - Mahayana
                - Vajrayana

        - Concepts and Philosophy in Buddhism
            - Buddhism is based on fundamental principles that explain the nature of suffering and the path to liberation.
            - The Four Noble Truths
                1.	Dukkha (The Truth of Suffering) – Life is full of suffering.
                2.	Samudaya (The Truth of the Origin of Suffering) – Suffering arises from desires and attachments.
                3.	Nirodha (The Truth of the Cessation of Suffering) – By eliminating desires, suffering can be ended.
                4.	Marga (The Truth of the Path to the Cessation of Suffering) – Liberation can be attained by following the Noble Eightfold Path.
            - The Noble Eightfold Path
                1.	Right Speech – Speak truthfully and kindly.
                2.	Right Action – Act honestly and peacefully.
                3.	Right Livelihood – Engage in a profession that does not cause harm.
                4.	Right Effort – Develop self-control and discipline.
                5.	Right Mindfulness – Be aware of thoughts and actions.
                6.	Right Meditation – Concentrate on the true meaning of life.
                7.	Right Thought – Cultivate sincerity and intelligence.
                8.	Right Understanding – Avoid superstition and seek wisdom.
            - Middle Path (Madhyam Marg)
                - Buddha emphasized the Middle Way, a balanced approach between extreme indulgence and severe asceticism. It serves as the foundation of the Noble Eightfold Path and leads to liberation.
            - Buddhism and Other Beliefs
                - Rejects the Vedas as authoritative scriptures.
                - Denies the existence of a permanent soul (atman), unlike Jainism.
            - Buddhist Councils and Tripitaka
                - After Buddha attained Mahaparinirvana at Kushinagar in 483 BC, efforts were made to preserve his teachings. 
                - Over 500 years, four Buddhist Councils were held, leading to the compilation of scriptures into three major texts, collectively called the Tripitaka, all written in Pali:
                    - Vinaya Pitaka – Rules for monastic discipline.
                    - Sutta Pitaka – Buddha’s discourses.
                    - Abhidhamma Pitaka – Philosophical and doctrinal analysis.
            - Major Schools of Buddhism
                - During the Fourth Buddhist Council under King Kanishka, Buddhism split into two major sects:
                    - Hinayana Buddhism – Focused on self-discipline and personal enlightenment.
                    - Mahayana Buddhism – Emphasized compassion and the worship of Bodhisattvas.
                - Later, two additional schools emerged:
                    - Theravada Buddhism – The oldest surviving form, focusing on strict adherence to Buddha’s original teachings and attaining Nirvana by practicing the Noble Eightfold Path.
                    - Vajrayana Buddhism – Incorporates esoteric rituals and meditation techniques.
            - Practices in Buddhism
                - Buddhist practice includes:
                    - Taking refuge in the Buddha, Dhamma, and Sangha.
                    - Studying scriptures and following moral precepts.
                    - Renouncing craving and attachment.
                    - Practicing meditation and cultivating wisdom, loving-kindness, and compassion.
                    - Bodhicitta (Mahayana) – The aspiration to attain Buddhahood for the benefit of all beings.
                    - Generation & Completion Stages (Vajrayana) – Advanced meditation practices.
                - Goals of Different Buddhist Schools
                    - Theravada Buddhism 
                        – Seeks Nirvana, the cessation of suffering and rebirth.
                    - Mahayana Buddhism 
                        – Aspires for Buddhahood through the Bodhisattva Path, where enlightened beings remain in the cycle of rebirth to help others attain awakening.
            - Four Buddhist Councils
                

                    

                        Buddhist Council
                        Place and Year
                        Patronage by and Chairman
                        Details
                    

                    

                        First Buddhist Council
                        At Rajgir (around 483 BC)
                        Under the patronage of King Ajatashatru. Chairman – Mahakasyapa
                        
                            - It was held at the Saptaparni cave immediately after the death of the Buddha. 

                            - Upali recited Vinaya Pitaka, which contains the rules of the Buddhist order. 

                            - Anand recited Sutta Pitaka, containing Buddha’s sermons on doctrine and ethics.
                        		
                    

                    

                        Second Buddhist Council
                        At Vaishali (383 BC)
                        Under the patronage of King Kalasoka. Chairman – Sabakami
                        
                            - Held approximately 100 years after Buddha’s death. 

                            - Mainly focused on 10 disputed points under the Vinaya Pitaka.
                        		
                    

                    

                        Third Buddhist Council
                        At Pataliputra (250 BC)
                        Under the patronage of King Ashoka. Chairman – Moggaliputta-Tissa
                        
                            - Compilation of Abhidhamma Pitaka took place.
                        
                    

                    

                        Fourth Buddhist Council
                        At Kundalvana, Kashmir (72 AD)
                        Under the patronage of King Kanishka. Chairman – Vasumitra
                        
                            - Resulted in the division of Buddhism into Hinayana and Mahayana. 

                            - Asvaghosha (deputy of Vasumitra) participated. 

                            - Deliberations were made in Sanskrit. 

                            - Another Fourth Buddhist Council was held in Sri Lanka.
                        		
                    

                

        - The Four Schools of Buddhism
            - Buddhism evolved into four major schools over time, each with distinct beliefs and practices.
                - Hinayana Buddhism
                    - Meaning: “Lesser Vehicle”
                    - Beliefs:
                        - Followers adhere to Buddha’s original teachings and maintain an orthodox approach.
                        - No idol worship; they do not believe in representing Buddha in physical form.
                        - Emphasizes individual salvation through self-discipline and meditation.
                        - The ultimate goal is Nirvana, achieved through personal effort.
                    - Sub-sect: Sthaviravada (Theravada)
                    - Language: Pali
                    - Historical Patronage: Emperor Ashoka supported Hinayana, as Mahayana developed much later.
                    - Current Status: The original form of Hinayana no longer exists.
                - Mahayana Buddhism
                    - Meaning: “Greater Vehicle”
                    - Beliefs:
                        - More liberal than Hinayana, emphasizing compassion and the divinity of Buddha and Bodhisattvas.
                        - Idol worship of Buddha and Bodhisattvas is accepted.
                        - Goal: Spiritual upliftment and the universal liberation of all beings from suffering.
                        - Introduces the concept of the Bodhisattva, one who delays Nirvana to help others attain enlightenment.
                        - Also called Bodhisattvayana or the Bodhisattva Vehicle.
                    - Sacred Texts: Lotus Sutra, Mahavamsa
                    - The Six Perfections (Paramitas) in Mahayana Buddhism:
                        1.	Dana – Generosity
                        2.	Sila – Morality and proper conduct
                        3.	Ksanti – Patience and tolerance
                        4.	Virya – Energy and effort
                        5.	Dhyana – Concentration
                        6.	Prajna – Wisdom and insight
                    - Sub-sect: Vajrayana Buddhism (developed later)
                    - Language: Sanskrit
                    - Historical Patronage: Emperor Kanishka (Kushana Dynasty, 1st century AD)
                    - Current Status: Majority of Buddhists (53.2% as of 2010) follow Mahayana.
                    - Countries Practicing Mahayana: Nepal, Bangladesh, Japan, Vietnam, Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Mongolia, China, Bhutan, Tibet, etc.
                    - Madhyamaka School (Sub-sect of Mahayana)
                        - Founder: Nagarjuna (2nd-3rd century AD)
                        - Belief: Based on Sunyata (emptiness) Doctrine, which teaches that all things are empty of inherent nature.
                        - Key Scholar: Chandrakirti
                - Theravada Buddhism
                    - Meaning: “School of Elder Monks”
                    - Sacred Texts: Pali Canon (the only complete Buddhist canon).
                    - Beliefs:
                        - The goal is to attain Nirvana by practicing the Noble Eightfold Path.
                        - Emphasizes Samatha (calmness) and Vipassana (insight) as essential meditation techniques.
                        - Three Marks of Existence: Impermanence, suffering, and non-self.
                        - Doctrine of Vibhajjavada (Teaching of Analysis).
                    - Major Text: Visuddhimagga (Path of Purification), written by Buddhaghosa in 5th century AD.
                    - Language: Pali
                    - Relation to Hinayana: Theravada is considered a successor of Hinayana.
                    - Current Status: Around 35.8% of Buddhists follow Theravada.
                    - Countries Practicing Theravada: Sri Lanka, Cambodia, Laos, Thailand, Myanmar.
                - Vajrayana Buddhism (Tantric Buddhism)
                    - Origin: Developed as a fusion of Buddhism and Shaivism under royal patronage.
                    - Main Deity: Tara (a female deity).
                    - Beliefs:
                        - Based on Mahayana philosophy.
                        - Incorporates Tantras, Mantras, and Yantras for a faster path to liberation.
                        - Mantras are considered a shortcut to Buddhahood compared to the Six Perfections (Paramitas) of Mahayana.
                    - Current Status: 5.7% of Buddhists follow Vajrayana.
                    - Countries Practicing Vajrayana: Tibet, Bhutan, Mongolia, Kalmykia.
        - Bodhisattva in Buddhism
            - A Bodhisattva is one who develops Bodhicitta, the compassionate desire to attain Buddhahood for the benefit of all beings.
            - Buddha himself, in his previous births (as per Jataka tales), was a Bodhisattva.
            - Path to Buddhahood: A Bodhisattva passes through 10 Bhumis (stages), including Great Joy, Radiant, Immovable, and Cloud of Dharma, before achieving enlightenment.
            - Famous Bodhisattvas
                - Avalokitesvara – Bodhisattva of compassion, depicted with a lotus flower (Padmapani). Revered in Ajanta Caves and worshipped in Theravada Buddhism (Cambodia). Also considered an incarnation of the Dalai Lama.
                - Vajrapani – Represents Buddha’s power and is associated with the Five Tathagatas.
                - Manjusri – Symbol of wisdom, depicted with a sword.
                    - 
                - Samantabhadra – Associated with meditation and practice.
                - Ksitigarbha – Took a vow not to attain Buddhahood until all hell beings are freed.
                - Maitreya – The Future Buddha, believed to bring a pure Dharma era. Laughing Buddha is considered an incarnation of Maitreya.
                - Akasagarbha – Associated with space.
                - Tara – Central to Vajrayana Buddhism, representing success and achievements.
                - Vasudhara – Associated with wealth and prosperity, worshipped in Nepal.
                - Skanda – Guardian of Buddhist monasteries and teachings.
                - Sitatapatra – Protector against supernatural dangers, worshipped in both Mahayana and Vajrayana.
            - Dipankara Buddha
                - A past Buddha who attained enlightenment before Gautama Buddha.
                - Recognized in Mahayana, Vajrayana, and Theravada traditions.
                - Maitreya is considered the next Buddha after Gautama.
        - Places Visited by Buddha
            - Buddha traveled extensively during his lifetime, primarily visiting regions within two major kingdoms: Kosala and Magadha.
                - Kosala Kingdom
                    - Capital: Shravasti
                    - Notable Places Visited:
                        - Kapilavastu (Buddha’s birthplace)
                        - Shravasti (capital of Kosala)
                        - Kosambi
                        - Saket
                - Magadha Kingdom
                    - Capital: Rajgriha
                    - Notable Places Visited:
                        - Rajgriha
                        - Vaishali
                        - Gaya
                        - Bodh Gaya (Buddha attained enlightenment)
                        - Nalanda
                        - Champapuri
                - Other Significant Places
                    - Sarnath (Buddha’s first sermon)
                    - Kushinagar (Buddha’s Mahaparinirvana)
                    - Mathura
                    - Varanasi
        - Prominent Disciples of Buddha in the Ancient Period
            - Chief Disciples
                - Sariputta 
                    – One of Buddha’s chief disciples, known for his deep wisdom.
                - Mahamoggallana 
                    – Another chief disciple, renowned for his mastery of supernatural powers.
            - Close Disciples and Followers
                - Ananda 
                    – Buddha’s attendant and the disciple who listened to his teachings the most.
                - Mahakasyapa 
                    – Led the First Buddhist Council after Buddha’s Mahaparinirvana.
                - Purna Maitrayani-putra 
                    – A prominent preacher of Buddha’s teachings.
                - Anuruddha 
                    – Known for his divine vision and meditation skills.
                - Rahul 
                    – Buddha’s son who became a monk.
                - Katyayana 
                    – Known for his expertise in explaining the Buddha’s teachings.
                - Upali 
                    – A former barber who became an expert in monastic discipline (Vinaya).
                - Anathapindika 
                    – A wealthy merchant and one of the most generous lay disciples.
                - Subhuti 
                    – Known for his mastery of emptiness (Sunyata) doctrine.
                - Jivaka 
                    – A renowned physician who treated Buddha and his disciples.
        '16 Arhats' of Buddha
            - Definition and Interpretation
                - In Theravada Buddhism, an Arhat is someone who has gained insight into the true nature of existence and has achieved nirvana.
                - In Mahayana Buddhism, an Arhat is a person who has advanced far on the path to nirvana but has not yet attained it.
            - Key Figures Among the 16 Arhats
                - Nagasena 
                    – A Sarvastivadan Buddhist scholar from the 2nd century BC, known for his dialogues in the Milindapanha.
                - Rahul 
                    – Gautam Buddha’s son, also considered one of the 16 Arhats.
            - Comparison: Arhat vs. Bodhisattva (Mahayana Perspective)
                - Arhat – Achieves peace and enlightenment for oneself but does not stay to help others.
                - Bodhisattva – An awakened being who chooses to remain in Samsara (cycle of rebirth) to assist others in reaching enlightenment.
                - Mahayana Buddhism favors the Bodhisattva path over the Arhat ideal, emphasizing compassion and selfless service.
        - Other Prominent Personalities associated with Buddhism
            - Nagasena: 
                - He answered to questions about Buddhism posed by Menander I (or Milinda), the Indo-Greek king, and the conversation is recorded in the book Milinda Panho around 150 BC.
            - Nagarjuna: 
                lived around 150-250 AD and founder of the Madhyamaka School of Mahayana Buddhism.
            - Vasubandhu: 
                - A proponent of Mahayana Buddhism in 4th-5th century AD from Gandhara and wrote from the perspectives of the Sarvastivada and Sautrantika schools.
            - Bodhidharma: 
                lived around 5th or 6th century AD and transmitted Buddhism to China.
            - Buddhaghosa: 
                - A 5th century Indian Theravada Buddhist commentator and scholar famous for his work Visuddhimagga (Path of Purification).
            - Padmasambhava: 
                - An 8th century monk and is contemplated as ‘Second Buddha’ across Tibet, Nepal, Bhutan, and the Himalayan States of India.
            - Atisa: 
                - A Buddhist Bengali religious leader and master, and is one of the major figures in the spread of 11th century Mahayana and Vajrayana Buddhism in Asia. He inspired Buddhist thought from Tibet to Sumatra.
            - Dalai Lama: 
                - They are modern spiritual leaders of the Yellow Hat School of Tibetan Buddhism.
        - Navayana Buddhism
            - Navayana (New Vehicle) Buddhism is a modern interpretation of Buddhism, propounded by Dr. B.R. Ambedkar in the 20th century.
            - It is distinct from Theravada, Mahayana, and Vajrayana and rejects their traditional doctrines.
            - Key Differences from Traditional Buddhism:
                - Discards concepts like monasticism, karma, rebirth, samsara, meditation, enlightenment, and the Four Noble Truths.
                - Radically reinterprets Buddha’s teachings to focus on class struggle and social equality.
            - It became a key movement for Dalit empowerment in India.

    - Jainism
        - Jain Teachings and Philosophy
            - 
            - Mahavira taught the right path (Dharma), emphasizing renunciation, strict asceticism, and moral cultivation. 
            - Jains are morally bound by their faith to live in a way that does not harm any living being.
        - Key Philosophical Concepts in Jainism
            - Anekantavada (Non-Absolutism)
                - The fundamental doctrine of Jainism states that truth and reality are complex and have multiple aspects.
                - No single statement can fully describe the nature of existence or the absolute truth.
            - Three Jewels (Ratnatraya) of Jainism
                - The path to liberation from karma and rebirth lies in following:
                    - Right belief (Samyak Darshana)
                    - Right knowledge (Samyak Gyana)
                    - Right conduct (Samyak Charitra)
        - Five Major Vows (Mahavratas) of Jainism
            - Jains must adhere to these five ethical principles in life:
                1.	Ahimsa (Non-violence) 
                    – Avoid harming any living being.
                2.	Satya (Truthfulness) 
                    – Speak only the truth.
                3.	Asteya (Non-stealing) 
                    – Do not take anything that is not given.
                4.	Aparigraha (Non-possessiveness) 
                    – Limit material possessions.
                5.	Brahmacharya (Chaste living) 
                    – Maintain celibacy or marital fidelity.
                    - The fifth tenet (Brahmacharya) was introduced by Mahavira.
        - Ashtamangala (Eight Auspicious Symbols in Jainism)
            

                

                    Symbol
                    Meaning
                

                

                    Swastika
                    It signifies peace and well-being of humans.
                

                

                    Nandavarta
                    
                        - A large swastika with nine endpoints. 

                        - One of the eight auspicious symbols of the Svetambara sect in Jainism. 

                        - An ashtamangala used for worship. 

                        - Has four arms with compulsory nine corners/turns. 

                        - Associated with the 18th Tirthankara, Aranatha.
                    		
                

                

                    Bhadrasana
                    A throne sanctified by the Jaina’s feet.
                

                

                    Shrivatsa (Endless Knot)
                    A mark on the chest of the Tirthankara image, signifying his pure soul.
                

                

                    Darpana
                    The mirror that reflects the inner self.
                

                

                    Minayugala
                    A pair of fish signifying conquest over sexual urges.
                

                

                    Vardhamanaka
                    A shallow dish used as a lamp, representing an increase in wealth, virtue, and merit.
                

                

                    Kalasha
                    A pot filled with pure water, signifying purity.
                

            

            - Special Symbol in Jainism
                - 
                - The hand with a wheel (Ahimsa Chakra) symbolizes non-violence (Ahimsa).
                - The word “Ahimsa” is written in the center of the wheel.
        - Two Major Schools (Sects) of Jainism
            - Jainism is divided into two major schools: Digambara and Svetambara, each with distinct beliefs and practices.
            - Digambara School
                - Monastic Practice:
                    - Monks of the Digambara sect do not wear clothes, as they believe in complete nudity as a form of renunciation.
                    - Female monks, known as Aryikas, wear unstiched plain white sarees.
                - Religious Beliefs:
                    - Digambaras strictly follow all five constraints (Ahimsa, Satya, Asteya, Aparigraha, and Brahmacharya), unlike Svetambaras.
                    - They believe women cannot be Tirthankaras, and they assert that Malli (the 19th Tirthankara) was a man.
                    - Monastic rules in Digambara tradition are more rigid.
                - Historical Background:
                    - Bhadrabahu, an exponent of the Digambara sect, migrated to Karnataka with his disciples after predicting a long famine.
                    - The earliest record of Digambara beliefs is found in the Prakrit Suttapahuda of Kundakunda.
                - Sub-sects of Digambara:
                    - Mula Sangh – The original Digambara monastic community.
                    - Bispanthi, Terapanthi, and Taranpanthi – Modern sub-sects.
                - Differences Between Digambara Terapanthis & Bispanthis:
                    - Bispanthis:
                        - Worship Tirthankaras along with Yaksha and Yakshini (e.g., Bhairava, Kshetrapala).
                        - Perform aarti and make offerings of flowers, fruits, and prasad.
                        - Their religious leaders, Bhattarakas, are mainly found in Rajasthan and Gujarat.
                    - Terapanthis:
                        - Worship only Tirthankaras, rejecting Bhattarakas.
                        - Use ashta-dravya for worship but replace flowers and fruits with dry substitutes.
            - Svetambara School
                - Monastic Practice:
                    - Monks wear simple white clothing and are allowed to have a begging bowl, books, writing materials, and a brush to remove insects from their path.
                - Religious Beliefs:
                    - Svetambaras follow the teachings of Parshvanatha, who prescribed only four constraints, omitting Brahmacharya.
                    - They believe the 23rd (Parshvanatha) and 24th (Mahavira) Tirthankaras were married, which Digambaras deny.
                    - Unlike Digambaras, Svetambaras accept that Malli was a woman and began life as a princess.
                    - They believe in five eternal substances:
                        - Soul (Jiva)
                        - Matter (Pudgala)
                        - Space (Akasha)
                        - Motion (Dharma)
                        - Rest (Adharma)
                    - In contrast, Digambaras add a sixth eternal substance: Time (Kala).
                - Historical Background:
                    - Sthulabhadra, a major exponent of this sect, remained in Magadha, while Bhadrabahu moved to Karnataka.
                - Sub-sects of Svetambara:
                    - Sthanakavasi:
                        - Reject idol worship and pray to saints instead.
                        - Their saints wear a muhapatti (mouth-covering cloth), unlike Murtipujakas.
                    - Murtipujaka (Deravasi):
                        - Keep idols of Tirthankaras in their temples and worship them.
                        - Their saints do not wear a muhapatti.
                    - Terapanthi:
                        - Similar to Sthanakavasi, they pray to saints rather than idols.
                        - Their saints wear a muhapatti.
        - Popular Practices in Jainism
            - Sallekhana
                - A religious practice of voluntary fasting to death by gradually reducing food and liquid intake.
                - Jain scholars do not consider it suicide, as it is not driven by passion and does not involve poisons or weapons.
                - It can be undertaken by both Jain ascetics and householders.
                - Legal Status in India:
                    - In 2015, the Rajasthan High Court banned Sallekhana, considering it a form of suicide.
                    - Later in 2015, the Supreme Court of India stayed the decision, lifting the ban.
            - Pratikraman
                - A spiritual practice of repentance, where Jains reflect on their daily sins and resolve not to repeat them.
                - It serves as a way to purify one’s thoughts and actions.
                - Types of Pratikraman:
                    - Devasi Pratikraman – Performed daily.
                    - Rai Pakhi Pratikraman – Performed fortnightly.
                    - Chaumasi Pratikraman – Performed quarterly.
                    - Samvatsari Pratikraman – Performed annually on Samvatsari (Jain forgiveness day).
            - Siddhachakra: A Mystical Jain Yantra 
                - 
                - A sacred diagram used for worship in Jainism.
                - Known as Navapada in the Svetambara tradition.
                - Known as Navadevta in the Digambara tradition.
        - Jain flag
            - 
            - The Jain flag consists of five colors:
                - Red, Yellow, White, Green, and Dark Blue.
            - These five colors represent the Pancha-Parameṣṭhi, the five-fold hierarchy of religious authorities in Jainism.
            - Each of the 24 Tirthankaras is associated with one of these colors.
            

                

                    Color
                    Representation
                

                

                    Red
                    Represents Arihant (one who has attained Kevala Jnana).
                

                

                    Yellow
                    Represents Siddha.
                

                

                    White
                    Represents Acharya.
                

                

                    Green
                    Represents Upadhyay.
                

                

                    Dark Blue
                    Represents Sadhu.
                

            

            - The Swastika in the middle of the flag symbolizes the embodiment of the soul in one of the four forms:
                - Deities
                - Human beings
                - Animals, birds, insects, and plants
                - Hell beings
            - The movement of the soul between these forms depends on Karma. The ultimate goal of the soul is to attain liberation from these four stages and eventually become an Arihant or Siddha.
            - Above the Swastika:
                - The three dots represent the Ratnatraya (Three Jewels) of Jainism:
                - Right Faith
                - Right Knowledge
                - Right Conduct
                - The curve above the three dots symbolizes Siddhashila, the eternal realm at the top of the Universe where Arihants and Siddhas reside.

- Indian Literature
    - Introduction:
        - The term Literature is derived from the Latin word litteratura, meaning “writing formed with letters.”
        - It refers to any form of writing that possesses literary merit.

    - Classifications of Literature:
        - Broadly categorized into:
            - Fiction
            - Non-fiction
        - Further divided into:
            - Poetry
            - Prose
        - Within these categories, different forms include:
            - Novels
            - Short stories
            - Drama
            - Novellas
        - Historical Background:
            - In Greco-Roman times, epics were predominantly oral before being compiled into written form as languages advanced.
        - Advancements in Literature Distribution:
            - 18th century:
                - Print technology revolutionized the accessibility of literature.
                - Enabled a larger audience to read and appreciate literary works.
            - Modern Era:
                - Electronic literature has taken center stage.
                - Digital platforms facilitate wider readership and engagement.
        - Difference between Didactic and Narrative Text
            

                

                    Points of Difference
                    Didactic
                    Narrative
                

                

                    Type of Text
                    Also known as Directive texts, it tries to influence the reasoning, thinking, and conduct of the reader.
                    Provides essential information about the topic to explain or enhance the narration, making it clear and engaging for the reader.
                

                

                    Objective
                    The writer aims to persuade, coax, and compel the reader into thinking a particular way.
                    The writer intends to sustain the interest and inquisitiveness of the reader about the topic.
                

                

                    Commonly Used For
                    Typically used in writings on political or moral issues, such as sermons and religious treatises.
                    The most common type of prose, widely used in storytelling and novels.
                

            

        - In India, four major speech groups are followed, i.e. the Austric, Dravidian, Sino-Tibetan and Indo-European. 

    - Hindu Literature In Ancient India
        - Ancient Indian Literature:
            - Contrary to popular belief, it was not limited to sacred texts like the Vedas and Upanishads.
            - Prakrit literature:
                - Rich in realism and moral values.
                - Lacks religious connotations.
        - Prominent Works of the Ancient Period:
            - Vedas:
                - Sacred texts used in religious rituals and daily life.
            - Other Literary Forms:
                - Includes epics and lyrical compositions.
                - Written in major ancient languages:
                    - Sanskrit
                    - Prakrit
                    - Pali
                    - Ardhamagadhi
        - Vedas
            - Derived from the word ‘Veda’ meaning knowledge.
            - Written in poetic style using symbols and myths.
            - Initially passed down orally before being compiled (1500-1000 BC).
            - Considered sacred divine revelations guiding human life.
            - Promotes Vasudhaiva Kutumbakam (universal family concept).
            - Composed by Vedic seers (Rishis) in Sanskrit poetry.
            - Emphasizes yagna (sacrifice) and has accompanying texts:
                - Brahmanas
                - Upanishads
                - Aranyakas
            - Four Major Vedas:
                - Rig Veda
                    - Oldest Veda with 1028 Sanskrit hymns.
                    - Organized into 10 books (Mandalas), further divided into Suktas (hymns).
                    - Focuses on worldly prosperity and natural beauty.
                    - Dedicated to Lord Indra and other deities:
                        - Agni (fire), Varuna (water), Rudra (wind/storm).
                        - Aditya (sun), Vayu (air), Ashwini twins.
                        - Female deities: Usha (dawn), Prithvi (earth), Vak (speech).
                - Yajur Veda
                    - ‘Yajus’ means sacrifice, focuses on rituals and mantras.
                    - Two major recensions (Samhitas):
                        - Shukla (Vajasaneyi Samhita) – pure/white.
                        - Krishna (Taittiriya Samhita) – dark/black.
                    - Acts as a guidebook for priests conducting rituals.
                - Sama Veda
                    - Named after ‘Saman’ (melody), focuses on chants and music.
                    - Contains 1875 hymns (only 75 original, others from Rig Veda).
                    - Includes 16,000 raga (musical notes) and raginis.
                    - Referred to as the ‘Book of Chants’, highlights Vedic music.
                - Atharva Veda
                    - Also known as Brahma Veda, attributed to Rishis Atharvan & Angiras.
                    - Focuses on human well-being, societal harmony, and healing.
                    - Prescribes treatments for 99 diseases.
                    - Contains black and white magic and everyday life guidance.
                    - Two major recensions (Shakhas): Paippalada & Saunakiya.
            - Vedangas (Limbs of the Veda)
                - Supplementary texts to help understand the Vedas:
                    - Siksha – Education
                    - Nirukta – Etymology (word origins)
                    - Chhanda – Sanskrit metrics
                    - Jyotisha – Astronomy
                    - Vyakarana – Grammar
                - Later developed into Sutras (treatises), e.g., Panini’s Ashtadhyayi (Sanskrit grammar).
        - Brahmanas
            - Part of Sruti (revealed knowledge) literature.
            - Attached to each Veda, serving as commentaries.
            - Mixture of legends, facts, philosophy, and Vedic ritual explanations.
            - Guides sacrificial rituals and explains their symbolic significance.
            - Composed between 900-700 BC.
            - Brahmanas by Veda:
                

                    

                        Veda
                        Brahmana
                    

                    

                        Rig Veda
                        Aitareya Brahmana, Kaushitaki Brahmana
                    

                    

                        Sama Veda
                        Tandya Mahabrahmana, Sadvimsha Brahmana
                    

                    

                        Yajur Veda
                        Taittiriya Brahmana, Shatapatha Brahmana
                    

                    

                        Atharva Veda
                        Gopatha Brahmana
                    

                

        - Aranyakas
            - Philosophical texts attached to the Vedas.
            - Focuses on rituals, birth & death cycles, and the soul’s complexity.
            - Taught by Munis (sages) living in forests.
            - Each Veda has an accompanying Aranyaka.
        - Upanishads
            - Derived from ‘Upa’ (near), ‘Ni’ (down), ‘Shad’ (to sit) – Sitting near the teacher.
            - Passed down orally in the guru-shishya (teacher-student) tradition.
            - More than 200 Upanishads, written in Sanskrit.
            - Vedanta (end of the Vedas) – deals with spiritual & philosophical questions.
            - Discusses moksha (liberation), human existence, and the universe’s origin.
            - Muktika Canon (108 Upanishads) considered the most significant.
            - Distinction between Upanishads & Aranyakas:
                

                    

                        Text
                        Section
                        Focus
                    

                    

                        Upanishad
                        Jnana-kanda
                        Knowledge / Spirituality
                    

                    

                        Aranyaka
                        Karma-kanda
                        Ritualistic Actions / Sacrifice
                    

                

        - Mahakavya (Great Epics): The Mahabharata & The Ramayana
            - Part of collective Hindu memory, expanded over centuries.
            - Ramayana
                - Composed by Valmiki (Adikavi – First Poet).
                - Adikavya (First poetry composition).
                - Estimated dating ~1500 BC.
                - Centers on Rama, the ideal man.
                - Consists of 24,000 verses, divided into seven Khandas (books).
                - Describes Rama’s battle with Ravana (Lanka war).
                - Symbolizes victory of good over evil.
                - Teaches the four Purusharthas (life goals):
                    

                        

                            Concept
                            Meaning
                        

                        

                            Dharma
                            Religion or righteousness
                        

                        

                            Artha
                            Achievements in the worldly sphere (monetary)
                        

                        

                            Kama
                            Fulfilling worldly desires
                        

                        

                            Moksha
                            Liberation from these desires
                        

                    

            - Mahabharata
                - Authored by Ved Vyas, originally called ‘Jaya’ (Victory Story).
                - Expanded from 8,800 verses → 24,000 verses (Bharata) → 100,000 verses.
                - Divided into 10 Parvas (chapters).
                - Centers on the Kaurava-Pandava conflict for Hastinapur’s throne.
                - Bhagavad Gita, a core text within, presents Hindu philosophical dilemmas.
                - Themes:
                    - Violence vs. Non-violence
                    - Action vs. Inaction
                    - Dharma (Duty & Morality)
                    - Nishkama Karma (Selfless Duty) – Lord Krishna’s central teaching.
        - Puranas
            - Meaning ‘that which renews the old’.
            - Mythological texts narrating the creation, history, and destruction of the universe.
            - Stories of kings, sages, deities, especially the Hindu Trinity (Brahma, Vishnu, Shiva).
            - 18 Major Puranas (Mahapuranas): Bhagavata, Brahma, Vayu, Agni, Garuda, Vishnu, etc.
            - Translated into regional languages, making them widely popular.
            - Panchtantra (by Vishnu Sharma) – Animal-based fables teaching worldly wisdom.
            - Hitopadesha (by Narayan Pandit) – Similar collection of didactic stories.
            - Uses Parables & Fables to convey messages:
                

                    

                        Parables vs Fables
                        Description
                    

                    

                        Parable
                        Short stories in prose or verse that illustrate a spiritual, moral, or religious lesson. It usually features a human character.
                    

                    

                        Fable
                        Short stories in prose or verse that illustrate a ‘moral’ through a pithy maxim or clever story. It features animals, inanimate objects, mythical creatures, or plants with human-like qualities.
                    

                

        - Upa-Puranas (Minor Puranas)
            - The Puranas were so popular with the masses that they gave rise to another sub-genre called Upa-Puranas or minor Puranas. 
            - There are about 19 minor Puranas and are based on five major subjects as dictated by the Sanskrit lexicographer from the Gupta period, Amarasimha:
                

                    

                        Term
                        Description
                    

                    

                        Sarga
                        The creation of the universe
                    

                    

                        Pratisarga
                        The periodic cycle of destruction and re-creation
                    

                    

                        Manvantra
                        The periods of Manu’s lifetime
                    

                    

                        Vansa (Chandra and Surya)
                        Genealogies of solar and lunar dynasties of Gods and sages
                    

                    

                        Vanshanucharita
                        Dynastic histories of kings
                    

                


    - Classical Sanskrit Literature
        - Sanskrit literature is divided into Vedic and Classical categories.
        - The two epics, Mahabharata and Ramayana, belong to the Classical category but are discussed separately due to their religious significance.
        - These epics also served as precursors to:
            - Sanskrit Kavya (epic poetry)
            - Nataka (classical drama)
            - Other treatises on medicine, statecraft, grammar, astronomy, and mathematics
        - Most Classical Sanskrit literature followed strict grammatical rules codified in Panini’s Ashtadhyayi.
        - Sanskrit Drama
            - One of the most popular genres of Sanskrit literature.
            - Included romantic tales written in the form of stories or elaborate dramas.
            - Aimed at entertaining the public (Lokaranjana).
            - Performance, acting, gestures, and stage directions were governed by Bharata’s Natyashastra.
            - Major Dramas and Playwrights
                - Kalidasa (Three Major Plays)
                    - Malavikagnimitra – Love story of Malavika (a maiden of the Queen) and Agnimitra (son of Pushyamitra Shunga).
                    - Vikramorvasiya – Love story of Vikram and Urvasi.
                    - Abhigyana Shakuntalam – The Recognition of Shakuntala.
                - Sudraka
                    - Mricchakatika (The Little Clay Cart) – Love affair of young Brahmin Charudatta with a wealthy courtesan.
                - Vishakhadutta
                    - Mudrarakshasa – Political drama narrating the ascent of King Chandragupta Maurya.
                    - Devi Chandraguptam – Another political drama.
                - Bhavabhuti
                    - Uttara Ramacharitam – The later life of Rama (written in 700 AD).
                - Bhasa
                    - Swapnavasavadatta – Vasavadatta in a dream.
                    - Pancharatra – A classic Sanskrit drama.
                    - Urubhanga – Story of Duryodhana before and after his battle with Bhima.
                - Harshavardhana (Three Sanskrit Plays)
                    - Ratnavali 
                        – Love story of Princess Ratnavali and King Udayana.
                        - First known mention of Holi celebrations.
                    - Nagananda 
                        – Story of Prince Jimutavahana sacrificing himself to stop serpent sacrifices.
                        - Features an invocation to Lord Buddha in the Nandi verse.
                    - Priyadarsika 
                        – Union of Udayana and Priyadarsika (daughter of King Dridhavarman).
        - Sanskrit Poetry
            - Also known as Kavya (poetry).
            - Focused on form, style, and figures of speech, unlike dramas that emphasized storytelling.
            - One of the greatest Sanskrit poets: Kalidasa.
            - Works of Kalidasa
                - Kumarasambhava – Birth of Kumar (Kartikeya, son of Shiva and Parvati).
                - Raghuvamsa – Chronicles the Raghu dynasty.
                - Meghaduta – The Cloud Messenger.
                - Ritusamhara – Medley of seasons.
            - Other Major Poets
                - Harisena (Gupta Period)
                    - Wrote poems praising Samudra Gupta.
                    - His works were inscribed on the Allahabad pillar.
                - Jayadeva (12th Century)
                    - Gita Govinda – Explores Krishna’s life, his love for Radha, and devotion.
                - Other Notable Poets
                    - Bharavi (6th Century) – Kiratarjuniya (Kirat, the hunter, and Arjuna).
                    - Magha (7th Century) – Sishupalavadha (The killing of Shishupal).
        - Other Major Sanskrit Texts
            - Many Sanskrit texts covered sciences and governance.
            - Between 500-200 BC, major books on law were compiled:
                - Dharamsutras – Guidelines on property, crime, and justice.
                - Dharamshastras – Basis of Hindu kingdom laws.
                - Manusmriti (200 BC - 200 AD) – Defines social roles and conduct.
            - Kautilya’s Arthashastra (Mauryan Period)
                - Treatise on statecraft, economy, and military strategy.
                - Written by Kautilya (Vishnugupta), believed to be Chanakya.
                - Sanskrit flourished in the Gupta Period, becoming the language of the elite.
            - Scientific Texts in Sanskrit
                

                    

                        Name
                        Work
                        Subject
                    

                    

                        Pingala
                        Chandasastra
                        Book on Mathematics
                    

                    

                        Charak
                        Charak Samhita
                        Book on Medicine
                    

                    

                        Sushruta
                        Sushruta Samhita
                        Book on Surgery

                        Pancha-Siddhantika, Brihat Samhita

        - Sanskrit Literature in the Medieval Period
            - Declined in popularity but some excellent works emerged.
            - Notable works from medieval Kashmir:
            - Kalhana’s Rajatarangini – A detailed history of Kashmir’s kings.
            - Somadeva’s Katha-sarit-sagar – A poetic collection of stories.

    - Literature in Pali and Prakrit
        - During the post-Vedic period, literature was composed not only in Sanskrit but also in Prakrit and Pali.
        - Prakrit refers to languages that deviated from classical Sanskrit.
        - These languages gained prominence through the religious literature of Buddhists and Jains.
        - Prakrit Poetry
            - Some Prakrit poetry contains elements of erotica.
            - Gatha Saptasati (700 Verses) – A notable Prakrit poetic work by Hala.
            - Hala contributed only 44 verses, while the rest were written by female poets.
            - Prominent female poets: Pahai, Roha, Sasippaha, Mahavi, and Reva.
            - Considered the opposite extreme of the Kamasutra.

    - Buddhist Literature
        - Canonical Literature – The Tripitaka
            - The Tripitaka (“Three Baskets of Knowledge”) is the sacred text of Buddhism, written in Pali. It consists of:
                - Vinaya Pitaka – Rules and regulations for Buddhist monks.
                - Sutta Pitaka – Dialogues and speeches of the Buddha on morality and righteous dharma.
                - Abhidhamma Pitaka – Philosophical and metaphysical discussions, covering ethics, knowledge, and psychology.
        - Non-Canonical Literature
            - Jatakas
                - Stories about the previous births of Buddha.
                - Also include Bodhisattva tales (stories of the would-be Buddha).
                - Written in both Sanskrit and Pali.
                - Buddha was believed to have passed through 550 births before being born as Gautam.
                - Reflect popular tales, mythology, and socio-political conditions of North India.
        - Other Buddhist literary texts:
            • Dipavamsa: 
                - It was probably composed in 3rd-4th centuries AD in Anuradhapura (Sri Lanka). 
                - It literally means “Chronicle of the Island”. It mentions about the visit of Buddha to Sri Lanka and the arrival of tooth relic of Buddha.
            • Milinda Panho: 
                - It contains a dialogue between King Meander (or Milinda) and Buddhist monk Nagasena. It means “Questions of Milinda”. 
                - These are one of the highest philosophical enquiries.
            • Mahavamsa: 
                - It is an epic poem similar to Dipavamsa and is written in Pali language. 
                - It dates back to 5th century AD and has account of Buddha’s visit to Ceylon (Srilanka), Chronicles of Kings of Ceylon, etc.
            • Mahavastu: 
                - It contains Jakata and Avadana tales. It is written in mixed Sanskrit, Pali and Prakrit. 
                - It is said to be compiled between 2nd century BC and 4th century AD.
            • Lalitavistara Sutra: 
                - Meaning “The play in full”, is an important Mahayana text. 
                - It contains various stories associated with the life of Buddha till his first sermon at Sarnath.
            • Udana: 
                - It is one of the oldest Theravada (Old School) Buddhist text. It contains the famous story of “Blind Men and the Elephant”.
            • Bodhi Vamsa: 
                - It was prose-poem, written in 12th century in Sri Lanka. 
                - It was translated from a Sinhalese version. It was written by Upatissa in Sanskritised Pali.
            • Mahavibhasa Shastra: 
                - It is said to be written around 150 AD. It contains discussions about other non-Buddhist philosophies also. 
                - It is essentially a Mahayan text.
            • Visuddhimagga: 
                - It was written by Buddhaghosa in 5th century in Sri Lanka. It is a text of Theravada doctrine. 
                - It contains discussions on various teachings of Buddha.

    - Jain Literature
        - Languages Used
            - Jain monks wrote in multiple languages based on era, region, and patronage.
            - Languages include:
                - Prakrit and Ardha-Magadhi (primary religious texts).
                - Tamil (Sangam Age, South India).
                - Sanskrit, Shauraseni, Gujarati, and Marathi.
        - Categories of Jain Literature
            - Canonical (Religious) Texts – Jain Agamas.
            - Non-Canonical Literary Works – Various texts on philosophy, logic, ethics, and literature.
        - Jain Agamas
            - Sacred texts containing teachings of the Jain Tirthankaras.
            - Originally compiled by the Ganadharas (chief disciples of Mahavira).
            - Recompiled in 5th century AD by the Svetambara sect at Vallabhi (Gujarat).
            - Digambara sect rejects these Agamas, believing that the original teachings were lost.
            - Written in Ardha-Magadhi Prakrit.
            - Promote non-violence, asceticism, vegetarianism, and compassion.
            - Structure of Agamas (46 Texts)
                - 12 Angas
                - 12 Upangas
                - 10 Prakirnakasutras
                - 4 Mulasutras
                - 6 Chedasutras
                - 2 Culikasutras
        - Digambara Sacred Texts
        - Shatkhandagama (Karmaprabhrita) – Discussion on Karma.
        - Kashayaprabhrita – Another sacred Digambara text.
        - Important Jain Works & Authors
            - Early Jain Scholars
                - Bhadrabahu (3rd century BC)
                    - Teacher of Chandragupta Maurya.
                    - Wrote Uvasaggaharam Stotra and Kalpa Sutra (Biographies of Jain Tirthankaras).
                    - Pioneer of the Digambara sect.
                - Acharya Kundkund
                    - Wrote Samayasara and Niyamasara on Jain philosophy.
                - Samanta Bhadra (2nd century AD)
                    - Wrote Ratna Karanda Sravakachara (Life of a Jain householder).
                    - Wrote Aptamimansa, an important Jain text.
            - Tamil Jain Literature
                - Ilango Adigal (2nd century AD)
                    - Wrote Silappadikaram, one of the greatest Tamil epics.
                    - A moralistic discourse revolving around Kannagi’s revenge on the Pandyan kingdom.
                - Tirutakkatevar
                    - Wrote Civaka Cintamani, another Tamil Jain epic.
                - Nalatiyar
                    - An ancient Tamil text composed by Jain monks.
            - Jain Texts in Sanskrit
                - Umaswati (2nd-5th century AD)
                    - Wrote Tattvartha Sutra, a key Jain text on logic, epistemology, ethics, and astronomy.
                    - Accepted by both Digambara and Svetambara sects.
                - Jinasena (8th-9th centuries AD)
                    - Revered Digambara monk.
                    - Wrote Mahapurana and Adipurana.
                    - Disciple of famous Jain monk Virasena.
                - Haribhadra Suri (6th century AD)
                    - A Svetambara Jain scholar.
                    - Wrote extensively in Sanskrit.
                - Hemachandra Suri (12th century AD)
                    - Famous Jain scholar, known for Sanskrit and Prakrit grammars, poetry, and prosody.
                    - Awarded the title Kalikalasarvajna (“The all-knowing of the Kaliyuga”).
                - Siddharasi (906 AD)
                    - Wrote Upamitibhava Prapancha Katha, a Jain text in Sanskrit.
                - Shantinatha Charitra
                    - A Sanskrit text on the 16th Tirthankara, Shantinatha.
                    - Declared a global treasure by UNESCO.
        - Centers of Jain Learning
            - Vallabhi and Kalinga Universities were major centers of Jain learning.
            - Jain monks wrote extensively in Kannada between the 9th-12th centuries.
            - Decline of Jain literature after the 12th-13th century due to:
                - Hindu revivalism.
                - Spread of Lingayatism in Karnataka.

    - Zoroastrian Literature
        - Zoroastrianism is a Persian religion founded by Zoroaster (Zarathushtra). 
        - It influenced Persian culture, art, and other religions. It introduced concepts like angels, judgment day, a demonic figure, and the battle between good and evil.
        - Avesta (Sacred Text)
            - The Avesta is the most important Zoroastrian text, compiled over time.
            - Written in Avestan language (now extinct, similar to Sanskrit).
            - Final compilation likely in the 4th century AD during Sassanian rule.
            - Key Sections in Avesta
                - Yasna:
                - A collection of 72 chapters.
                - Includes Gathas (5 chapters, 17 hymns), believed to be written by Zoroaster himself.
                - Most important ceremony of the faith.
        - Other Important Zoroastrian Texts
            - Denkard (10th century AD)
                - Collection of books covering various aspects of the faith.
                - Regarded as the Encyclopedia of Zoroastrianism (not divine scripture).
            - Bundahishn (8th-9th centuries AD)
                - Meaning “Primal Creations”.
                - Describes the creation theory, astronomy, and battles between Ahura Mazda & Angra Mainyu.

    - Sikh Literature
        - Sikhism was founded in the 15th century by Guru Nanak. 
            - The teachings are recorded in Guru Granth Sahib, composed of Gurbani (hymns of Sikh Gurus).
        - Important Sikh Texts
            - Adi Granth (1604)
                - Compiled by Bhai Gurdas under Guru Arjan Dev (5th Guru).
                - Predecessor to Guru Granth Sahib.
                - Includes teachings of Sikh Gurus & 15 Bhagats from Bhakti & Sufi traditions.
            - Guru Granth Sahib (1678)
                - Expanded version of Adi Granth, compiled under Guru Gobind Singh (10th Guru).
                - Considered the final (11th) spiritual authority of Sikhs.
                - Written in Gurmukhi script using Sant Bhasha (a mix of Punjabi, Apabhramsa, Hindi, Braj Bhasha, Sanskrit, Khadiboli, and Persian).
                - Includes teachings of Bhakti saints (Bhagats) like Ramananda, Namdev, Ravidas, and two Muslim Bhagats—Kabir & Baba Farid.
            - Dasam Granth
                - Believed to contain hymns written by Guru Gobind Singh, though its authenticity is debated.
                - Includes fables and Puranic stories.
                - Some hymns are part of daily prayers (Nitnem).
            - Janamsakhis
                - Mythological and exaggerated biographies of Guru Nanak.
                - Notable versions:
                    - Bhai Bala Janamsakhi (most popular).
                    - Miharban Janam Sakhi.
                    - Adi Janam Sakhi.
        - Ardas (Prayer)
            - Set of prayers performed in Gurudwaras.
            - Offered during opening/closing of Guru Granth Sahib.
            - Composed of three parts, the first detailing virtues of the 10 Sikh Gurus.

    - Dravidian Literature
        - Dravidian literature includes works from the four major Dravidian languages: Tamil, Kannada, Telugu, and Malayalam. 
        - Among them, Tamil is considered the oldest and shares similarities with Sanskrit, especially in grammar and vocabulary.
        - Tamil Literature
            - Tamil Sangam Literature
                - Sangam means fraternity, and this literature was popular among the masses.
                - It is a collection of 2381 poems attributed to 473 poets, with 102 poets remaining anonymous.
                - The poets belonged to different social classes, including men and women.
                - Composed between 300 BC and 300 AD, this period is called the Sangam period.
                - Major Schools of Sangam Literature
                    - Aham (Agam) – The inner field, focusing on love, relationships, and emotions.
                    - Puram – The outer field, covering social life, ethics, valor, and customs.
                - Sangam Assemblies
                    - The Pandya Kingdom organized three Sangams over 600-700 years, where poets and scholars gathered. 
                    - The first two Sangams are largely mythical, with historical records mostly from the third Sangam.
                        

                            

                                Sangam
                                Kingdom
                                Place Organised
                                Chairman
                                Books
                            

                            

                                First or Head Sangam
                                Pandya
                                Madurai
                                Agastya or Shiva
                                No book has survived. Used Agattiyam as grammar.
                            

                            

                                Second or Middle Sangam
                                Pandya
                                Kapatapuram
                                Initially Agastya, then his disciple Tolkappiyar
                                
                                    - Around 2,000 poems were collected and compiled into Tolkappiyam. 

                                    - Tolkappiyam is a textbook on Tamil grammar, covering inflection and syntax of words and sentences, 
                                    as well as classification of habitats, animals, plants, and human beings.
                                		
                            

                            

                                Third Sangam
                                Pandya
                                Madurai
                                Nakkirar
                                Most of the existing corpus of Sangam literature was written.
                            

                        

                - Key Works & Poets
                    - Ettuthogai (Eight Anthologies) – A collection of 30,000 lines of poetry.
                    - Tirukkural – Written by Saint Thiruvalluvar, it has been translated into several languages and covers ethics, governance, and love.
                    - Avvaiyar – A female saint who contributed significantly to Tamil literature.
                - Post-Sangam Tamil Literature
                    - Tolkappiyam – Detailed Tamil grammar and poetry.
                    - Silappadikaram (Tale of an Anklet) – Written by Ilango-Adigal (6th century AD).
                    - Manimekalai (The Story of Manimekalai) – Written by Satthanar (6th century AD), discussing Tamil society, economy, and politics.
                - Medieval Bhakti Literature (7th-12th Century)
                    - Alvars – 12 Vaishnava saint poets, devoted to Vishnu/Krishna. Andal was the only female Alvar.
                    - Nayanars – Devotees who sang praises of Shaivism.
                    - Periya Puranam & Kambaramayanam – Major works of secular Tamil literature.
        - Malayalam Literature
            - Predominantly spoken in Kerala and surrounding regions.
            - Ramacharitam (12th century) by Cheeraman – An epic poem.
            - Kokasandisan & Bhasa Kautilya (a commentary on Arthashastra).
            - Ezhuthachan, a major Bhakti poet, is known as the Father of Malayalam Literature.
            - Champu Style
                - A literary style blending poetry and prose, used in Telugu, Odia, Kannada, and Sanskrit literature.
        - Telugu Literature
            - Nannayya (11th century AD) – The first known Telugu poet.
            - Reached its peak during the Vijayanagara period, known as the Golden Age of Telugu Literature.
            - Uttaraharivamsam – Composed by Nachana Somanatha, a court poet of King Bukka I.
            - Krishnadevaraya (1509-1529) – A scholar-king who composed Amuktamalyada.
                - Krishnadevaraya’s Contributions
                    - Patronized Telugu, Kannada, Tamil, and Sanskrit literature.
                    - Patronized the Ashtadiggajas, with Allasani Peddana being the most important.
                    - Promoted Kannada poets like Mallanarya, Chatu Vittalanatha, and Thimmana.
                    - Rajaguru was Vyasatirtha, a Kannada saint.
                    - Authored Krishna Deva Rayana Dinachari (Kannada).
                    - Wrote Sanskrit works: Madalasa Charita, Satyavadu Parinaya, Rasamanjari, and Jambavati Kalyana.
                    - Patronized Tamil poet Haridasa.
            - Ashtadiggajas – The Eight Poets of Krishnadevaraya’s Court
                

                    

                        Poet
                        Name of the Work

        - Kannada Literature
            - Early Kannada Literature (Jain Influence)
                - Dharmanathapurana – Written by Madhava (Life of the 15th Tirthankara).
                - Dharma Parikshe – Written by Uritta Vilasa, covering Jain teachings.
