                - Kavirajamarga (9th century) – First recorded Kannada text by Amoghavarsha I, a Rashtrakuta king.
            - The Three Gems of Kannada Literature
                • Pampa (9th-10th century AD),                
                - Pampa (Father of Kannada Literature) was attached to the court of Chalukya Arikesari.
                • Ponna (9th-10th century AD) and
                • Ranna (10th-11th century AD).
                    - Ponna & Ranna were associated with Rashtrakuta King Krishna III.
            - Medieval & Bhakti Kannada Literature
                - Shabdamanidarpana – A major Kannada grammar book by Keshiraja.
                - Torave Ramayana – First Kannada retelling of Ramayana by Narahari.
                - Jaimini Bharata – Written by Lakshmisha.
                - Sarvajna (16th century) – Famous for Tripadi (Three-lined poems).
                - Honnamma – First notable Kannada female poet, author of Hadibadeya Dharma (Duty of a Devout Wife).
    - Medieval Literature
        - Persian
            - Brought by Turks & Mongols in the 12th century.
            - Court language of Delhi Sultanate & Mughals.
            - Major Writers:
                - Amir Khusrau – Nuh Sipihr, Duval Rani-Khizr Khan.
                - Ziauddin Barani – Tarikh-e-Firoz Shahi.
                - Minhaj-i-Siraj – Historian.
                - Ibn Battuta – Travel accounts.
            - Mughal Period Works:
                - Babur – Baburnama (Turkish).
                - Jahangir – Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri (autobiography).
                - Gulbadan Begum – Humayun-namah.
                - Abul Fazl – Ain-e Akbari, Akbarnama.
                - Badauni – Translated Mahabharata (Razmnama).
                - Malik Muhammad Jayasi – Padmavat (1540).
                - Faizi – Persian poet, one of Akbar’s Navaratnas.
            - Later Works:
                - Inayat Khan – Shahjahannama.
                - Jafar Zatalli – Satirical poetry.
                - Abdul Hamid Lahori – Padshahnama (Shah Jahan’s reign).
                - Zeb-un-Nissa (Aurangzeb’s daughter) – Diwan-i-Makhfi.
        - Urdu
            - Developed from Persian & Hindi, used in Bahmani states.
            - Major Writers:
                - Amir Khusrau – Early Urdu works.
                - Mirza Ghalib – Diwan (Urdu poetry).
                - Sauda, Dard, Mir Taqi Mir – Urdu poets.
                - Iqbal – Bang-e-Dara, wrote Saare Jahan Se Achcha.
                - Bahadur Shah Zafar – Last Mughal emperor, Urdu poet.
                - Sir Syed Ahmad Khan – Modern Urdu literature.
        - Hindi & Its Dialects
            - Evolved (7th–14th century) from Apabhramsa → Prakrit.
            - Grew with the Bhakti movement, replacing Sanskrit.
            - Major Works & Poets:
                - Prithviraj Raso – First Hindi epic (Prithviraj Chauhan’s life).
                - Kabir – Dohas (couplets).
                - Tulsidas – Ramcharitmanas.
                - Surdas – Sur Sagar (Lord Krishna’s life).
                - Rahim, Bhushan, Raskhan – Devotional poets.
                - Mirabai – Bhakti poetry for Lord Krishna.
                - Bihari – Satsai (Hindi poetry).
    - Modern Literature
        - Hindi Literature
            - Marked by British influence, focus shifted to prose, nationalism, and revival of Sanskrit classics.
            - Key Writers:
                - Bharatendu Harishchandra – Andher Nagari (1881), Bharat Durdasha (Father of Modern Hindi Literature).
                - Mahavir Prasad Dwivedi – Led an era of Hindi writing.
                - Munshi Premchand – Godan, Karmabhoomi.
                - Suryakant Tripathi ‘Nirala’, Maithili Sharan Gupt, Sumitranandan Pant, Ramdhari Singh ‘Dinkar’.
                - Harivansh Rai Bachchan – Madhushala.
                - Mahadevi Verma – Leading 20th-century female writer.
                - Jaishankar Prasad – Kamayani (1936), masterpiece of Chhayavaad.
            - Periods of Modern Hindi Literature:
                - Bhartendu Yug (1868-1893)
                - Dwivedi Yug (1893-1918)
                - Chhayavad Yug (1918-1937)
                - Contemporary Period (1937-today)
            - Swami Dayanand promoted Hindi as a national language (Satyartha Prakash, 1875).
        - Bengali, Odia, and Assamese Literature
            - Bengali Literature:
                - Spread through Baptist Mission Press (Serampore, 1800).
                - Raja Ram Mohan Roy, Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar, Akshay Kumar Dutta – Early writers.
                - Bankimchandra Chatterjee – Anandamath (Vande Mataram originated).
                - Rabindranath Tagore – Geetanjali (Nobel Prize, 1913).
                - Sharat Chandra Chatterjee, Qazi Nazrul Islam, R.C. Dutt.
            - Assamese Literature:
                - Medieval period – Buranjis (court chronicles), devotional poetry by Sankardev.
                - Modern period – Padmanath Gohain Baruah, Lakshminath Bezbaroa.
            - Odia Literature:
                - Sarala Das (15th century), Upendra Bhanja (18th century).
                - Modern era – Radhanath Ray, Fakir Mohan Senapati.
        - Gujarati, Rajasthani, and Sindhi Literature
            - Gujarati Literature:
                - Bhakti movement influence – Narsinh Mehta (15th century).
                - Narmad (poetry, 19th century), Govardhan Ram (Saraswati Chandra).
                - Dr. K.M. Munshi – Prithivivallabh (1928).
            - Rajasthani Literature:
                - Oral traditions – Dingal & Pingal forms, Dhola Maru tale.
                - Mirabai (Braj poetry), victory songs (virkavya).
            - Sindhi Literature:
                - Influences from Rajasthan, Gujarat, Islam, and Sufism.
                - Notable writers – Dewan Kauramal, Mirza Kalich Beg (18th-19th century).
        - Kashmiri Literature
            - Early Texts: Kalhana’s Rajatarangani (12th century, Sanskrit).
            - Medieval Era: Lal Ded (Shaivite mystic, first female poetess).
            - Sufi Influence: Sufi Ghulam Mohammad, Zinda Kaul, Mahjoor.
            - Other Notable Writers: Noor Din (Nund Rishi) – Combined Hindi & Islamic elements.
            - Modern Decline: Dogri overshadowed Kashmiri under Dogra rule (1846).
        - Punjabi Literature
            - Religious Works:
                - Adi Granth (Sikh scripture, in Gurmukhi & Persian).
                - Guru Gobind Singh – Savaiye (poetry).
            - Love Stories & Epics:
                - Sohni-Mahiwal, Sassi-Punnu, Heer-Ranjha (Waris Shah).
            - Sufi Influence: Baba Farid, Bulley Shah (Kafi songs).
            - Modern Era:
                - Nationalist works – Bhagat Singh (Rang de Basanti Chola).
                - Bhai Vir Singh – Rana Surat Singh.
                - Contemporary Writers: Dr. Mohan Singh, Puran Singh.
        - Marathi Literature
            - Early Works:
                - Saint Jnaneshwar (13th century) – Bhagavad Gita commentary.
                - Bhakti poets – Namdev, Sena, Gora.
                - Janabai (13th-14th century, first female poet).
            - Medieval Saints:
                - Eknath (16th century) – Commentaries on Bhagavata Purana, Ramayana.
                - Tukaram (17th century), Ramdas (17th century).
            - Modern Nationalist Influence:
                - Bal Gangadhar Tilak – Kesari (Marathi newspaper).
                - Hari Narayan Apte, V.S. Chiplunkar, Kesavasut.
                - Contemporary Writers: M.G. Ranade, G.T. Madkholkar, K.T. Telang.


- Schools of Philosophy
    - Introduction
        - Indian philosophy has a long tradition. Philosophers explored life, death, and beyond. Many ideas overlapped with religion. 
        - Differences became clear as the state and varna-based order grew.

    - Major Goals of Life
        

            

                Goals for Life
                Meaning
                Treatise on the Goal
            

            

                Artha
                Economic means or wealth
                Matters related to the economy were discussed in Arthashastra.
            

            

                Dharma
                Regulation of social orders
                Matters related to the State were discussed in the Dharamsastra.
            

            

                Kama
                Physical pleasures or love
                The Kamasastra/Kamasutra were written to elaborate on sexual pleasure.
            

            

                Moksha
                Salvation
                There are several texts on Darshana or philosophy that deal with salvation.
            

        

        - All agreed on these goals, but moksha was the ultimate aim. Over time, views on achieving it differed. By the Christian Era, two major schools emerged.
        - These schools were:
            - Orthodox School
                - This school upheld the Vedas as the ultimate scriptures and did not question their authority. It had six sub-schools, known as Shada Darshana:
                    - Samkhya School
                        - Oldest school, founded by Kapil Muni (Samkhya Sutra).
                            - 
                        - “Samkhya” means “count.”
                        - Two phases of development:
                        - This school went through two phases of development that were:
                            

                                

                                    Original Samkhya View
                                    New Samkhya View
                                

                                

                                    This view is considered to be the early Samkhya philosophy and dates around 1st century AD.
                                    This view emerged when newer elements merged with the older Samkhya view during the 4th century AD.
                                

                                

                                    They believed that the presence of any divine agency was not necessary for the creation of the Universe.
                                    They argued that along with the element of nature, Purusha or spirit was necessary for the creation of the Universe.
                                

                                

                                    They propounded a rational and scientific view of the creation of the Universe.
                                    They propounded a spiritual view of the creation of the Universe.
                                

                                

                                    They also argued that the world owed its existence to nature or Prakriti.
                                    They argued that the coming together of nature and spiritual elements created the world.
                                

                                

                                    This view is considered to be a materialistic school of philosophy.
                                    This view is considered to be belonging to a more spiritual school of philosophy.
                                

                            

                        - Salvation is attained through knowledge.
                        - Believed in dualism (dvaitavada): Soul & matter are separate.
                        - Three sources of knowledge: Perception (Pratyaksha), Inference (Anumana), Hearing (Shabda).
                        - Prakriti = Thought, movement, transformation (female-like).
                        - Purusha = Consciousness (male-like).
                    - Yoga School
                        - 
                        - Means union of mind & body.
                        - Founded by Patanjali (Yogasutra, 2nd century BC).
                        - Salvation through meditation & yogic techniques (release of Purusha from Prakriti).
                        - Key practices:
                            

                                

                                    Means of Achieving Freedom
                                    Meanings/Ways of Achieving It
                                

                                

                                    Yama
                                    Practicing self-control
                                

                                

                                    Niyama
                                    Observation of the rules governing one’s life
                                

                                

                                    Pratyahara
                                    Choosing an object
                                

                                

                                    Dharna
                                    Fixing the mind (over the chosen object)
                                

                                

                                    Dhyana
                                    Concentrating on the (above-mentioned) chosen object
                                

                                

                                    Samadhi
                                    It is the merging of the mind and the object and that leads to the final dissolution of the self
                                

                            

                        - Helps control mind, body, and senses.
                        - God is a guide & mentor to attain salvation.
                    - Nyaya School
                        - Founded by Gautama (Nyaya Sutra).
                        - Believed in logical reasoning to attain salvation.
                        - Life, death & salvation are mysteries solved through logic & analysis.
                        - Truth is verified through Inference, Hearing, and Analogy.
                        - God creates, sustains, and destroys the Universe.
                    - Vaisheshika School
                        - Founded by Kanada.
                            - 
                        - Focused on realism & physical Universe.
                        - Universe created from five elements: Fire, Air, Water, Earth, Ether (Dravya).
                        - Developed atomic theory: Universe is made of atoms & molecules.
                        - God guides the Universe but is also bound by laws of Karma.
                        - Salvation is linked to creation & destruction of the Universe (cyclic process).
                    - Mimamsa School
                        - 
                        - “Mimamsa” means reasoning & interpretation.
                        - Focused on Vedic rituals to attain salvation.
                        - Key text: Sutras of Jaimini (3rd century BC).
                        - Proponents: Sabar Swami, Kumarila Bhatta.
                        - Performing Vedic rituals leads to heaven & salvation.
                        - Brahmins used this philosophy to maintain social hierarchy.
                    - Vedanta School
                        - “Vedanta” = End of the Vedas.
                        - Based on Upanishads, Brahmasutra (Badrayana).
                        - Core belief: Brahm is real; everything else is Maya (illusion).
                        - Atma (soul) = Brahm (universal reality).
                        - Salvation through self-knowledge.
                        - Evolved in 9th century AD under Shankaracharya (commentaries on Upanishads, Bhagavad Gita).
                        - Later developed into Advaita Vedanta.
                        - Ramanujan (12th century AD) modified it, creating differences:
                            

                                

                                    Shankaracharya’s View
                                    Ramanujan’s View
                                

                                

                                    He considers Brahm to be without any attributes.
                                    He considers Brahm to possess certain attributes.
                                

                                

                                    He considers Knowledge or Jnana/Gyan to be the main means of attaining salvation.
                                    He considers loving faith and practicing devotion as the path to attain salvation.
                                

                            

                        - Vedanta also supports Karma theory & rebirth (Punarjanma).
                        - One suffers in this life due to past karma.
                        - Salvation comes through realization of Brahm.
            - Heterodox School
                - This school rejects the authority of the Vedas and questions the existence of God. It is divided into three sub-schools:
                    - Buddhist Philosophy (explained in Chapter 14)
                    - Jain Philosophy (explained in Chapter 14)
                    - Charvaka (Lokayata) Philosophy
                        - 
                        - Founded by Brihaspati.
                        - One of the earliest philosophical schools, mentioned in the Vedas and Brihadaranyaka Upanishad.
                        - Materialistic philosophy focused on the physical world.
                        - Called Lokayata, meaning “derived from the common people.”
                - Core Beliefs:
                    - No supernatural or divine being controls human actions.
                    - No need for salvation, no Brahm or God.
                    - Only what can be touched and experienced by the senses is real.
                - Main Teachings:
                    - Against Gods & priests: Believed priests fabricate rituals to receive gifts (dakshina).
                    - Man-centered philosophy: Life’s goal is pleasure; enjoy all earthly goods & sensual pleasures.
                    - Universe consists of four elements (fire, earth, water, air); rejected ether as it cannot be perceived.
                    - No afterlife: Death is the end of existence, so one should “eat, drink, and be merry.”


- Indian Cinema
    - Introduction
        - India has one of the largest film industries in Asia and produces the highest number of films globally. 
        - Films are made in multiple languages, including Hindi, Telugu, Tamil, and Bhojpuri.
            - 2014 survey: India produced nearly 3000 celluloid films, including 1000 short films and 1969 feature films.
            - Foreign Investment:
                - Full FDI allowed in the film sector.
                - International media houses like 20th Century Fox and Warner Bros. invested in Indian cinema.
                - Foreign directors have begun exploring Indian socio-cultural issues in their films.
    - Importance of Indian Cinema
        - Identity & Representation:
            - Post-independence films have shaped national and individual identity.
        - Socioeconomic & Political Reflection:
            - Films portray and analyze India’s changing social, economic, and political landscape.
        - Psychological Impact:
            - Studies show films deeply influence people’s psyche.
            - Audiences connect with actors and empathize with their characters.
        - Beyond Entertainment:
            - Cinema is not just three hours of entertainment; it creates lasting experiences.
        - Types of Cinema:
            - Mainstream Cinema: Focuses on entertainment.
            - Parallel Cinema: Showcases real-life issues.
        - Widespread Reach:
            - Cinema has expanded beyond urban metros to rural areas and smaller cities.
    - History of Indian Cinema
        - Early Beginnings (1896-1916)
            - Lumiere Brothers introduced motion pictures to India in 1896, showcasing six silent short films in Bombay.
            - The first film shot in India, Coconut Fair and Our Indian Empire, was recorded in 1897 by an unknown photographer.
            - Colorello and Cornaglia started exhibitions in tents at Azad Maidan, Bombay (1898).
            - Indian filmmakers entered the scene:
                - Harishchandra Bhatavdekar (Save Dada) made India’s first two short films (1899).
                - F.B. Thanawalla produced Taboot Procession (1900).
                - Hiralal Sen made Indian Life and Scenes (1903).
            - Growth of the industry led to permanent cinema houses:
                - Major Warwick established India’s first cinema house in Madras (1900).
                - Jamshedjee Madan set up Elphinstone Picture House in Calcutta (1907).
            - Universal Studios set up India’s first Hollywood-based agency in 1916.
        - Era of Silent Films (1910-1920s)
            - Silent films were not truly silent; screenings included live music (sarangi, tabla, harmonium, violin).
            - Pundalik (1912): First Indo-British silent film by N.G. Chitre & R.G. Torney.
            - Raja Harishchandra (1913):
                - First indigenous Indian silent film by Dadasaheb Phalke.
                - Phalke, known as the Father of Indian Cinema, also made Lanka Dahan (1917), India’s first box office hit.
            - Film Companies Established (1918):
                - Kohinoor Film Company
                - Dadasaheb Phalke’s Hindustan Cinema Films Company
            - Censorship and Controversies:
                - Bhakta Vidur (1921) was banned in Madras for political reasons.
            - Women in Cinema:
                - Fatma Begum became India’s first woman director with Bulbul-e-Paristan (1926).
        - The Talkies and Colour Films (1930s)
            - Alam Ara (1931):
                - 
                - First Indian talkie directed by Ardeshir Irani.
                - First song in Indian cinema: De de khuda ke naam par by W.M. Khan.
            - Rise of Studio System (1930s):
                - Major studios: Bombay Talkies, New Theatres, Prabhat.
                - First film using the studio system: Devdas (1935) by P.C. Barua.
            - Early Colour Films:
                - Sairandhri (1933) – First colour film (processed in Germany).
                - Kisan Kanya (1937) – First indigenously processed colour film by Ardeshir Irani.
            - Notable Films of the 1930s:
                - Hunterwali (1935) – First Indian stunt film featuring Fearless Nadia.
                - Premsagar (1939) – First South Indian film and first songless film.
        - War Ravaged 1940s
            - Indian politics influenced films, with independence fervor reflected in:
                - Dharti ke Lal
                - Do Aankhen Baarah Haath
            - Historical and tragic love stories gained popularity:
                - Chandralekha, Laila Majnu, Sikander, Chitralekha.
        - Coming of Age - 1950s
            - Central Board of Film Certification (CBFC) established to regulate film content.
            - Rise of film stars: Dilip Kumar, Dev Anand, Raj Kapoor.
            - First Technicolor film (1953): Jhansi Ki Rani by Sohrab Modi.
            - Global Recognition:
                - First International Film Festival of India (IFFI) – Bombay (1952).
                - Do Bigha Zameen (Bimal Roy) – First Indian film to win at Cannes.
                - Pather Panchali (Satyajit Ray) – Won awards at Cannes.
                - Mother India (1957) – First Indian film nominated for an Oscar.
            - National Film Awards introduced:
                - First winner: Shyamchi Aai.
                - President’s Gold Medal (1954): Mirza Ghalib by Sohrab Modi.
        - The Golden Era - 1960s
            - Music became a crucial element in films:
                - Jis Desh Mein Ganga Behti Hai, Guide, Waqt.
            - Impact of Wars (1962, 1965):
                - Nationalistic films: Haqeeqat, Aradhana, Sangam.
            - Film and Television Institute of India (FTII) established in Pune (1960).
                - 
            - Dadasaheb Phalke Award for Lifetime Achievement introduced in 1969.
        - The ‘Angry Young Man’ Era - 1970s-1980s
            - Films reflected urban struggles, featuring rags-to-riches stories.
            - Amitabh Bachchan became the symbol of this era with hits like Zanjeer, Agneepath, Amar Akbar Anthony.
            - Sholay (1975):
                - First Indian film on 70mm.
                - Longest-running film till the 1990s.
        - Romantic Cinema - 1980-2000
            - Rise of social issues, romance, and family dramas:
                - Tezaab, Ram Lakhan, Hum, Phool aur Kaante.
            - Anti-hero trend started with:
                - Baazigar, Darr (launched Shah Rukh Khan’s stardom).
            - Technological Advances (1990s):
                - Liberalization (LPG policy) allowed advanced filmmaking.
                - First 3D movie: My Dear Kuttichatan (Malayalam).
                - Dolby Sound System introduced in India.
        - Parallel Cinema
            - Emerged in the late 1940s, focusing on realism and artistic excellence.
            - Key Factors for Growth:
                - Post-WWII neorealism influenced Indian films.
                - Film study institutions like National Film Archive of India (1964).
                - Global exposure through film festivals.
            - Pioneers:
                - Satyajit Ray: The Apu Trilogy – Pather Panchali, Apur Sansar, Aparajito.
                - Ritwik Ghatak: Nagarik, Ajantrik, Meghe Dhaka Tara.
                - 
            - Women Filmmakers (1980s):
                - Sai Paranjpye (Chashme Buddoor, Sparsh).
                - Kalpana Lajmi (Ek Pal).
                - Aparna Sen (36 Chowringhee Lane).
                - Mira Nair (Salaam Bombay!) – won at Cannes (1989).
        - Changing Role of Women in Indian Cinema
            - Silent Era: Films depicted restrictions on women.
            - 1920s-40s: Directors like V. Shantaram, Dhiren Ganguli tackled women’s emancipation (child marriage, sati abolition).
            - 1950s-70s: Films glorified motherhood, fidelity, sacrifice.
            - Parallel Cinema: Showed strong female leads, challenging stereotypes.
            - Modern Era: Portrays career-oriented, independent women balancing work and life.

    - South Indian Cinema
        - South Indian cinema includes five industries: Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, Malayalam, and Tulu. Telugu and Tamil are the largest.
            - Mythological Films :
                - Telugu cinema is known for mythological stories from Ramayana and Mahabharata. N.T. 
                - Rama Rao was famous for roles like Krishna, Rama, and Shiva. Kannada and Tamil films also depict such themes.
            - Social Issues :
                - Films address topics like corruption, dowry, unemployment, remarriage, and violence against women. 
                - From the 1940s-1960s, many had political messages.
            - Notable Actors :
                - M.G. Ramachandran, N.T. Rama Rao, Shivaji Ganesan, Gemini Ganesan, Rajkumar, Vishnuvardhan, Rajinikanth, Mohan Lal, Kamal Haasan, Mammootty, Ajith Kumar, Chiranjeevi, Mahesh Babu, Vijay.
            - Notable Actresses :
                - Savitri, Jayasudha, Lakshmi, Suhasini, Sridevi, Revathy, Shobana, Soundarya, Padmini, Jayalalitha, Anjali Devi.

    - Recent Film Controversies
        - Several films faced bans or protests.
            - Censorship Issues –
                - Bandit Queen (1994) was banned for nudity.
                - Water was banned for showing widow struggles in the 1930s.
                - Fire (1996) was banned for depicting a lesbian relationship.
            - Political & Religious Sensitivities –
                - Madras Cafe was banned in Sri Lanka and parts of the UK.
                - Anurag Kashyap’s Paanch was banned due to drug abuse and violence.
                - Black Friday was initially banned but later released.
                - Messenger of God (2014) sparked violence in North India.
                - Vishwaroopam faced protests in Tamil Nadu but was later cleared.

    - Indian Cinematograph Act, 1952
        - The Act regulates film certification and censorship in India.
        - Key Features –
            - Established the CBFC (Censor Board).
            - CBFC has a Chairman and 12-25 members, appointed by the government.
            - Reviews films for concerns related to religion, politics, and public sentiment.
            - Can suggest modifications or refuse certification.
        - Film Certification Categories –
            - U – Universal exhibition.
            - A – Adults only.
            - UA – Parental guidance for children under 12.
            - S – Restricted to specific audiences (e.g., doctors, engineers).

    - Central Board of Film Certification (CBFC)
        - 
        - Created in 1950, renamed under the 1952 Act.
        - Works under the Ministry of Information & Broadcasting.
        - Head office in Mumbai; regional offices across India.
        - All films, including foreign and dubbed films, need CBFC certification.
        - Exceptions – Doordarshan films do not need CBFC approval.
        - Shyam Benegal Committee (2016) suggested:
            - CBFC should only certify films, not censor them.
            - Online application process for film certification.
            - New age-based categories – UA12+, UA15+, A, AC (Adult with Caution).

    - Need for a National Film Policy
        - India is the world’s largest film producer.
        - Government controls content but not industry growth.
        - Regional cinema needs more recognition.
        - More film schools should be established.
        - Policies should promote Indian cinema globally.
        - Digitization and anti-piracy laws are essential.
        - Censorship laws should adapt to changing times.

- Science and Technology Through the Ages
    - Introduction
        - India has a rich history of both spiritual and material contributions to the world. 
        - While Indian religions such as Buddhism, Jainism, and Hinduism influenced many invaders like Greeks, Persians, Huns, and Mongols, India’s scientific advancements also left a global impact. 
        - Key contributions include:
            - Distillation of perfumes
            - Dye-making techniques
            - Sugar extraction
            - Cotton weaving
            - Algebra and algorithm development
            - Concept of zero
            - Surgical techniques
            - Atomic and relativity concepts
            - Herbal medicine
            - Alchemy
            - Metal smelting
            - Chess, martial arts, and karate
    
    - Mathematics (Ganita)
        - Mathematics in ancient India was classified into five major branches:
            - Arithmetic (Pattin Ganita/Anka Ganita)
            - Algebra (Bija Ganita)
            - Geometry (Rekha Ganita)
            - Astronomy (Khagolshastra)
            - Astrology (Jyotisa)
        - Between 1000 BC and 1000 AD, Indian mathematicians authored numerous treatises covering these topics.
        - Early Developments
            - The Harappan town planning shows knowledge of measurement and geometry.
            - Geometric patterns appear in temples as motifs.
            - Bijaganita (Algebra) means “the other mathematics,” implying an advanced system beyond conventional computation.
            - Shulbasutra (6th century BC by Baudhayana) mentions:
                - Square root of 2
                - Concepts similar to Pythagoras’ Theorem
                - Fire altar construction involving geometry
            - Apastamba (2nd century BC) introduced acute, obtuse, and right angles, aiding altar construction.
        - Aryabhata (499 AD)
            - 
            - Wrote Aryabhatiya, covering:
                - Large time units
                - Number theory, geometry, trigonometry, and algebra
                - Astronomy (Jyotisa)
            - First to state:
                - The Earth is round and rotates on its own axis.
                - Pi’s value more accurately than Greeks.
                - Lunar and solar eclipses are caused by shadows, not mythological demons.
            - Arabs called mathematics Hindisat (Indian Art).
        - Concept of Zero
            - Derived from Shunya (void), a Hindu philosophical concept.
            - Linked to Buddhist Nirvana (salvation through merging into eternity).
            - The concept of zero was developed in India by the mathematician Brahmagupta in the 7th century.
        - Brahmagupta (7th century AD)
            - Wrote Brahmasputa Siddhanta, defining zero as a number.
            - Introduced negative numbers (debts) and positive numbers (fortunes).
            - First clear description of the quadratic formula.
        - Bhaskara I (7th century AD)
            - Wrote Aryabhatiyabhasya, dealing with mathematics and astronomy.
            - Honored by ISRO with Bhaskara I satellite (1979).
        - Bhaskaracharya (Bhaskara II, 12th century AD)
            - Wrote Siddhanta Shiromani, divided into:
                - Lilavati (Arithmetic)
                - Bijganita (Algebra)
                - Goladhyaya (Spheres)
                - Grahaganita (Planetary Mathematics)
            - Introduced Chakrawat (Cyclic) method for solving algebraic equations.
            - Lilavati was translated into Persian by Faizi (court of Akbar) and later by James Taylor in the 19th century.
        - Later Contributions
            - Narayan Pandit (Medieval period) wrote:
                - Ganitakaumudi
                - Bijaganitavatamsa
            - Nilakantha Somayaji wrote Tantrasamgraha (astronomy and trigonometry).
            - Feroz Shah Tughlaq and Feroz Shah Bahamani established observatories in Delhi and Daulatabad.
            - Mahendra Suri (court astronomer of Bahamani) invented Yantaraja (astronomical instrument).
            - Sawai Jai Singh built five observatories in Delhi, Jaipur, Varanasi, Ujjain, and Mathura.

    - Medicine
        - Vedic Medicine
            - Ashwini Kumars were considered divine healers.
            - Dhanvantari was regarded as the God of Ayurveda.
            - Atharva Veda (first medical text) attributed diseases to demons and spirits, prescribing charms and spells as cures.
        - Classical Period (600 BC Onwards)
            - Takshila and Varanasi emerged as medical education centers.
            - Charak Samhita (Father of Ayurveda, Charak) emphasized:
                - Prevention over cure.
                - Digestion, metabolism, and immunity.
                - Three Doshas (Bile, Phlegm, Wind) and their balance.
            - Sushruta Samhita (Father of Surgery, Sushruta) covered:
                - 
                - Plastic surgery (Rhinoplasty)
                - Cataract surgery (Ophthalmology)
                - Detailed surgical procedures.
        - Medieval Period
            - Sarangdhara Samhita (13th century) discussed opium in medicine and urine-based diagnostics.
            - Rasachikitsa introduced mineral-based medicine.
            - Unani Medicine entered India via Firdous-al-Hikmah (Ali-ibn-Rabban).

    - Physics and Chemistry
        - Atomic Theory
            - Kanada and Pakudha Katyayana (6th century BC) introduced the concept of Parmanu (atoms).
            - They suggested that indivisible particles form matter, a precursor to modern atomic theory.
        - Chemistry
            - Known as Rasayan Shastra, Rasatantra, Rasa Vidya, or Rasakriya.
            - Key applications:
                - Metallurgy (smelting of metals)
                - Perfume distillation
                - Dye-making
                - Sugar extraction
                - Gunpowder production
                - Paper manufacturing
        - Metallurgy
            - Iron Pillar of Mehrauli (Delhi) and Buddha idol (Sultanganj, Bihar) exhibit rust-resistant metallurgy.
            - Nagarjuna (931 AD), a renowned alchemist, attempted to transform base metals into gold.
                - 
            - Rasaratnakara described mercury-based experiments.
            - Rasarnava (12th century) discussed alchemy and Tantrism.
            - Ain-i-Akbari documented Akbar’s perfume regulation office.
        - Geology, Hydrology, and Ecology
            - Varahamihira (Gupta Period) predicted earthquakes, underground water sources, and plant indicators of water.

    - Shipbuilding and Navigation
        - Yukti Kalpa Taru (Sanskrit treatise) describes shipbuilding techniques, materials, and classifications:
            - Samanya (Ordinary Class) – for sea voyages
            - Vishesha (Special Class) – advanced design
        - Ship types:
            - Dirgha (long, narrow hull)
            - Unnata (higher hull)
        - Ship terminology:
            - Anchor – Nava Bandhan Kilaha
            - Sail – Vata Vastra
            - Compass – Machayantra (Fish Machine)

    - Ancient Indian Sports
        - Kalaripayattu (Kerala) – Influenced Chinese martial arts, leading to Judo and Karate.
        - Chess (Chaturanga) – Played with dice and counters, mentioned in Mahabharata.


- Calendar and Timekeeping Systems in India
    - Calendar
        - A calendar is a system for organizing days for social, religious, commercial, or administrative purposes. 
        - It assigns names to periods such as days, weeks, months, and years. A date refers to a specific day within this system. 
        - A calendar can also be a physical record (often on paper) of this system.
        
    - About Calendars in India
        - In India, different systems have emerged over time to mark the beginning of a new year.
        - These calendar systems fall into three main categories:
            - Solar System
            - Lunar System
            - Luni-Solar System
        - These systems are based on astronomical calculations that track the movement of celestial bodies.
        - Types of Years in Indian Calendars
            - Solar Year
                - Represents the time taken by the Earth to complete one revolution around the Sun.
                - Includes 365 days, 5 hours, 48 minutes, and 46 seconds.
                - Corresponds closely to seasonal cycles.
                - Divided into 12 months.
            - Lunar Year
                - Consists of 12 lunations (lunar months).
                - Each month is measured between two successive full moons or new moons.
                - A lunar year has 354 days, making it 11 days shorter than the solar year.
                - To align with the solar year, an extra month (Adhik Masa) is added every 2 years and 6 months.
            - Luni-Solar Year
                - Months are based on the lunar cycle, but the year follows the solar cycle.
                - Adjustments are made through intercalation (adding days or months) and suppression.
        - Months in the Indian Calendar Systems
            - Solar Month
                - 
                - The solar year has 12 months, named after the zodiac signs (Rashis), which are:
                    - Mesha (Aries)
                    - Vrishabha (Taurus)
                    - Mithuna (Gemini)
                    - Karka (Cancer)
                    - Simha (Leo)
                    - Kanya (Virgo)
                    - Tula (Libra)
                    - Vrischika (Scorpio)
                    - Dhanus (Sagittarius)
                    - Makara (Capricorn)
                    - Kumbha (Aquarius)
                    - Meena (Pisces)
            - Lunar Month
                - A lunar month ends with either a new moon (Amavasya) or a full moon (Purnima).
                - Two methods determine when a lunar month starts:
                - Amasanta: Starts with the bright fortnight (Bright Half).
                - Purnimanta: Starts with the dark fortnight (Dark Half).
                - The lunar month system is widely followed in most regions of India.
                - Adhik Masa (Intercalary Month)
                    - Since the lunar year is shorter than the solar year, an extra month (Adhik Masa or Mal Masa) is added every 2 years and 6 months to synchronize the two.
                        - The Sun moves through one rashi (zodiac sign) per month, but its motion is not uniform.
                        - Sankranti marks the entry of the Sun into a new zodiac sign and occurs 12 times a year.
                        - If a lunar month lacks a sankranti, an Adhik Masa is added.
                        - If two sankrantis occur in one month, that month is Kshaya Masa (a deleted month).
        - Divisions of Time in the Indian Calendar
            - Pakshas (Fortnights) in the Lunar Calendar
                - Shukla Paksha – Bright half, starting after the new moon.
                - Krishna Paksha – Dark half, starting after the full moon.
        - Days in the Calendar
            - Lunar Day (Tithi or Vasara)
                - A tithi (lunar day) is shorter than a solar day, lasting about 23 hours and 37 minutes (23 minutes less than a solar day).
            - Solar Day (Divasa)
        - Further Divisions of Time
            - 1 Divasa (day and night) = 24 hours = 60 Ghatikas
            - 1 Ghatika = 60 Palas = 24 minutes
            - 1 Pala = 60 Vipalas = 24 seconds
            - 2 Ghatikas = 1 Muhurta = 48 minutes
            - 2.5 Muhurtas = 2 hours

    - Hindu Calendar (Panchanga)
        - The Panchanga (Hindu calendar) is based on five elements:
            1.	Tithi (Lunar Day)
            2.	Vaara (Weekday)
            3.	Nakshatra (Lunar Mansion)
            4.	Yoga (Astronomical Combination)
            5.	Karana (Half-Tithi)
        - The twelve zodiac signs (Rashis) are linked to Nakshatras (constellations). There are 28 Nakshatras, each consisting of a different number of stars.
        - The Hindu solar year is divided into two halves:
            - Uttarayana (God’s Day) – From Makara Sankranti to Karka Sankranti (January to June).
            - Dakshinayana (God’s Night) – From July to December.
        - One solar year equals one day and night of the gods.
        - The Four Yugas (Eras in Hindu Timekeeping)
            - According to Hindu beliefs, time is divided into four Yugas (ages), forming a repeating cycle:
                1.	Satya Yuga (Krita Yuga) – The golden age of truth and perfection.
                    - No suffering, agriculture, or mining.
                    - Humans were virtuous and saintly.
                    - Lifespan: Extremely long.
                2.	Treta Yuga – The rise of emperors and wars.
                    - Weather became extreme.
                    - Agriculture and labor began.
                    - Lifespan: 1,000-10,000 years.
                3.	Dvapara Yuga – The decline of virtue.
                    - Diseases and conflicts increased.
                    - Humans became weaker.
                    - Lifespan: A few centuries.
                4.	Kali Yuga – The current age of darkness and ignorance.
                    - Moral and physical decline.
                    - People are driven by greed and passions.
                    - Lifespan: 100 years (expected to decrease to 20 years by the end of the Yuga).
            - The current era is Kali Yuga, which began in 3102 BCE.
            - Brahma’s Timekeeping
                - One Mahayuga = 4.32 million years
                - 1,000 Mahayugas = One Day of Brahma (4.32 billion years)
                - A Mahakalpa (Brahma’s lifespan) = 100 Brahma years

    - Classification of Indian Calendar Forms
        - Vikram Samvat
            - Origin: Started in 56 BCE.
            - Founder: Attributed to King Vikramaditya of Ujjain.
            - Type: Luni-solar calendar, 56.7 years ahead of the Gregorian calendar.
            - New Year: Begins after the new moon in Chaitra (March–April).
            - Months: 12 lunar months – Chaitra, Vaisakha, Jyeshtha, Ashadha, Sravana, Bhadrapada, Ashwina, Kartika, Margashirsha, Pausha, Magha, Phalguna.
            - Adjustment: Adhik Masa (extra month) added every 3 years.
        - Saka Samvat
            - Origin: Began in 78 CE.
            - Founder: King Shalivahan.
            - Type: Luni-solar calendar.
            - New Year: Starts on March 22 (March 21 in leap years).
            - Months: Same as Vikram Samvat, but start differently.
            - Days in a Year: 365 days.
            - Zero Year: 78 CE.
        - Hijri Calendar
            - Origin: Based on Prophet Muhammad’s migration in 622 CE.
            - Type: Lunar calendar, 354 days in a year.
            - Months: Muharram, Safar, Rabi-al-Awwal, Rabi-ath-Thani, Jumada-al-Ula, Jumada-al-Akhirah, Rajab, Shaban, Ramadan, Shawwal, Dhu-al-Qadah, Dhu-al-Hijjah.
            - Sacred Months: 1st, 7th, 11th, and 12th.
            - Difference: Falls one year short every 33 years compared to the Gregorian calendar.
        - Gregorian Calendar
            - Origin: Based on the birth of Jesus Christ.
            - Type: Solar calendar.
            - Year Length: 365 days, 5 hours, 48 minutes, 46 seconds.
            - Leap Year: Adds one extra day to February every 4 years.
            - Use: Official civil calendar worldwide.
        - Zoroastrian Calendar
            - Origin: Began in 632 CE.
            - Type: Solar calendar.
            - Nowruz: New Year on March 20/21/22.
            - Variants:
            - Shahenshahi Calendar (used by Parsis in India).
            - Qadimi Calendar (used by Iranians).

    - National Calendar of India
        - Official Name: Saka Calendar.
        - Adopted: 1957 by the Government of India.
        - Implementation Date: March 22, 1957 (Chaitra 1, 1879 Saka Era).
        - Purpose: To unify 30 different calendars used in India.
        - Usage:
        - Government documents.
        - News broadcasts (All India Radio).
        - Religious calculations in Hinduism.


- Fairs and festivals of India
    - Introduction
        - Festivals and fairs are an integral part of Indian culture.
        - They serve as an expression of beliefs and emotions.
        - India, being a secular country, observes holidays for festivals from different religions and communities.
        - Some festivals fall under the “restricted list”, meaning employers can choose whether to declare a holiday or not.
        - Festivals are an important way to understand Indian culture, and many tourists include them in their travel plans.

    - National Festivals
        - Celebrated to mark historical events of national significance.
        - Instill patriotism in the hearts of Indians.
        - India has three National Festivals:
            - Republic day (26th January)
            - Independence day (15th August) 
            - Gandhi Jayanti (2nd October)
        
    - Religios festivals
        - Religious festivals are celebrated by specific communities based on their beliefs. However, in a secular country like India, people from different religions also participate.
        - For example, Holi, a Hindu festival, is enjoyed by non-Hindus as well.
        - Hindu Festivals
            

                

                    Name
                    Details
                

                

                    Diwali or Deepawali
                    It is the festival of lights celebrated on the new moon (Amavasya) day in the month of Kartik, which generally falls in October and November. The day before the festival is called Naraka Chaturdashi.
                

                

                    Dev Deepawali
                    Celebrated in Varanasi (Uttar Pradesh), it falls on the full moon of the Hindu month of Kartika (November-December), 15 days after Diwali. People light lamps on the ghats of River Ganga. It is also observed as Tripura Purnima Snan.
                

                

                    Holi
                    It is the festival of colors, celebrated across religions in the month of Phalguna (February-March). It signifies the victory of good over evil, i.e., the burning of Holika and the saving of Bhakt Prahlad. In West Bengal and parts of Assam, it is known as Dol Jatra.
                

                

                    Makara Sankranti
                    Dedicated to the Sun God, it celebrates the transition of the Sun in the Northern Hemisphere. Celebrated in January, lakhs of people pilgrimage to Gangasagar (West Bengal) and Prayagraj for a holy dip. It is also celebrated as the Kite Flying Festival in some parts of India.
                

                

                    Janamasthami
                    Celebrates the birth anniversary of Lord Krishna and generally falls in August.
                

                

                    Dussehra
                    Also known as Bijaya Dashmi, it is celebrated across India to honor the victory of Lord Rama over Ravana. Ravana Dahan (burning of Ravana effigies) is a common feature, especially in North India.
                

                

                    Durga Puja
                    Majorly celebrated in Eastern India (especially West Bengal), it marks Goddess Durga’s triumph over demon Mahisasura.
                

                

                    Ganesh Chaturthi
                    Celebrates the birth anniversary of Lord Ganesha. It is widely celebrated across India, with Maharashtra hosting the grandest festivities.
                

                

                    Rath Yatra (Chariot Festival)
                    The biggest festival of Odisha, dedicated to Lord Jagannath, Balabhadra (his brother), and Subhadra (his sister). The chariot festival of Puri is the largest and most famous.
                

                

                    Chhath Puja
                    The main festival of Bihar and Jharkhand, celebrated to honor the Sun Goddess. It involves rigorous fasting, holy baths, and offerings to the Sun Goddess. Thekua (a sweet dish) is a special offering.
                

                

                    Nabakalebar Festival
                    Observed at Shri Jagannath Temple, Puri (Odisha) after every 8 to 19 years. Nabakalebar means "new body," where the idols of Lord Jagannath, Balabhadra, Subhadra, and Sudarshan are replaced with new ones made from selected neem trees. In March 2018, the President of India released Rs 1000 and Rs 10 commemorative coins for this festival.
                

            

        - Muslim Festivals
            - Eid-ul-Fitr
                - Celebrated worldwide by the Muslim community, marking the end of the holy month of Ramadan (ninth month of the Islamic calendar).
                - During Ramadan, Muslims fast from sunrise to sunset as ordained in Sharia law.
                - The festival is observed on the first day of Shawwal, following the sighting of the moon at the end of Ramadan.
                - According to Islamic traditions, the Holy Quran was revealed on one of the odd nights during the last ten days of Ramadan, often calculated as the 27th night.
                - Ramadan is historically significant as:
                    - The Prophet Muhammad achieved victory in the Battle of Badr, leading to the conquest of Mecca.
                    - The martyrdom of Ali (the Prophet’s son-in-law) occurred on the 21st day of Ramadan.
            - Eid-ul-Zuha (Eid-al-Adha / Bakr-Eid)
                - Celebrated on the 10th day of Dhu-al-Hijjah (12th month of the Islamic calendar).
                - Honors the devotion of Prophet Ibrahim, who was willing to sacrifice his son as an act of obedience to Allah.
                - Instead, Allah replaced the sacrifice with a goat, and in remembrance, Muslims sacrifice a goat on this day.
                - The meat is distributed as follows:
                    - One-third for family
                    - One-third for relatives and neighbors
                    - One-third for the poor
                - This Eid also marks the beginning of Hajj, the sacred pilgrimage to Mecca.
            - Milad-un-Nabi (Barah-wafat)
                - Commemorates the birth anniversary of Prophet Muhammad, observed on the 12th day of Rabi-al-Awwal (3rd month of the Islamic calendar).
                - The day is believed to also mark the Prophet’s passing, making the celebrations subdued.
                - A national holiday in many countries, observed with solemnity and reverence.
                - Celebrations include:
                    - Quran recitations in mosques
                    - Recital of Qasida al-Burda Sharif, a sacred 13th-century poem by Sufi poet Busiri
                    - Singing Nats (poems praising the Prophet)
                - Known as Barah (12) wafat (death), symbolizing the 12 days of illness before the Prophet’s passing.
                - Holds special significance in Kashmir, where the Prophet’s relics are displayed at Hazratbal Shrine in Srinagar, drawing thousands of devotees.
            - Muharram
                - A solemn occasion marking the martyrdom of Hussain, the grandson of Prophet Muhammad.
                - Falls in the first month of the Islamic calendar, which also marks the Islamic New Year.
                - The 10th day, Yaum-al-Ashura, is a day of mourning, particularly for Shia Muslims.
                - Observed in memory of Hussain Bin Ali’s martyrdom in the Battle of Karbala (61 Hijri / 680 AD).
                - Traditions in India:
                    - Processions (Tajia)
                    - Self-flagellation with chains (by some Shia mourners) to replicate Hussain’s suffering
                    - Wearing black clothing as a sign of mourning
                    - Distribution of sherbet or juice
            - Shab-e-Barat (Night of Emancipation)
                - Observed on the night between the 14th and 15th of Shaban (8th month of the Islamic calendar).
                - Believed to be the night when people’s destinies are determined.
                - Among Shia Muslims, the 15th of Shaban is celebrated as the birth anniversary of Imam Muhammad Al-Mahdi, the 12th Imam, revered for his role in eliminating oppression and injustice.
            - Shab-e-Miraj (Night of Ascent)
                - Observed on the 27th day of Rajab (7th month of the Islamic calendar), two years before Hijra.
                - Commemorates Prophet Muhammad’s miraculous journey through the heavens, where he reached near Almighty.
                - It was on this journey that five daily prayers were made obligatory for Muslims.
                - Observances include:
                    - Mosques decorated with lights and candles
                    - Recitations of hymns and praises for the Prophet
                    - Narration of spiritual stories of the Holy Prophet
                    - Acts of charity, including food distribution to the poor
                    - Devotees spending the entire night in prayer and remembrance of God.
        - Christian Festivals
            - Christmas
                - Celebrated worldwide on December 25 to mark the birth of Jesus Christ.
                - Festivities begin with midnight mass held in churches on the night of December 24–25, signifying Christ’s birth at midnight.
                - People attend church services, which include special programs in remembrance of Christ’s teachings.
                - Traditions associated with Christmas:
                    - Christmas Tree: Decorated with lights and ornaments in homes.
                    - Santa Claus: A mythical figure who brings gifts to children.
                    - Carol Singing: People sing religious hymns and distribute sweets and cakes.
                    - Exchange of gifts and visiting loved ones.
            - Easter and Good Friday
                - Easter: Celebrates the resurrection of Jesus Christ, three days after his crucifixion.
                - Signifies the victory of life over death and is one of the most important Christian festivals.
                - In early Christianity, Jewish Christians observed Easter on Nisan 14, while others celebrated it on the Sunday closest to Nisan 14.
                - The Council of Nicaea (325 AD) fixed Easter’s date as the first Sunday after the first full moon following the vernal equinox (around March 21).
                - Good Friday:
                    - Commemorates the crucifixion of Jesus Christ and falls in April each year.
                    - Considered necessary for Jesus’s resurrection, symbolizing hope and love for humanity.
                    - Special masses are held in churches worldwide.
        - Sikh Festivals
            - Gurpurab
                - Celebrated by Sikhs worldwide to mark the birth anniversaries of the 10 Sikh Gurus.
                - The most significant Gurpurabs honor Guru Nanak and Guru Gobind Singh.
                - Other Gurpurabs commemorate the martyrdom of Guru Arjan Dev and Guru Tegh Bahadur, who sacrificed their lives resisting Mughal persecution.
                - Celebrations include:
                    - Akhand Path (continuous reading of Guru Granth Sahib).
                    - Prabhat Pheris (early morning processions with hymn singing).
                    - Langar (community meals at Gurudwaras).
                    - A grand procession with Guru Granth Sahib placed on a floral float, led by Panj Pyare (five beloved ones of Guru Gobind Singh) carrying Sikh flags (Nishan Sahib).
            - Parkash Utsav Dasveh Patshah
                - Celebrates the birth of Guru Gobind Singh, the 10th Sikh Guru.
                - Also called the Birth Celebration of the 10th Divine Light.
                - Observed annually on January 31.
            - Maghi
                - An annual Sikh gathering celebrated in Muktsar.
                - Honors the 40 Sikh martyrs (Chali Mukte) who fought against the Mughals.
                - The 10th Guru, Guru Gobind Singh, died in battle against Wazir Khan in 1705.
                - Devotees take a procession to the battlefield and bathe in the sacred waters of Muktsar.
                - Observed on January 14 each year.
            - Hola Mohalla
                - A grand Sikh festival held annually in Anandpur Sahib in March.
                - Started by Guru Gobind Singh as an event for mock battles and military drills.
                - Includes displays of horse riding, swordsmanship, kirtan, and poetry competitions.
                - Often referred to as the “Sikh Olympics” for its martial arts competitions.
            - Vaisakhi/Baisakhi
                - Celebrated on April 13 or 14, marking the Sikh New Year and the founding of Khalsa Panth.
                - Also a spring harvest festival.
                - Observances include:
                    - Gurudwaras decorated with kirtans (hymns).
                    - Sikhs taking a ritual bath in sacred rivers.
                    - Visiting temples, meeting friends, and celebrating with festive food.
            - Lohri
                - Celebrated on January 13, a day before Makar Sankranti.
                - Marks fertility and the spark of life.
                - Observances:
                    - People gather around bonfires, throw sweets, puffed rice, and popcorn into the flames.
                    - Singing of folk songs and exchanging greetings.
                    - Symbolizes the victory of light over darkness.
            - Sodal Mela
                - One of the major fairs of Punjab, held in Jalandhar in September (month of Bhadon).
                - Organized in homage to Baba Sodal, a revered spiritual figure.
                - Celebrations include:
                    - The Samadhi of Baba Sodal is adorned with rosaries and flowers.
                    - Devotees take a holy dip in Sodal ka Sarovar (a sacred tank).
                    - Offerings are made at the Samadhi, and prayers are conducted.
        - Jain Festivals
            - Mahavir Jayanti
                - Celebrates the birth anniversary of Lord Mahavira, the 24th Tirthankara of Jainism.
                - Observed on the 13th day of Chaitra’s waxing moon.
                - Jain temples are decorated with saffron flags.
                - The idol of Mahavira is given a ceremonial bath (abhishek) and taken in a procession.
            - Paryushana
                - An annual Jain festival lasting 8 days for Svetambaras and 10 days for Digambaras in Bhadrapada (August/September).
                - Marks the monsoon retreat of Jain monks.
                - Rituals include temple visits, listening to Kalpa Sutra discourses, and Pratikraman (meditation kriya).
                - Concludes with Kshamavani (Forgiveness Day), where people seek forgiveness by saying “Micchami Dukkadam”.
            - Mahamastakabhisheka
                - Held once every 12 years in Shravanabelagola, Karnataka.
                - A holy bathing ceremony of the 57-ft high statue of Siddha Bahubali, son of Rishabhdev.
                - The statue is bathed with milk, sugarcane juice, saffron paste, and powders of sandalwood, turmeric, and vermilion.
                - Devotees offer flower petals, gold and silver coins, and precious stones.
            - Gyana Panchami
                - Celebrated on the fifth day of Kartika as Knowledge Day.
                - Holy scriptures are displayed and worshipped.
            - Varshi Tapa (Akshay Tritiya Tapa)
                - Honors Rishabhdev, the first Jain Tirthankara, who fasted for 13 months and 13 days.
                - The fast ended on the third day of Vaishakh’s bright fortnight in the Jain calendar.
                - Observers of this fasting are called Varshi Tapa.
            - Maun-Agiyara
                - Celebrated on the 11th day of Magshar (October/November).
                - Observers maintain complete silence and fast for the entire day.
                - Meditation is a key part of the observance.
            - Navapad Oli
                - A nine-day semi-fasting festival.
                - Observers eat only one plain meal per day.
                - Occurs twice a year: March/April and September/October.
        - Buddhist Festivals
            - Buddha Purnima (Buddha Jayanti)
                - Celebrates the birth anniversary of Lord Buddha in April/May.
                - Known as Saga Dawa (Dasa) in Sikkim and Vishakha Puja in Theravada tradition.
                - Major celebrations in Sarnath (Uttar Pradesh) and Bodh Gaya (Bihar).
                - Rituals include prayers, sermons on Buddha’s life, chanting scriptures, worshipping Buddha’s idol and the Bodhi tree, and meditation.
                - Different sects celebrate differently:
                    - Mahayana Buddhists: Organize large processions with musical instruments like gyalings and read Kangyur texts.
                    - Theravada Buddhists: Focus on offering ceremonial prayers to Buddha’s idols.
            - Songkran
                - A spring cleaning festival held in mid-April.
                - People clean houses, wash clothes, and sprinkle perfumed water on monks.
            - Ploughing Festival
                - Celebrates Buddha’s first enlightenment experience at the age of seven.
                - Held in May, marking the occasion when Buddha watched a ploughing event with his father.
                - Ritual reenactment includes two white oxen pulling a gold-painted plough, followed by four girls dressed in white scattering rice seeds.
            - Ulambana (Ancestor Day)
                - Observed from the 1st to the 15th day of the 8th lunar month.
                - Based on the belief that Hell’s gates open, allowing ghosts to visit the world for 15 days.
                - Food offerings are made to ease the suffering of wandering spirits.
                - On the 15th day, people visit cemeteries and make offerings to ancestors.
            - Hemis Gompa Festival
                - Held at Hemis Gompa Monastery (Ladakh) in honor of Guru Padmasambhava (Rinpoche), the founder of Tibetan Buddhism.
                - Celebrates the victory of good over evil.
                - Main attraction: Mask dance performed by Lamas, accompanied by traditional music using cymbals, large-pan drums, small trumpets, and wind instruments.
        - Sindhi Festivals
            - Chaliho Sahib
                - A 40-day fast observed in July-August.
                - Devotees pray to Lord Jhulelal for 40 days.
                - The festival ends with Thanksgiving Day celebrations.
                - The festival originates from the time when Mirkshah Badshah, a Muslim invader of Sindh, persecuted the people of Thatta.
                - The people prayed to Varun Devta (Water God) by fasting for 40 days along the riverbanks.
                - On the 40th day, Jhulelal appeared as an answer to their prayers.
            - Cheti Chand (Sindhi New Year)
                - Celebrated worldwide on Chaitra’s first day.
                - Honors the birth anniversary of Jhulelal, the patron saint of Sindhis.
                - Celebrated with pomp and joy.
                - Sindhis take Baharana Sahib (a ceremonial offering) to a nearby river. It consists of:
                    - Jyot (sacred flame)
                    - Misiri (crystals of sugar)
                    - Phota (cardamom)
                    - Fal (fruits)
                    - Akha (rice grains)
                - A Jhulelal idol is carried in procession as part of the celebrations.
        - Parsi (Zoroastrian) Festivals
            - Jamshedi Navroz
                - Celebrates the New Year for the Parsi community.
                - Falls on Roj Hormuzd, the first day of Mah Frawardin in the Shehanshahi calendar.
                - Marks the end of winter and the beginning of the New Year, symbolizing the Universal Dawn.
                - Parsis pay respect to Khorshed and Meher Yazads, divine beings associated with the Sun.
                - People visit each other and the Fire Temple to offer prayers.
            - Other Parsi Festivals
                

                    

                        Name of the Festival
                        Falls on the Day
                        Occasion
                        Rituals Observed
                    

                    

                        Zarthost No Deeso
                        On the 11th day of the 10th month (Khorshedroz, Daemah)
                        Death anniversary of Prophet Zoroaster
                        Parsis or Zoroastrians go to the Fire Temple, offer prayers, and listen to the life and works of the Prophet.
                    

                    

                        Khordad Sal
                        On the sixth day of the Parsi month of Farvardin (August/September)
                        Birth anniversary of Prophet
                        Parsis offer prayers and hold special grand feasts.
                    

                    

                        Pateti
                        On the last Gatha day of the Parsi calendar
                        To offer repentance for crimes committed during the year
                        Parsis offer prayers, give alms to the poor, and make feasts at home.
                    

                


    - Secular Festivals
        - Khajuraho Dance Festival
            - Instituted in 1975 by the Government of India in collaboration with the Madhya Pradesh Kala Parishad.
            - Promotes tourism in the state and highlights the beauty and eroticism of Khajuraho temples.
            - Reflects the spirit of eternal glory, perseverance of dance, and architectural heritage.
        - New Year
            - Marks the beginning of a new Hindu calendar, believed to be the day when Lord Brahma began the creation of the world.
            - Celebrated under different names across India:
                

                    

                        Festival Name
                        Region
                    

                    

                        Ugadi or Chaitra Shudhdha Paadyami
                        Andhra Pradesh and Karnataka
                    

                    

                        Gudi Padwa or Gudi Padvo
                        Maharashtra
                    

                    

                        Samvatsar Padvo
                        Goa
                    

                    

                        Naba Barsha (Poila Boisakh)
                        West Bengal
                    

                    

                        Puthandu
                        Tamil Nadu
                    

                    

                        Vishu
                        Kerala
                    

                

        - Sair-e-Gul Faroshan (Phool Walon Ki Sair)
            - Annual three-day festival of flowers in Delhi, symbolizing communal harmony.
            - A procession of decorated palm leaf fans (pankhas) is taken from the tomb of Khwaja Bakhtiyar Kaki (Mehrauli) to the Yogmaya Temple.
            - Initially patronized by Mughal Emperor Akbar II, banned by the British, and reinstated in 1962 by J. L. Nehru.
        - Tyagaraja Aradhana
            - Commemorates the ‘Samadhi’ day of the famous Telugu Saint and composer Tyagaraja.
            - Held in Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu, primarily at Thiruvaiyaru.
            - Attended by leading exponents of Carnatic music who pay tribute to Tyagaraja.
            - Tyagaraja, along with Muthuswami Dikshitar and Syama Sastri, forms the Trinity of Carnatic music.
        - Onam
            - State festival of Kerala, celebrated in the first month of the Malayalam calendar (Chingam).
            - A harvest festival that also marks the homecoming of King Mahabali.
            - Celebrations include feasts, dances, floral arrangements, boat races, and elephant processions.
            - The highlight is the Vallam Kali (Snake Boat Race) at Punnamada Lake, with winners awarded the Nehru Boat Race Trophy.
        - Pongal
            - Four-day Tamil harvest festival in January, marking the beginning of Uttarayan (northward journey of the Sun).
            - The word ‘Pongal’ means ‘to boil’ in Tamil, signifying the ritual of boiling the first rice of the season.
            - Held in the month of Thai when crops like rice, sugarcane, and turmeric are harvested.
            - Dedicated to Sun God, celebrating the life cycle of grain.
        - Sarhul
            - Tribal New Year festival in Jharkhand, Odisha, and West Bengal, mainly celebrated by Munda, Oraon, and Ho tribes.
            - ‘Sarhul’ means ‘Worship of Sal’ (Sal tree is revered in Sarnaism).
            - Celebrated in the spring season (Phalgun month in Hindu calendar).
            - Tribals worship Mother Earth and perform the traditional Sarhul dance.

    - FESTIVALS OF NORTH-EAST INDIA
        - Saga Dawa (Triple Blessed Festival) – Sikkim
            - Celebrated by Buddhist communities on the full moon day of the Tibetan lunar month Saga Dawa (May-June).
            - Commemorates Buddha’s birth, enlightenment, and parinirvana (death).
            - People visit monasteries, offer incense, chant mantras, recite religious texts, and turn prayer wheels.
            - Observes three Buddhist principles: generosity (dana), morality (sila), and meditation (bhavana).
        - Losoong Festival – Sikkim
            - Sikkimese New Year, mainly celebrated by the Bhutia tribe, also observed by Lepchas.
            - Harvest festival marked by traditional dances like Cham Dance and Black Hat Dance at monasteries.
            - Festivities include drinking locally brewed wine (Chaang) and archery competitions.
        - Bihu Festival – Assam
            - 
            - Set of three non-religious Assamese festivals:
                - Bohag Bihu (Rongali Bihu) – Assamese New Year (April)
                - Kongali Bihu (Kati Bihu) – October
                - Bhogali Bihu (Maagh Bihu) – January
            - Bohag Bihu is the most important, celebrated for many days (starting April 14).
            - Includes traditional Assamese songs, dances, and community feasts.
            - Rituals: bathing cows (Gora Bihu), exchanging Gamosa (woven cotton towels), and preparing Pitha (rice-based sweets).
        - Hornbill Festival – Nagaland
            - 10-day festival starting on December 1 at Kisama Heritage Village.
            - Showcases Naga tribes’ cultures, including costumes, weapons, headgears, and traditional performances.
            - Moatsu Mong Festival – Nagaland
            - Celebrated by the Ao tribe in May after sowing is done.
            - A period of recreation and entertainment marked by songs, dances, and the lighting of a bonfire (Sangpangtu).
        - Yemshe Festival – Nagaland
            - Harvest festival of the Pochuri tribe, celebrated in September.
            - Unique custom: catching frogs is prohibited during the festival.
        - Kharchi Puja – Tripura
            - 
            - Hindu festival originally celebrated by the royal family, now widely observed.
            - Held in July over a week to worship Earth and 14 other deities.
            - Thousands of devotees visit the temple in Agartala.
        - Cheiraoba Festival – Manipur
            - Manipuri New Year, celebrated in April.
            - Associated with Sanamahi, the domestic deity of the Meitei tribe.
            - Households clean and buy new utensils and clothes.
        - Wangala Festival (100 Drums Festival) – Meghalaya
            - 
            - Celebrated by the Garo tribe to mark the end of the harvest season.
            - A thanksgiving festival for the deity ‘Misi Saljong’.
            - Features drum performances, feathered headgear, and traditional dances.
        - Kang Chingba (Ratha Yatra of Manipur)
            - Major Hindu festival similar to Puri’s Jagannath Rath Yatra.
            - Celebrated for 10 days in July, with chariots (Kang) carrying deities from Sri Govindajee Temple in Imphal.
        - Ambubachi Mela – Assam
            - 
            - Held in June at Kamakhya Temple (Guwahati), known as the ‘Mahakumbh of the East’.
            - Associated with fertility rituals; devotees seek blessings for childbirth.
            - Temple remains closed for three days, as the goddess is believed to undergo her annual menstrual cycle.
        - Other North-East Festivals:
            - Sekrenyi Festival (Purification festival of Angami tribe, Nagaland, February).
            - Majuli Festival (Assam, November, cultural event in Majuli).
            - Lui-Ngai-Ni Festival (Nagaland & Manipur, seed-sowing festival).
            - Dree Festival (Apatani tribe, Arunachal Pradesh, harvest festival).
            - Losar Festival (Monpa tribe, Arunachal Pradesh, Tibetan New Year).
            - Khan Festival (Miji tribe, Arunachal Pradesh, religious festival).

    - Fairs of India
        - A fair is a temporary gathering of people for various activities, including religious, entertainment, and commercial purposes. 
        - India hosts numerous fairs across different regions, each with unique cultural and historical significance.
        - Religious and Cultural Fairs
            - Kumbh Mela
                - The largest religious gathering in the world, where millions of people take a dip in the holy river.
                - Held at four Hindu pilgrimage sites: Prayagraj, Haridwar, Nashik-Trimbak, and Ujjain.
                - Based on Hindu mythology, linked to the churning of the ocean (‘Samudra Manthan’) and the divine nectar (‘Amrit’).
                - Held every 12 years at each location, with Ardh-Kumbh Mela at Prayagraj and Haridwar every six years.
                - UNESCO recognized it as an intangible cultural heritage in 2017.
            - Kumbh Mela Locations:
                

                    

                        Place
                        River
                    

                    

                        Prayagraj (Uttar Pradesh)
                        At the confluence of the Ganga, Yamuna, and the mythical Saraswati
                    

                    

                        Haridwar (Uttarakhand)
                        Ganga
                    

                    

                        Nashik-Trimbak (Maharashtra)
                        Godavari
                    

                    

                        Ujjain (Madhya Pradesh)
                        Shipra
                    

                

            - Sonepur Mela (Bihar)
                - One of the largest cattle fairs in Asia, held at the confluence of the Ganga and Gandak rivers.
                - Takes place in November on Kartik Poornima.
                - Historically, Chandragupta Maurya is said to have bought elephants and horses here.
            - Chitra Vichitra Fair (Gujarat)
                - Largest tribal fair in Gujarat, celebrated by the ‘Garasia’ and ‘Bhil’ tribes.
                - Tribal women mourn for their departed loved ones on ‘Amavasya’ after Holi, followed by festivities.
                - Features traditional dance, handicrafts, and silver jewelry, attracting many tourists.
            - Shamlaji Fair (Gujarat)
                - Held in honor of Lord Shamlaji, believed to be an incarnation of Krishna or Vishnu.
                - Bhil tribes deeply revere Shamlaji, calling him ‘Kaliyo Dev’.
                - Devotees take a holy bath in the Meshwo River.
                - Celebrated in November for about three weeks, with Kartik Poornima as the main day.
            - Gangasagar Mela (West Bengal)
                - Held in January-February at the mouth of the Hooghly River.
                - Pilgrims take a holy dip in the Ganges on Makar Sankranti.
                - Naga Sadhus play a prominent role in the fair’s spiritual identity.
        - Livestock and Trade Fairs
            - Pushkar Fair (Rajasthan)
                - One of the world’s largest camel and cattle fairs, held annually in Pushkar.
                - Begins on Kartik Poornima and lasts for a week.
                - Features events like camel races, moustache competitions, turban tying, and cultural performances.
                - A major attraction for foreign tourists.
            - Kolayat Fair (Kapil Muni Fair, Rajasthan)
                - Held in Bikaner on Kartik Poornima.
                - Devotees take a dip in Kolayat Lake to cleanse their sins.
                - Named after sage Kapil Muni, known for intense meditation.
                - Includes a large cattle fair and showcases Rajasthani culture.
        - Cultural and Entertainment Festivals
            - Desert Festival (Rajasthan)
                - A three-day event in Jaisalmer, usually in February.
                - Highlights Rajasthani culture with folk dances, camel rides, sand dune trips, and competitions.
                - Concludes with a folk music performance under the moonlit sky.
            - Surajkund Crafts Fair (Haryana)
                - An international crafts fair held annually in February near Faridabad.
                - Features traditional Indian crafts, pottery, weaving, sculpture, embroidery, and metalwork.
                - Includes cultural performances and regional cuisine.
            - Goa Carnival
                - Introduced by the Portuguese, celebrated 40 days before Lent.
                - Involves feasting, masked parades, street parties, and live performances.
                - Showcases Goan heritage and Portuguese cultural influence.
                - Attracts thousands of tourists annually.


- Awards and Honours
