    - Introduction
        - Awards and honours recognize outstanding achievements.
        - The Government of India grants several awards annually.

    - Awards Given by the Government of India
        - Bharat Ratna
            - 
            - Highest civilian award in India.
            - Instituted in 1954.
            - Originally for art, science, literature, and public service.
            - Expanded in 2011 to include all fields.
            - Recommended by the Prime Minister, approved by the President.
            - Maximum of three awardees per year.
            - No monetary reward, only a medal and certificate.
            - Ranked seventh in the Indian Order of Precedence.
            - Cannot be used as a prefix or suffix.

        - Padma Awards
            - Instituted in 1954.
            - Given for exceptional service in various fields.
            - Announced annually on Republic Day.
            - Not awarded in 1977, 1980, and 1993–1997.
            - Rules
                - Higher degrees of the award can be given after five years.
                - Rarely given posthumously, except in exceptional cases.
                - Must include an element of public service.
                - Government servants (except doctors & scientists) are ineligible.
            - Categories
                - Padma Vibhushan: 2nd highest civilian award.
                    - 
                - Padma Bhushan: 3rd highest civilian award.
                    - 
                - Padma Shri: 4th highest civilian award.
                    - 
        - National Film Awards
            - Started in 1954.
            - Organized by the Directorate of Film Festivals since 1973.
            - Winners chosen by a national selection panel.
            - Awarded by the President of India.
            - Includes Bollywood and regional films.

            - Dadasaheb Phalke Lifetime Achievement Award
                - Introduced in 1969.
                - India’s highest film award.
                - Given by the Directorate of Film Festivals.
                - Recognizes outstanding contributions to Indian cinema.
                - Includes a Swarna Kamal (Golden Lotus) medallion and ₹10 lakh prize.
        - Sahitya Akademi Award
            - Instituted in 1954.
            - Given by the Sahitya Akademi (National Academy of Letters).
            - Awarded annually for outstanding literary work in 24 languages.
            - Includes a ₹1 lakh cash prize and a plaque.
            - Plaque designed by filmmaker Satyajit Ray.
            - Other Literary Honours
                - Sahitya Akademi Fellowship: 
                    - Highest honour by Sahitya Akademi.
                - Bhasha Samman: 
                    - For contribution to Indian languages not covered by the Sahitya Akademi Award. ₹1 lakh prize.
                - Translation Awards: 
                    - For translating major works into 24 major languages. ₹50,000 prize.
                - Jnanpith Award: 
                    - Given for excellence in literature. ₹11 lakh prize and Saraswati statue.
                    - 
                - Saraswati Samman: 
                    - Instituted in 1991 by the K.K. Birla Foundation. ₹15 lakh prize.
                - Vyas Samman: 
                    - Instituted in 1991 by the K.K. Birla Foundation. ₹2.5 lakh prize.
        - The Fukuoka Prize
            - Established by the city of Fukuoka, Japan.
            - Recognizes contributions to Asian culture.
            - Three categories: Grand Prize, Academic Prize, Arts & Culture Prize.
            - Notable Indian Recipients
                - 1997: Romila Thapar (Historian).
                - 2004: Amjad Ali Khan (Musician).
                - 2015: Ramachandra Guha (Historian).
                - 2016: A.R. Rahman (Music Composer).
                - 2018: Teejan Bai (Folk Singer).
        

- Law and Culture
    - Introduction       
        - The Constitution of India has three sections specifically designated for the preservation of Indian culture. 
        - The Government and the Constitution focus on preservation because history, fine arts, and literary artifacts create lasting impressions on the world about our nation’s heritage. 
        - Below are the related constitutional provisions and Acts.

    - Constitutional Provisions
        - Article 29: Protection of Interests of Minorities
            - Focuses on safeguarding the culture of minority communities.
            - States:
                “Any section of the citizens residing in the territory of India or any part thereof having a distinct language, script or culture of its own shall have the right to conserve the same.”
            - Ensures communities like the tribal populations of Chhattisgarh, Rajasthan, North-East, Odisha, and small groups like Parsis can preserve their culture, language, and literature.
            - Guarantees state assistance for cultural preservation.
            - No citizen shall be denied state-funded institutional aid based on religion, caste, language, or race.
        - Article 49: Protection of Monuments and Places and Objects of National Value
            - Ensures the protection of India’s heritage sites and objects.
            - Obligates the State to protect artistic and historic monuments.
            - Prohibits spoilation, disfigurement, destruction, removal, disposal, or export of any monument declared of national importance by Parliament.
        - Article 51A(f): Value and Preserve the Rich Heritage of Indian Culture
            - Part of Fundamental Duties.
            - Commands citizens to preserve both tangible and intangible heritage.
            - Highlights the link between culture, societal evolution, and legal preservation.

    - Acts for Cultural Preservation
        - Indian Treasure Trove Act, 1878
            - Instituted by the British to protect discovered treasures.
            - Key Provisions:
                - Discoveries must be reported to the District Collector or Government treasury.
                - Failure to report or attempts to alter the treasure incur fines or imprisonment.
                - Landowners must share a portion of discovered treasures with the Government.
        - Ancient Monuments Preservation Act, 1904
            - Established by the British to safeguard privately owned historical monuments.
            - Requires a conservation agreement between the Central Government and monument owners.
            - Prevents alteration, demolition, or sale of protected monuments.
            - Later amendments:
                - 1932: Introduced the Ancient Monuments Preservation (Amendment) Act.
                - 1958: Expanded scope under “The Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Site and Remains Act.”
                - 2010: Further refined as “The Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains (Amendment and Validation) Act.”
        - The Antiquities (Export Control) Act, 1947
            - Enacted to regulate the export of antiquities after Independence.
            - Major provisions:
                - Export requires a license from the Director General of ASI.
                - ASI Director General has the authority to determine whether an object is an antiquity.
        - Ancient and Historical Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains (Declaration of National Importance) Act, 1951
            - Reaffirmed monuments and sites from the 1904 Act as nationally significant.
            - Expanded the list of protected sites to 450.
            - Later amendments:
                - 1958: Broadened protection for sculptures and carvings.
                - 2010: Further strengthened preservation measures.
            - Key provisions:
                - The Central Government can declare any monument of ancient or medieval times as nationally significant.
                - The Director General is authorized to acquire, maintain, and protect such sites.
                - Allows acquisition and preservation of antiquities, with penalties for damage.
        - Antiquities and Art Treasures Act, 1972
            - Enacted to regulate trade and prevent smuggling of cultural artifacts.
            - Key provisions:
                - Defines antiquities as objects over 100 years old, including sculptures, manuscripts, and paintings.
                - Exporting antiquities without government authorization is illegal.
                - Dealers must obtain a license and register with the Government.
                - Unauthorized export results in imprisonment (3 months to 3 years) and fines.
        - Public Records Act, 1993
            - Enacted for the permanent preservation of public records.
            - Key provisions:
                - Covers government documents, manuscripts, microfilms, and other records.
                - Each government agency must appoint a “Records Officer” and maintain a “Record Room.”
                - Records must be appraised every 25 years in consultation with the National Archives.
                - Unauthorized removal, destruction, or alteration of records is punishable.

    - Conclusion
        - The Indian Constitution and government laws ensure the preservation of cultural heritage through various provisions and Acts. 
        - These evolving legal measures reflect the dynamic relationship between law and culture, adapting to societal changes while safeguarding India’s rich legacy.


- Cultural institutions in India
    - Introduction
        • The Constitution of India assigns the Government the duty to preserve, conserve, and promote Indian culture.
        • Various government and non-government organizations work to safeguard India’s cultural heritage.
        
    - Some of these prominent institutions are:
        - The Archaeological Survey of India (ASI)
            - 
            - Operates under the Ministry of Culture.
            - India’s primary institution for archaeological research.
            - Focuses on preserving tangible heritage in ancient monuments and archaeological sites.
            - Governed by:
                - Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958.
                - Antiquities and Art Treasure Act, 1972 (prevents illegal export of Indian antiquities).
            - Employs:
                - Archaeologists, architects, conservators, epigraphists.
            - Functions through specialized branches:
                - Museums.
                - Excavation branches.
                - Epigraphy branches.
                - Building Survey Projects.
                - Horticulture branch.
                - Temple Survey Projects.
                - Underwater Archaeology Wing (unique project).
            - Offers diplomas and degrees in archaeology through the Institute of Archaeology, New Delhi.
        - Crafts Council of India (CCI)
            - Non-profit organization promoting India’s handicraft industry.
            - Founded in 1976 by Kamala Devi Chattopadhyay.
            - Purpose:
                - Support craftspersons with work and recognition.
            - Headquarters: Chennai.
            - Structure:
                - 10 State Councils affiliated with the parent organization.
            - Affiliation:
                - World Crafts Council (gained a global platform).
            - Activities:
                - Safeguards craft traditions.
                - Protects artisans’ interests.
                - Operates craft shops named Kamala to display and sell supported arts and crafts.
        - Indira Gandhi National Centre for the Arts (IGNCA)
            - Established in 1985 as an autonomous institution.
            - Focuses on:
                - Research, conservation, display, and dissemination of arts.
                - Promotion of critical and creative literature.
            - Functional Units:
                - Kala Nidhi – Multi-form library.
                - Kala Kosa – Studies and publishes fundamental Indian texts.
                - Janapada Sampada – Conducts lifestyle studies.
                - Cultural Informatics – Uses technology for cultural preservation and propagation.
                - Sutradhara – Administrative section supporting all activities.
                - Kala Darsana – Executes exhibitions based on IGNCA research.
            - Objective:
                - To be a major resource center for oral and visual art forms in India.
        - All India Radio (AIR)
            - 
            - Premier public service radio broadcaster under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting.
            - Motto: Bahujan Hitaya: Bahujan Sukhaya (“For the welfare and happiness of the masses”).
            - Coverage: Reaches 99.19% of India’s population.
            - Responsibilities:
                - Inform citizens on matters of public interest with a balanced flow of information.
            - Languages: Produces content in 23 languages.
            - Governed by:
                - Prasar Bharati Act (amended in 1990).
            - Objectives under the Act:
                - Promote national integration and constitutional values.
                - Disseminate education and literacy, covering environment, health, family welfare, agriculture, and rural development.
                - Highlight issues concerning women, children, disabled persons, minorities, and tribal communities.
                - Conduct research and develop new broadcasting technology.
                - Showcase India’s cultural diversity and promote youth activities, including sports.
        - Nehru Memorial Museum and Library (NMML)
            - Built in 1929–30 as part of Lutyens’ Delhi.
            - Originally the Teen Murti House, former residence of:
                - British Commander-in-Chief (pre-Independence).
                - India’s first Prime Minister, Jawaharlal Nehru (1947–1964).
            - Converted into a museum and library in 1964 after Nehru’s death.
            - Library expansion:
                - An exclusive library was built in 1974 due to extensive data collection.
            - Purpose of Museum:
                - Exhibit memorabilia, personal items, and objects related to Nehru and India’s freedom struggle.
            - Activities:
                - Organizes lectures, seminars, conferences, and symposiums on Indian history and Nehru’s legacy.
        - Centre for Cultural Resources and Training (CCRT)
            - 
            - Established in 1979 under the Ministry of Culture.
            - Founded by Dr. Kapila Vatsyayan and Kamala Devi Chattopadhyay.
            - Headquarters: New Delhi.
            - Regional Centers:
                - Udaipur (West).
                - Hyderabad (South).
                - Guwahati (North-East).
            - Purpose:
                - Strengthen national identity by integrating culture into education.
            - Focus Areas:
                - Not just students, but also teachers, principals, and administrative staff.
                - Promote awareness of India’s cultural and linguistic diversity.
            - Suggested Teaching Methods:
                - Conduct workshops to train teachers in traditional Indian crafts.
                - Develop curriculum on Indian art and culture for educators.
                - Establish a library with scripts, digital photographs, and audio-visual educational materials.
            - Government Support:
                - Provides scholarships for students, teachers, and artists.
                - Offers Young Artists, Junior, and Senior Fellowships for research.
                - Runs Cultural Heritage Young Leadership Programme to promote social values and community participation.
        - National Archives of India (NAI)
            - Established: 1891 as Imperial Records Department (IRD) in Calcutta (now Kolkata).
            - Shift to New Delhi: 1911, following the capital shift.
            - Renaming & Reorganization: After Independence, renamed National Archives of India (NAI), with a Director heading the institution.
            - Public Access: Opened for research in 1939.
            - Major Developments
                - Conservation Research Laboratory (CRL) (1940): Focuses on document preservation.
                - Expansion of Responsibilities:
                    - Acquisition of public records and private collections.
                    - Research and reference programs.
                    - Formal training for conservation.
                    - Establishment of regional centers in Jaipur, Bhubaneswar, and Puducherry.
            - Objectives
                - Preserve India’s documentary heritage and ensure future generations have access.
                - Collect and scientifically manage archival documents.
                - Strengthen national and international archival collaboration.
                - Develop scientific awareness among archivists and users.
        - Indian Council for Cultural Relations (ICCR)
            - 
            - Established: 1950 by Maulana Abul Kalam Azad.
            - Ministry: Under the Ministry of Culture.
            - Functions
                - Formulates and implements policies for international cultural exchange.
                - Funds visual and performing arts programs, e.g., Jazz Festival (Delhi), North-East Music Festival (Guwahati).
                - Collaborates with international cultural organizations.
            - Objectives
                - Promote cultural exchange and international friendship.
                - Blend traditional and contemporary Indian culture.
                - Foster global appreciation of Indian arts.
        - Directorate of Film Festivals (DFF)
            - Established: 1973 by the Government of India.
            - Ministry: Under the Ministry of Information and Broadcasting.
            - Functions
                - Organizes National and International Film Festivals in India.
                - Promotes Indian cinema on global platforms.
                - Organizes the National Film Awards.
                - Manages Indian Panorama film selections.
                - Hosts Dadasaheb Phalke Award functions.
                - Conducts Cultural Exchange Programs via Indian film screenings abroad.
                - Maintains a Print Collection and Documentation Centre for film studies.
        - Indian Council of Historical Research (ICHR)
            - Established: 1972 under Societies Registration Act.
            - Funding: University Grants Commission (UGC).    
            - Objectives
                - Provide an objective and scientific platform for historical research.
                - Focus on neglected areas (Borders, Northeast, Women’s History, etc.).
                - Ensure balanced research efforts across India.
                - Offer funding and academic support to historical research.
                - Disseminate research through journals like:
                - Indian Historical Review (English).
                - Itihas (Hindi).
            - Activities
                - Organizes seminars, conferences, and academic discussions.
                - Provides fellowships and financial aid to historians.
                - Maintains a library and documentation center for historical research.
        - National Mission for Manuscripts (NMM)
            - Established: 2003 on recommendations from Archaeological Survey of India & National Archives of India.
            - Executing Agency: Indira Gandhi National Centre for the Arts (IGNCA).
            - Ministry: Under Ministry of Culture, Government of India.
            - Key Tasks
                - Develop National Electronic Database with 1M+ manuscripts.
                - Use modern & indigenous methods for manuscript conservation.
                - Train conservators through diploma & degree courses.
                - Provide linguistic, script, and critical editing training.
                - Catalog and digitize rare and endangered manuscripts.
                - Engage scholars via lectures, seminars, and publication of critical editions.
        - Indian National Trust for Art and Cultural Heritage (INTACH)
            - 
            - Established: 1984, New Delhi.
            - Type: Non-profit NGO.
            - Structure: 170+ Chapters across Indian & international cities.
            - Key Functions
                - Heritage Conservation: Restoration and management of monuments.
                - Public Awareness:
                    - Heritage walks in historic cities.
                    - School awareness programs and workshops.
                - Focus Areas:
                    - Monuments outside Archaeological Survey of India’s purview.
                    - Collaboration with local authorities for preservation.
            - International Collaborations
                - INTACH UK Trust (1987) – Funds heritage projects in India.
                - Partnerships for integrating tourism & monument conservation.
        - Sahitya Akademi
            - Established: 1954 by the Government of India.
            - Purpose: Promote and coordinate literature in Indian languages.
            - Languages Covered:
                - 22 constitutional languages + English & Rajasthani (24 total).
            - Key Activities
                - Organizes literary gatherings (Samvad, Meet the Author, Kathasandi, Asmita).
                - Preserves oral & tribal literature.
                - Publishes books and research materials.
            - Awards & Fellowships
                - Bhasha Samman
                    - Recognizes contributions to non-recognized languages, Ancient & Medieval literature.
                - Dr. Anand Coomaraswamy Fellowship
                    - For work in Ancient Indian Art.
                - Premchand Fellowship
                    - Honors contributions to Hindi literature
        - Sangeet Natak Akademi (SNA)
            - Established: 1952 as India’s first National Academy for Performing Arts.
            - Inaugurated by: Dr. Rajendra Prasad.
            - Key Responsibilities
                - Promote music, dance, and drama across India.
                - Preserve intangible cultural heritage through arts.
                - Collaborate with State & Union Territories for cultural projects.
                - Supervises institutions like:
                    - National School of Drama (NSD) (1959, independent since 1975).
                - Works with UNESCO for global cultural conservation.
            - Objectives
                - Encourage regional theatre, acting, and stagecraft education.
                - Publish research literature on Indian performing arts.
                - Revive and sustain folk dance, music, and drama.
                - Establish cultural ties with international performing arts communities.
        - Lalit Kala Akademi
            - 
            - Established: 1954 as National Academy of Art.
            - Ministry: Under Ministry of Culture.
            - Primary Focus: Promotion & preservation of fine arts in India.  
            - Key Activities
                - Organizes national & international exhibitions (e.g., National Exhibition of Art, International Triennale India).
                - Maintains visual art archives for modern & contemporary Indian art.
                - Operates regional centers:
                    - Chennai, Kolkata, Lucknow, Shimla, Shillong, Bhubaneswar.
                - Offers scholarships & grants to artists.
                - Runs art conservation laboratory.


- Coins in Ancient and Medieval India
	- Origins of Coins
        - The word “coin” comes from the Latin word Cuneus.
        - First recorded use of coins:
            - China & Greece: Around 700 BC.
            - India: 6th century BC.
        - The study of coins and medallions is known as Numismatics.
	- Other Forms of Exchange
        - Cowrie Shells
            - 
            - Used as an early medium of exchange.
            - Common among the masses for small-scale transactions.
            - Held definite market value similar to coins.

    - Punch marked coins
        - Features
            - Earliest coins were casted and die-struck on one side only.
            - Incused with one to five symbols—hence called Punch Marked Coins.
            - Mentioned in Panini’s Ashtadhyayi as metallic pieces stamped with symbols.
            - Weight unit: Ratti (0.11 grams).
            - Circulated between 6th and 2nd century BC.
        - Classifications
            - Punch Marked Coins of Mahajanapadas (6th Century BC)
                - First punch-marked coins were called Puranas, Karshapanas, or Pana.
                - Minted by various Janapadas and Mahajanapadas of the Indo-Gangetic Plain.
                - Made of silver, with different symbols:
                    - Saurashtra: Humped bull.
                    - Dakshin Panchala: Swastika.
                    - Magadha: Five symbols.
                - Magadhan punch-marked coins became the most circulated in South Asia.
                    - 
                - Mentioned in Manusmriti and Buddhist Jataka tales.
            - Punch Marked Coins during Mauryan Period (322–185 BC)
                - 
                - Chanakya’s Arthashastra mentions various types:
                    - Rupyarupa (Silver).
                    - Suvarnarupa (Gold).
                    - Tamrarupa (Copper).
                    - Sisarupa (Lead).
                - Most consistent symbols: Sun and six-armed wheel.
                - Weight: 50–54 grains of silver (~32 rattis).
                - Known as Karshapanas.

    - Indo-Greek coins
        - Reign Period: 180 BC – 10 AD.
        - Significance
            - Introduced ruler’s bust or head on coins.
            - Bilingual legends:
                - Greek on one side.
                - Kharosthi on the other.
            - Initially depicted Greek gods/goddesses (Zeus, Hercules, Apollo, Pallas Athene).
            - Later, included Indian deities.
        - Material & Features
            - Made of silver, copper, nickel, and lead.
            - Provided detailed information about the monarch, year of issue, and sometimes the reigning king’s image.
            - Kushan Kings introduced the portrait-head style:
                - 
                - Helmeted bust of the king on one side.
                - The king’s favorite deity on the reverse.
                - Kanishka’s coins had Greek inscriptions only.

    - Coins by Satavahanas (232 BC - 227 AD)
        - 
        - Material Used
            - Mostly lead (primary metal).
            - Rarely silver.
            - Potin (alloy of silver and copper) also used.
            - Copper coins were common.
        - Design & Features
            - Lacked artistic beauty but were historically significant.
            - Common symbols:
                - Elephant, horse, lion, or Chaitya on one side.
                - Ujjain symbol (cross with four circles at the ends) on the other.
            - Inscriptions in Prakrit.

    - Coins of the Western Satraps (35–405 AD)
        - 
        - Regions Controlled: Malwa, Gujarat, Kathiawar.
        - Significance
            - First to use Saka era dates (starting from 78 AD) on coins.
            - Coins depicted:
                - King’s head on one side.
                - Buddhist Chaitya or Stupa on the other (influenced by Satavahanas).
            - Inscriptions in Prakrit, written in various scripts.

    - Coins Issued in the Gupta age (319–550 AD)
        - 
        - Materials Used
            - Mostly gold.
            - Silver and copper coins were issued later (silver introduced by Chandragupta II).
        - Designs
            - Depicted the king in different activities:
                - Making oblations, playing veena, riding a horse/elephant, slaying animals, performing Ashvamedha sacrifice.
            - Reverse side:
                - Goddess Lakshmi seated on a throne/lotus seal.
                - Sometimes depicted the queen.
        - Inscriptions
            - First coins to have Sanskrit inscriptions (in Brahmi script).
            - Depicted Hindu deities like Durga, Ganga, Garuda, Kartikeya.
        - Post-Gupta Period
            - After the Hun invasion (6th century AD), coinage declined.
            - Regional kingdoms issued low-quality coins with mixed designs.
            - South India shifted towards a gold standard, inspired by Roman gold coins.

    - Coins of the Vardhanas (6th Century AD)
        - 
        - Harshavardhana’s silver coins had:
            - King’s head on one side.
            - Peacock on the other.
        - Coins marked the beginning of a new era (606 AD), his coronation year.
        
    - Coins of the Chalukyans (6th Century AD)
        - 
        - Western Chalukyas (Pulakeshin I):
            - Coins had temple/lion images with inscriptions on one side.
            - Reverse side was blank.
        - Eastern Chalukyas (7th–12th Century AD):
            - Central boar symbol with king’s name inscribed.
            - Reverse side blank.

    - Coins of the Rajput dynasties (11th–12th Century AD)
        - Made of gold, copper, or billon (silver-copper alloy).
        - Two major types:
            - Sanskrit inscriptions on one side, goddess on the other (Kalachuris, Chandelas, Tomars, Rathores).
            - Seated bull on one side, horseman on the other (Gandhara & Sindh kings).

    - Coins of the Pandyan & Chola dynasty
        - 
        - Pandyan Coins:
            - Early coins: Square-shaped with elephant symbol.
            - Later coins: Fish symbol.
        - Chola Coins:
            - Raja Raja I: Standing king on one side, seated goddess on the other.
            - Rajendra I: Inscription ‘Sri Rajendra’ with tiger & fish emblems.

    - Turkish & Delhi Sultanate coins
        - Introduced Islamic inscriptions (king’s name, title, Hijri date).
        - No human images due to idolatry prohibition.
        - Notable Innovations:
            - Iltutmish: Silver Tanka, Copper Jital.
            - Muhammad bin Tughlaq: Attempted token currency (failed).
            - Sher Shah Suri:
                - Introduced Rupee (silver, 178 grains).
                - Dam (copper, 330 grains).
            
    - Vijayanagara empire coins (14th–17th Century)
        - Issued large quantities of gold coins along with silver and copper coins.
        - Types of Coins:
            - Pagodas – Higher denomination; featured a running warrior and a dagger symbol.
            - Gold Fanams – Fractional units.
            - Silver Taras – Fractional units.
            - Copper Coins – Used for daily transactions.
        - Coinage & Inscriptions
            - Early Vijayanagara coins were minted in different regions and had different names, such as Barkur Gadyanas and Bhatkal Gadyanas.
            - Inscriptions were in Kannada or Sanskrit.
            - Common imagery included:
                - Double-headed eagle holding an elephant in each beak and claw.
                - Bull, elephant, and various Hindu deities.
            - The Gold Varahan Coin issued by Krishna Deva Raya (1509–1529) featured:
                - Obverse: Seated Vishnu.
                - Reverse: Three-line Sanskrit legend “Shri Pratap Krishna Raya”.
                
    - Mughal coinage
        - Standard Gold Coin: Mohur (170–175 grains).
            - As per Abul Fazl’s Ain-i-Akbari, one Mohur = 9 Rupees.
            - Half and quarter Mohurs also existed.
        - Silver Rupee:
            - Adopted from Sher Shah Suri’s currency.
            - Became the most famous Mughal coin.
        - Copper Coins:
            - Based on Sher Shah’s Dam (320–330 grains).
        - Notable Mughal Rulers & Their Coins
            - Akbar (1556–1605)
                - 
                - Issued both round and square coins.
                - In 1579, introduced Ilahi Coins to propagate Din-i-Illahi.
                    - Inscribed with “God is great, may his glory be glorified”.
                    - Value: 10 Rupees.
                - Sahansah – Largest gold coin, bore names of Persian solar months.
            - Jahangir (1605–1627)
                - 
                - Introduced couplet legends on coins.
                - Some coins included Noor Jahan’s name.
                - Most famous coins featured Zodiac signs.

    - Other important facts
        - Historical References:
            - The earliest reference to coins in India is found in the Vedas.
            - Nishka was the term used for metal coins in Vedic texts.
        - Sher Shah Suri’s Rupee (16th Century)
            - Introduced silver Rupee (~11.34g).
            - 1 Rupee = 4 Copper Coins.
            - The Indian currency is still called Rupee today.
        - Ancient Indian Money Trees
            - Coins were sometimes stored on “money trees”—flat metal pieces with branches, each ending in a round disk (coin).
            - When needed, people broke off a coin from the money tree.
        - Gupta Coinage (4th–6th Century AD)
            - Front Side: King’s given name.
            - Back Side: Assumed name ending with “aditya” (Sun God).
        - Maratha & Mysore Coinage
            - Chhatrapati Shivaji (1674–1680)
                - Issued gold Huns and copper Shivarais with Nagari script inscriptions.
            - Wodeyar Dynasty (1399–1947, Mysore)
                - King Kanthiraya Narasa issued coins featuring Narasimha Avatar of Vishnu (weighed 6–8 grains).
            - Haidar Ali (r. 1761–1782)
                - Continued Wodeyar coinage; featured Shiva & Parvati on gold pagodas.
            - Tipu Sultan (r. 1782–1799)
                - Used two eras in his coinage.

    - The Coinage Act, 2011
        - Replaced the Coinage Act, 1906.
        - Definition of “Coin”:
            - A coin is any metal or other material stamped by the Government or an authorized entity.
            - Includes:
                - Commemorative Coins.
                - GOI One Rupee Note.
        - Forgery & Penalties:
            - Prescribes penalties for forgery, minting violations, etc.


- Indian culture abroad
    - Introduction
        - India never invaded any country in the last 10,000 years, yet its cultural influence is widespread.
        - Unlike many other civilizations that remained regionally confined, Indian culture is often regarded as the First World Culture due to its extensive global impact.

    - Who took Indian culture abroad?
        - Since the Indus Valley Civilization, India has maintained contact with the outside world through trade, migration, and religious outreach.
            - Roma (Wanderers):
                - Migrated via Iran and Iraq to Turkey and Europe.
                - In Europe, they became known as Gypsies.
            - Trade & Migration:
                - Trade activities began with Vietnam, Italy, and China.
                - Many traders migrated, carrying the legacy of Indian culture.
                - In the 1st century BC, Indian traders traveled to Indonesia and Cambodia in search of gold.
                - The Kalinga Dynasty established trade relations with Sri Lanka.
            - Religious Expansion:
                - Emperor Ashoka sent his son and daughter to Sri Lanka to spread Buddhism.
            - Cultural Exchange through Foreign Visitors:
                - Foreign travelers, monks, and missionaries visiting India or traveling abroad contributed to cross-cultural exchanges across regions.

    - Prominent ancient ports of India
        - Naval trade with various countries during ancient and medieval times played a significant role in spreading Indian culture abroad.
        - Detailed accounts of ancient Indian ports can be found in the books Periplus of the Erythraean Sea and Geographia.
        - During the Mauryan rule, the Navadhyaksha (Superintendent of Shipping) managed the shipping administration.
        - Coins of the last Satavahana King, Yajna Sri Satakarni, depicted ships, possibly symbolizing the dynasty’s naval strength.
        - List of prominent ports in ancient times.
            

                

                    Name of the Port
                    Region
                    Associated Dynasty & Time Period
                    Brief Details
                

                

                    Lothal
                    Gujarat (near present Ahmedabad)
                    Indus Valley Civilisation; 2400 BC
                    Export of copper, hardwoods, ivory, pearls, carnelian, and gold to Mesopotamia; timber wood and lapis lazuli to Sumeria.
                

                

                    Barygaza
                    Present Bharuch, Gujarat
                    3rd century BC onwards; Major trade center under Western Satraps
                    Spice and silk trade with Arab countries. Plundered twice in the 17th century but resurged quickly. Import of wheat, rice, sesame oil, cotton, and cloth.
                

                

                    Muziris
                    Malabar Coast, Kerala (near present-day Kochi)
                    1st century BC onwards; Chera Kingdom
                    Mentioned in Sangam literature. Export of spices, semi-precious stones (beryl, pearls, diamonds, sapphires), ivory, Chinese silk, Gangetic spikenard, and tortoise shells to Persia, Rome, Greece, and Egypt.
                

                

                    Korkai
                    Tamil Nadu
                    Early Pandyan Kingdom
                    Well-known center of pearl fishery. Mentioned in Sangam literature.
                

                

                    Puhar (also known as Cauveri Poompattinam)
                    Tamil Nadu (at the mouth of the Cauvery river)
                    Early Chola Dynasty; 400 BC – 200 AD
                    Import of various articles. Mentioned in Silappadikaram.
                

                

                    Podouke
                    Present Arikamedu near Puducherry
                    2nd century BC – 8th century AD
                    Greek trading port for trade with Rome. Export of gems, pearls, spices, and silk. Import of wine.
                

                

                    Barbarikon
                    Near Karachi, Pakistan
                    Parthians and Scythians
                    Import of linens, topaz, coral, storax, frankincense, vessels of glass, silver, gold plate, and wine. Exports included turquoise, lapis lazuli, Seric skins, cotton cloth, silk yarn, and indigo.
                

                

                    Sounagora
                    Wari (Bateshwar, present Bangladesh)
                    450 BC; Mauryan dynasty
                    Mentioned by mathematician Ptolemy in his book Geographia.
                

                

                    Maisolia or Masulipatnam
                    Machilipatnam, Andhra Pradesh
                    From 3rd century BC onwards; Satavahana’s reign
                    Muslin clothes were traded by ancient Greeks. Principal sea port of the Golconda Kingdom from the 15th to 17th centuries.
                

                

                    Tamralipti
                    Present-day Tamluk, West Bengal
                    Mauryan Dynasty
                    Exit point of the Mauryan trade route for South and Southeast Asia. Mentioned by Ptolemy and Chinese pilgrims Hiuen-Tsang and Fa-hien.
                

                

                    Palur
                    Odisha
                    2nd century AD; Kalinga Dynasty
                    Place of voyage to Southeast Asia and China. Mentioned by Ptolemy and Xuanzang (Hiuen-Tsang).
                

            


    - Mathematics from India to abroad
        - As early as 500 BC, Indians devised a system of different symbols for numbers (1-9).
            - This notation system was adopted by the Arabs, who called it numerals.
            - Indian maritime traders took the decimal system to Arabia, from where it later spread to the Western world.
            - Arabs acknowledged India’s contribution by calling mathematics ‘Hindisat’ (pertaining to India).
        - Binary number system was first described by Vedic scholar Pingala in his book Chandashastra, which is the earliest known Sanskrit treatise on prosody.
        - Fibonacci numbers were first described in Indian mathematics as Matrameru, mentioned by Pingala in connection with Sanskrit prosody.
            - Later, methods for generating Fibonacci sequences were given by Virahanka, Gopala, and Hemacandra, long before Fibonacci introduced them to European mathematics.
        - Baudhayana explained the Pythagoras theorem.
        - Concepts of Algebra, Geometry, and Trigonometry also originated in India and spread abroad.

    - Indian science and technology abroad
        - India made significant contributions to science and technology long before modern laboratories. 
        - Many theories and techniques discovered by ancient Indians laid the foundation for modern advancements.
            - Bhaskaracharya correctly calculated the time taken by Earth to orbit the Sun centuries before astronomer Smart:
                - 365.258756484 days.
            - Kanad developed the atomic theory centuries before John Dalton, introducing the concept of Anu (indestructible particles), much like atoms.
            - Zinc extraction was known to Indians thousands of years before the British discovered it.
            - The first seamless celestial globe was made in Kashmir by Ali Kashmiri Ibn Luqman during Akbar’s reign.
            - Wootz Steel, developed in ancient India, was used to make Damascus swords, known for their sharpness.
            - Ancient Indians defined the smallest and largest time units:
                - Smallest – 34,000th of a second (Krati).
                - Largest – 4.32 billion years (Mahayuga).
            - India was the first to invent and use buttons (Indus Valley Civilization, 2000 BC).
            - First iron-cased rockets were developed by Tipu Sultan in the 1780s, successfully used against the British East India Company.
            - Shampoo originated in India:
                - Derived from the Hindi word ‘Champo’.
                - Introduced in Britain by Bengali entrepreneur Sake Dean Mahomed (1762).
            - Sushruta – Father of Surgery (2600 years ago):
                - Conducted C-sections, cataract removal, artificial limbs, fractures, plastic surgery, brain surgery, and used anesthesia.
            - First recorded mention of leprosy – Sushruta Samhita (6th century BC).
            - Cataract surgery was performed in ancient India using a curved needle to push the lens out. The method later spread worldwide.
            - Ayurveda – the earliest school of medicine, dating back to 5000 BC.
                - Charaka (Father of Medicine) consolidated Ayurveda 2500 years ago.
                - Foreign scholars studied Ayurveda at Taxila and Kashi.
            - Siddha system (regional variant of Ayurveda):
                - Originated in Tamil Nadu, used metals, minerals, and chemical products.
                - Alchemy has its roots in Siddha medicine.
                - ‘Varmam’ – Siddha medicine branch dealing with traumatology and injuries.
            - Ancient Indian Herbal Knowledge:
                - Karela (bitter melon) resembles the pancreas and was used as a diabetes remedy.
                - Akhrot (walnut) resembles the human brain and was used as a brain tonic.

    - Influence of Indian Religion Abroad
        - India’s religious and philosophical traditions influenced South-East Asia, East Asia, Europe, and North America.
        - Hinduism Abroad
            - Thailand (3rd-4th century AD):
                - Brahminical images and Hindu temples were constructed.
                - Earliest images found are of Lord Vishnu.
            - Vietnam:
                - Cham people built many Hindu temples dedicated to Shiva, Ganesha, Saraswati, Lakshmi, and Parvati.
                - Shivalingas and deity images were housed in temples.
            - Cambodia:
                - Champa and Kambuja (Cambodia) kingdoms were ruled by Hindu kings of Indian origin.
                - Government followed Hindu polity and Brahminical jurisprudence.
            - Malaysia:
                - Shaivism evidence found in Kedah and Wellesley.
                - Nandi statues, Durga reliefs, Ganesha, and Shivalingas excavated.
            - Hinduism is the world’s third-largest religion, with over 1.15 billion adherents.
        - Buddhism Abroad
            - Acharya Atisa (Vikramshila University) went to Tibet (11th century AD) and strengthened Buddhism.
            - Emperor Ashoka sent his son Mahendra and daughter Sanghamitra to Sri Lanka to spread Buddhism.
                - Mahavihara and Abhayagiri were the first monasteries built there.
            - Thonmi Sambhota, a Tibetan minister, studied at Nalanda and introduced Buddhism to Tibet.
            - Silk Route scholars helped spread Buddhism across China and Korea.
            - Buddhism in Korea:
                - First Buddhist monk, Sundo (352 AD), carried Buddha images and sutras.
                - Acharya Mallananda (384 AD) followed.
                - Buddhist texts were printed in 6000 volumes.
            - Buddhism in Japan:
                - Given State Religion status.
                - Thousands became Buddhist monks and nuns.
            - Myanmar:
                - Pagan Kingdom (11th-13th century AD) was a major Buddhist center.
            - Buddhist missionaries spread Theravada Buddhism to South-East Asia, Mahayana Buddhism to East Asia, and Vajrayana Buddhism to Central Asia.
        - Jainism Abroad
            - Jainism (2500+ years old) was described by Greek geographer Strabo (64 BC – 23 AD).
            - Jain diaspora spread to East Africa, Britain, Japan, and North America.
            - Jain business families settled in East Africa decades ago.
            - Jains migrated to Britain after Indian independence, with a large influx from Uganda during Idi Amin’s rule.
            - In Japan, Jains were involved in the diamond trade (Kobe).
            - Jains migrated to North America after 1965, establishing temples and organizations to maintain their identity.
            - Jainism’s environmental ethics align with modern sustainability and activism.

    - Indian languages abroad
        - India (780 languages) has the world’s second-highest number of languages, after Papua New Guinea (839).
        - Sanskrit:
            - Originated in India and is the mother of all European languages.
            - Thousands of Sanskrit books were translated into Chinese.
            - Accepted as the sacred language in Japan, where scholars still study it.
        - Other Indian Languages:
            - Bengali – Official language of Bangladesh.
            - Tamil – Official language of Sri Lanka and Singapore.
        - Indian Influence in Other Countries:
            - Myanmar: 
                - Developed their own Pali language and translated Buddhist and Hindu scriptures.
            - Tibet:
                - Thonmi Sambhot wrote Sanskrit grammar based on Panini’s work.
                - 9,600 Sanskrit books were translated into Tibetan.
            - Sri Lanka: 
                - Pali became the literary language.
            - Thailand:
                - Thai kingdoms were named in Sanskrit (Dwaravati, Shrivijay, Sukhodaya, Ayutthiya).
                - Cities like Prachinaburi and Singhaburi derive their names from Sanskrit.
            - Cambodia: 
                - Sanskrit was used for administration until the 14th century AD.
            - Malaysia:
                - Ancient script was Brahmi in its late form.
                - Buddhist texts in a script resembling old Tamil were found in Kedah.
                - Many Sanskrit words exist in the language (e.g., svarga, rasa, guna, dahda, mantri, laksha).
            - Indonesia: Sanskrit hymns are recited during religious ceremonies.

    - Indian temple architectural tradition abroad
        - Sthapatya-Shastra: Ancient Indian science of architecture and civil construction.
        - Spread of Indian Art and Architecture:
            - Afghanistan, Baluchistan, Seistan (Mauryan Empire – Ashoka’s reign): Buddhist stupas were constructed.
            - Central Asia:
                - Indian art blended with Greek and Kushana styles.
                - India’s cultural frontiers extended up to Balkh (Oxus River).
                - Gandhara School of Art (Afghanistan and Central Asia) was derived from Indian styles.
            - South-East Asia:
                - Indian traders introduced architecture to Indonesia, Malaysia, Vietnam, Laos, Cambodia, Thailand, Burma, China, Korea, Japan.
                - Portuguese (Manuel I, 1495–1521) exchanged architectural influences with India.
            - China:
                - Built Indian-style cave temples and monastic complexes.
                - Dun-huang, Yun-kang, and Lung-men are famous cave complexes.
            - Indonesia:
                - Prambanan – Largest Shiva temple on Java island.
                - Buddha statue at Dong Duong (108m high) resembles Amaravati sculptures.
                - Ganesha idols found in Bali.
            - Thailand:
                - Ayutthiya (Ayodhya): Many ancient Indian-style temples exist (400 in Bangkok alone).
            - Korea:
                - An Indian monk built two temples in Pyongyang (5th century AD).
            - Myanmar:
                - King Aniruddha built Shwezegon Pagoda and ~1,000 temples in Indian style.
            - Cambodia:
                - Angkor Wat represents Vishnu’s abode (Vaikunthadhama).
                - Baphuon (11th century) is another grand Indian-style temple.
            - Malaysia:
                - Ligor: Over 50 Indian temples exist.
            - Afghanistan:
                - Bodhisattva statues (Bamiyan, 1st century AD): Carved under Kushana Emperor Kanishka’s patronage.
            - Sri Lanka:
            - Stupas (3rd century BC – 4th century AD): Followed Indian hemispherical design (Anda).
            - Europe:
                - Christian Basilicas show similarities to Buddhist stupas.
                - Gothic sculpture in Bayeux, Aachen, Trier has Indian motifs.
            - Islamic Architecture Influence:
                - Dome mosques derive from Indian stupas.
                - Byzantine architecture (e.g., Hagia Sophia) has domes resembling Buddhist stupas.
            
    - Global influence of Indian epics
        - Ramayana & Mahabharata Influence:
            - Wayung (shadow play) in Southeast Asia is based on these epics.
            - Thailand: Streets named after Ramayana characters (Rajaram, Rajajrani, Mahajaya, Cakravamsha).
            - Cambodia:
                - Angkor Wat has Ramayana and Mahabharata engravings.
                - Baphuon temple: Depicts Rama-Ravana battle, Shiva-Parvati on Kailash, Kamadeva’s destruction.
            - Malaysia:
                - Mentioned in Ramayana, Jataka tales, Milind Panha, Silappadikaram, Raghuvamsha.

    - Role of Indian universities and scholars
        - Major Ancient Indian Universities:
            - Takshila (700 BC) – World’s first university (10,500+ students, 60+ subjects).
            - Nalanda Mahavihara (5th century AD):
                - Hiuen Tsang studied under Shilabhadra for 2 years.
                - I-Tsing stayed for 10 years.
                - Famous scholars: Aryabhatta, Atisa, Dignaga, Dharmapala, Nagarjuna, Shantarakshita.
            - Vikramshila:
                - Tibetan king invited its scholars.
            - Odantapuri (Bihar):
                - Flourished under Pala kings.
                - Many monks migrated to Tibet.
        - Indian Scholars Abroad:
            - Kashyapa Martanga & Dharmarakshita – Sent to China in 67 AD by the Emperor.
            - Kamalasheel (Nalanda scholar): Invited by the King of Tibet.
            - Bodhidharma (Yoga & Zen Buddhism master): Venerated in China & Japan.
            - Indian sages spread Yogic science across Asia, the Middle East, North Africa, South America.
            - Agastya (Saptarishi): Propagated a yogic way of life.

    - Indian sports and games abroad
        - Chess:
            - Originated in Gupta Empire (Chaturanga).
            - Mentioned in Mahabharata.
        - Snakes & Ladders:
            - Created by Gyandev (13th century) – Originally Mokshapat.
            - Ladders = virtues, Snakes = vices.
        - Polo: 
            - Originated in India.
        - Judo & Karate: 
            - Martial arts originated in India before spreading to East Asia.
        - Kho-Kho:
            - Originated in Maharashtra.
            - Played on ‘raths’ (chariots), initially called Rathera.
        - Indian Card Games:
            - Ganjifa playing cards.
            - Suits (Kridapatram) – Ancient Indian painted rags for playing.


- India through the eyes of foreign travelers
    - Introduction
        • India as a Center of Attraction:
            • Throughout history, India attracted rulers and travelers worldwide due to its vast opportunities, diverse culture, religions, and traditions.
            • Many travelers documented their experiences, focusing on different aspects of Indian civilization.
        • Themes in Traveler Accounts:
            • Some accounts described court affairs.
            • Others highlighted contemporary architecture and monuments.
            • Some depicted social and economic life.
            • These records provide an authentic picture of Indian civilization.
        • Travelers in Ancient and Medieval India:
            • Many prominent travelers visited India and recorded their observations.
            • No accounts from women travelers are available from this period.
